**14.1.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Putting King Dhṛtarāṣṭra before him, once the water-offerings had been made, the mighty-armed Yudhiṣṭhira, his senses confounded with grief, climbed up from the river.

**14.1.2**  And having ascended, the lord of the earth, his eyes brimming with tears, fell upon the bank of the Gaṅgā like an elephant struck down by a hunter.

**14.1.3**  As he sank down, Bhīma, urged on by Kṛṣṇa, took hold of him; and Kṛṣṇa, crusher of hostile armies, said to him, "Do not act so."

**14.1.4**  Anguished, fallen to the ground, sighing again and again, O king, the Pāṇḍavas beheld the righteous-souled Yudhiṣṭhira.

**14.1.5**  Seeing the lord of men downcast in heart and drained of his strength, the Pāṇḍavas, overcome anew with sorrow, sat down beside him.

**14.1.6**  And King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the mighty-armed, the deeply wise, seated near him, spoke these words to the one tormented by overwhelming grief.

**14.1.7**  "Rise, O tiger among the Kurus, and do the task that comes next. By the law of the warrior, O Kauravya, this earth has been won by you.

**14.1.8**  Enjoy it together with your brothers and your friends, O lord of men. I see nothing here for you to mourn, O best of those who uphold the law.

**14.1.9**  It is I who must mourn, and Gāndhārī too, O lord of peoples, bereft of our sons and our kingdom, like one whose wealth was won only in a dream.

**14.1.10**  Having failed to heed the words of deepest meaning spoken by the great-souled Vidura, who sought my good, I, fool that I am, now burn with regret.

**14.1.11**  Long ago that righteous-souled one of divine sight told me: 'Through Duryodhana's transgression your whole line will perish.'

**14.1.12**  'If you wish well-being, O king, for your line and for yourself, let this evil-natured, dull-witted prince Suyodhana be put to death.

**14.1.13**  Let neither Karṇa nor Śakuni so much as set eyes on him ever again; vigilant, forbid even their gathering for the dicing.'

**14.1.14**  'Consecrate as king the righteous-souled Yudhiṣṭhira; self-controlled, he will guard this earth in accordance with the law.'

**14.1.15**  'But if you will not have Kuntī's son Yudhiṣṭhira for king, then become the very pivot yourself and take up the kingdom, O prince.'

**14.1.16**  'Bearing yourself with even regard toward all beings, O ruler of men, let all your kinsmen live under your protection, O increaser of your kin.'

**14.1.17**  Though far-seeing Vidura spoke thus, O son of Kuntī, I, with my mind turned to vanity, followed the wicked Duryodhana.

**14.1.18**  For not having listened to that hero's gentle words, I have reaped a great misery as their fruit, and am sunk in an ocean of sorrow.

**14.1.19**  For your two parents here are aged, O king, and you see us both stricken with grief; yet I see nothing for which you should mourn, O lord of men."

**14.2.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by the wise King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the sagacious one fell silent; and then Keśava spoke to him.

**14.2.2**  "Grief nursed too greatly in the heart, O lord of men, scorches even the ancestors long departed before him.

**14.2.3**  Offer sacrifice with many and varied rites, with ample priestly fees; gratify the gods with soma, and the fathers too with the ancestral oblation.

**14.2.4**  For one such as you, O great of intellect, this present grief is not fitting; what is to be known you have known, and what was to be done you have done.

**14.2.5**  You have heard the duties of kings from Bhīṣma, the son of the Bhāgīrathī, and likewise from Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, from Nārada, and from Vidura.

**14.2.6**  You should not follow this course that befits the deluded; take up rather the way of your fathers and forefathers, and bear the yoke of rule.

**14.2.7**  For the warrior, joined with glory, attains heaven beyond doubt; and not one among the brave was slain here with his back turned.

**14.2.8**  Cast off your sorrow, O great king, for what was to be has come to pass thus; those slain by you in this battle can never be seen again.

**14.2.9**  Having said this much to the Dharma-king Yudhiṣṭhira, the mighty-splendored Govinda fell silent; and Yudhiṣṭhira answered him.

**14.2.10**  "Govinda, the love you bear me is well known to me; with friendship and affection you have ever shown me your tender care.

**14.2.11**  And it would be the greatest of kindnesses to me, O wielder of the discus and the mace, O glorious giver of every joy, if with a willing heart you did all this.

**14.2.12**  If only you would grant me leave to go to the forest of penance; for I see no peace, having caused the death of our grandfather, and of Karṇa, that tiger among men who never fled from battle.

**14.2.13**  By what deed I might be freed from this cruel act, O tamer of foes—ordain that deed for me here, by which my mind may be made clean."

**14.2.14**  To him who spoke thus, Vyāsa, knower of the law, of vast splendor, then spoke—comforting him with auspicious words full of meaning:

**14.2.15**  "Your understanding is unformed, my child; through childishness you are bewildered once again. Are we all to keep babbling into the empty air, over and over?

**14.2.16**  Known to you are the duties of the warrior, whose livelihood is in war; a king who has acted as ordained is not bound by the fetters of guilt.

**14.2.17**  And the entire doctrine of liberation you have heard truly as it is; more than once I myself have severed your doubts that spring from desire.

**14.2.18**  Faithless, dull of wit, your memory clearly gone astray—do not be so; such ignorance as this is not worthy of you.

**14.2.19**  All the rites of expiation are known to you, O sinless one; all the laws of war and all the laws of giving you have heard.

**14.2.20**  How is it that you, knower of all laws, master of all the sacred lore, are bewildered yet again as though from ignorance, O Bhārata?"

**14.3.1**  Vyāsa said: Yudhiṣṭhira, your discernment is not sound—such is my judgment. For no mortal of his own accord, master of himself, performs his deeds.

**14.3.2**  Appointed by the Lord, this man does the good and the evil; the person performs the deed—what cause for lamentation is there in that?

**14.3.3**  And if in the end you deem yourself a doer of evil, then hear how the evil may be drawn away from you, O Bhārata.

**14.3.4**  By austerities, by sacrifices, and by giving, O Yudhiṣṭhira, those men who have done evil deeds forever cross beyond them.

**14.3.5**  By sacrifice, by penance, and by giving, O ruler of men, O tiger among kings, are men who have done ill deeds made pure.

**14.3.6**  Both the Asuras and the gods, those great-souled ones, strive to perform the rite of sacrifice for the sake of merit; therefore sacrifices are the surest refuge.

**14.3.7**  It was through sacrifices that the great-souled gods became supreme; and then the gods, rich in rites, overpowered the Dānavas.

**14.3.8**  The Rājasūya and the Aśvamedha, the Sarvamedha, O Bhārata, and the Naramedha too—offer them, O king, O Yudhiṣṭhira.

**14.3.9**  Sacrifice with the horse-sacrifice, duly performed and richly endowed with priestly fees, with abundance of choice food and treasure, as Rāma the son of Daśaratha did.

**14.3.10**  And as did King Bharata, son of Duḥṣanta, lord of the earth, the son of Śakuntalā, of mighty valor, your own ancient forefather.

**14.3.11**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Beyond doubt the horse-sacrifice would purify even the whole earth; yet I have a certain thought, which you ought to hear here.

**14.3.12**  Having wrought this vast slaughter of my kinsmen, O best of the twice-born, I cannot bring myself to give even a small gift—and treasure I have none.

**14.3.13**  Nor can I bring myself to beg wealth of these wretched children, these sons of kings still bleeding from fresh wounds and dwelling in distress.

**14.3.14**  Having myself laid the earth waste, O most excellent of the twice-born, how shall I levy tribute for the sake of sacrifice from those given over to grief?

**14.3.15**  Through Duryodhana's transgression the earth and the lords of the earth have perished, fastening upon us an enduring infamy, O best of sages.

**14.3.16**  By Duryodhana the earth was wasted for the sake of wealth, and that treasury of the evil-minded son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra is itself emptied away.

**14.3.17**  And in this rite the earth itself is the sacrificial fee—such is the ordinance of the first and highest order, beheld by the learned; any departure from it is a lesser prescription.

**14.3.18**  And I have no wish to make any substitute for it, O you whose wealth is penance; in this, O blessed one, you ought to render me your full counsel.

**14.3.19**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by the son of Pṛthā, Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana then, having pondered a moment, spoke to the Dharma-king.

**14.3.20**  "There is treasure, O Pārtha, lying in the Himavat mountain, left behind by the brāhmaṇas at the sacrifice of King Marutta. Bring that, O son of Kuntī; it will be sufficient."

**14.3.21**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: How was that treasure heaped up at the sacrifice of Marutta, and in what age did that king come to be, O best of speakers?

**14.3.22**  Vyāsa said: If you wish to hear, O Pārtha, listen concerning King Karaṃdhama, and in what age that mighty and greatly wealthy king lived.

**14.4.1**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: I long to hear, O knower of the law, the full recital of that royal seer; O Dvaipāyana, tell me the tale of Marutta, O sinless one.

**14.4.2**  Vyāsa said: In the Kṛta age of old there was Manu, the lord who bore the rod of rule; his son was a great archer renowned by the name Prajāti.

**14.4.3**  Prajāti's son was the one widely known as Kṣupa; and Kṣupa's son was Ikṣvāku, who became a guardian of the earth and a sovereign lord.

**14.4.4**  He had a hundred sons, O king, supremely righteous; and Ikṣvāku the lord made every one of them a guardian of the earth.

**14.4.5**  The eldest of them was Viṃśa, a paragon among bowmen; and Viṃśa's noble son was named Viviṃśa, O Bhārata.

**14.4.6**  Viviṃśa had sons, O king, fifteen in number, all valiant with the bow, devoted to the brāhmaṇas, and speakers of the truth.

**14.4.7**  Given to the law of giving, virtuous, and ever sweet of speech; the eldest among them was Khanīnetra, and he oppressed them all.

**14.4.8**  But Khanīnetra, valiant though he was, having won the kingdom free of thorns, could not keep it; the people did not give him their love.

**14.4.9**  And casting him aside, that realm consecrated his son Suvarcas, O lord of kings, and then there was rejoicing.

**14.4.10**  Beholding his father's misdeed and his expulsion from the kingdom, he, self-restrained, conducted himself in the wish to do good to the people.

**14.4.11**  Devoted to the brāhmaṇas, truthful, pure, endowed with calm and self-mastery—and the people gave their love to that high-minded one, ever steadfast in the law.

**14.4.12**  As he held to the path of righteousness, his treasury and his forces wasted away; and the neighboring kings, his treasury now drained, beset him on every side.

**14.4.13**  Hard-pressed by the many, his treasury spent and his forces gone, the king fell into the deepest distress, together with his servants and his city.

**14.4.14**  Yet in his utter ruin they could not bring themselves to forsake him, for that king was right in his conduct and ever steadfast in the law, O Yudhiṣṭhira.

**14.4.15**  But when that king with his city had come to the utmost distress, then he blew upon his hand, and from it an army sprang into being.

**14.4.16**  Then he conquered all those bordering kings; and for this reason, O king, he became renowned as Karaṃdhama.

**14.4.17**  His son Kāraṃdhama was born at the dawn of the Tretā age, no less than Indra, glorious, and hard for even the gods to conquer.

**14.4.18**  In his sway all the guardians of the earth then abided; for he became their sovereign emperor, by his conduct and by his might.

**14.4.19**  His name was Avikṣit, righteous of soul, the equal of Indra in valor; devoted to sacrifice, delighting in rites, steadfast, and master of his senses.

**14.4.20**  In radiance he was the equal of the sun, in forbearance the equal of the earth, in wisdom the equal of Bṛhaspati, and steadfast as the Himālaya.

**14.4.21**  By deed, by thought, by word, by self-restraint and by tranquillity, that lord of the earth won the hearts of his subjects.

**14.4.22**  That mighty king performed a hundred horse-sacrifices according to the rites, and the learned lord Aṅgiras himself officiated as his priest.

**14.4.23**  His son surpassed his father in excellence—Marutta by name, knower of dharma, a world-emperor of great renown.

**14.4.24**  With the strength of ten thousand elephants, like a second Viṣṇu in the flesh, that righteous one, intent upon sacrifice, had vessels of gold, gleaming and pure, fashioned by the thousand.

**14.4.25**  Going to Mount Meru, on the northern slope of the Himālaya, where lies its vast golden base, he performed his rite.

**14.4.26**  There the goldsmiths, whose number none could reckon, fashioned the fire-pits and the dishes, the pots and the seats.

**14.4.27**  Close by it the sacrificial enclosure was raised, and there that righteous lord of the earth, King Marutta, performed the rite according to the ordinance, attended by all the guardians of his people.

**14.5.1**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: How great was the valour of that king, O best of speakers, and how, O brāhmaṇa, did he come to possess such an abundance of gold?

**14.5.2**  And where, blessed one, does that wealth now abide, and how, O treasury of austerities, might we be able to obtain it?

**14.5.3**  Vyāsa said: Both the Asuras and the gods, my son, were the numerous offspring of Dakṣa Prajāpati, and they contended against one another.

**14.5.4**  Likewise the two sons of Aṅgiras were equal in their vows: Bṛhaspati of vast splendour, and Saṃvarta, rich in austerity.

**14.5.5**  These two, O king, dwelt apart, rivals to each other, and Bṛhaspati harassed Saṃvarta again and again.

**14.5.6**  Being thus ever harassed by his elder brother, O Bhārata, he cast off his possessions, went clad only in the sky, and chose to dwell in the forest.

**14.5.7**  Now Vāsava, having conquered and slain all the Asuras and attained the lordship of Indra over the worlds, chose as his household priest the eldest son of Aṅgiras, Bṛhaspati, foremost of brāhmaṇas.

**14.5.8**  Now the patron of Aṅgiras in earlier days had been King Karaṃdhama, unrivalled in the world in valour, in conduct, and in might—blazing with energy like Śatakratu, righteous of soul, firm in his vows.

**14.5.9**  His mounts and his warriors and his manifold treasures, O king, came into being by his very thought, by the breath of his mouth alone.

**14.5.10**  By his virtues that ruler of men brought all kings under his sway, and having lived out the span he desired, he ascended to heaven in his own body.

**14.5.11**  His son was a knower of dharma like Yayāti—Avikṣit by name, slayer of foes, who brought the earth under his rule; in prowess and in virtue that king was as a father to his people.

**14.5.12**  His son was the valiant Marutta, the equal of Vāsava, and to him the sea-girdled earth was devoted.

**14.5.13**  That king ever contended with the lord of the gods, and Vāsava too contended with Marutta, O delight of Pāṇḍu.

**14.5.14**  Pure and full of virtue was Marutta, lord of the earth, whom Śakra, strive as he might, could not surpass.

**14.5.15**  Unable to outdo him, the bay-horsed god, together with the gods, summoned Bṛhaspati and spoke these words.

**14.5.16**  O Bṛhaspati, if you would do what is dear to me, then by no means perform for Marutta any rite—neither for the gods nor for the ancestors.

**14.5.17**  For I alone, O Bṛhaspati, have attained the lordship of Indra over the gods in the three worlds, while Marutta is but a lord of the earth.

**14.5.18**  How, O brāhmaṇa, having served as priest for the immortal lord of the gods, could you without scruple serve as priest for Marutta, who is subject to death?

**14.5.19**  Choose, then—good fortune to you—either me or Marutta the king; forsake Marutta and attend upon me as you please.

**14.5.20**  Thus addressed by the king of the gods, O Kauravya, Bṛhaspati reflected for a moment and then spoke to the lord of the gods.

**14.5.21**  You are the lord of all beings; upon you the worlds rest; you are the slayer of Namuci, of Viśvarūpa, and of Bala.

**14.5.22**  You alone, O hero, have won for the gods the highest splendour; you uphold the earth and the heavens forever, O destroyer of Bala.

**14.5.23**  Having held the priesthood for you, O lord of the hosts of gods, how should I serve as priest for the mortal Marutta, O chastiser of Pāka?

**14.5.24**  Be reassured, O lord of the gods: never for a mortal shall I take up the sacrificial ladle at any rite. Hear, too, this word of mine.

**14.5.25**  Sooner would fire become water, sooner would the earth turn upside down, sooner would the sun give no light, than my pledged word should waver.

**14.5.26**  Hearing the words of Bṛhaspati, Śakra, his jealousy gone, praised him and then entered his own abode.

**14.6.1**  Vyāsa said: On this they also relate this ancient tale, the dialogue of Bṛhaspati and Marutta, O Bhārata.

**14.6.2**  When King Marutta heard of the compact that the son of Aṅgiras had made with the king of the gods, he then gave way to wrath.

**14.6.3**  Resolving in his mind upon a sacrifice, the grandson of Karaṃdhama, eloquent of speech, approached Bṛhaspati and spoke these words.

**14.6.4**  Blessed one, treasury of austerities, the agreement for a sacrifice that I once came and made at your word, O teacher—

**14.6.5**  that rite I now wish to perform, and my materials are gathered. I am your patron, good sir; take it up, then, and conduct it.

**14.6.6**  Bṛhaspati said: I do not wish to officiate for you, O lord of the earth; I have been chosen by the king of the gods, and I have given him my pledge.

**14.6.7**  Marutta said: I am your patrimony, your own field, and I hold you in the highest regard. I have not become unworthy to be served; attend, then, to one who attends upon you.

**14.6.8**  Bṛhaspati said: Having officiated for an immortal, I will not officiate for a mortal man. Go, Marutta, or stay—from today I have given up the priestly office for you.

**14.6.9**  Today I will not be your priest. Choose, O mighty-armed one, whatever preceptor you wish, one who will perform your sacrifice for you.

**14.6.10**  Vyāsa said: Thus addressed, King Marutta was abashed, and as he came back, troubled, he met Nārada upon the road.

**14.6.11**  Meeting with the divine sage Nārada, the king stood before him with joined palms in due form, and Nārada then spoke to him.

**14.6.12**  O royal sage, you do not seem glad. Is all well with you, sinless one? Where have you been, and whence has this cause of distress come upon you?

**14.6.13**  If it is fit for me to hear, O king, tell me, O bull among rulers; I will dispel your grief by every effort, O lord of men.

**14.6.14**  Thus addressed by the great sage Nārada, Marutta laid before him all of it—the rebuff he had suffered from his preceptor.

**14.6.15**  I went to the son of Aṅgiras, Bṛhaspati, the teacher of the gods, to see him as my officiating priest for a sacrifice, and he did not welcome me.

**14.6.16**  Rejected by him, I now have no wish to live; forsaken by my teacher, I am disgraced, O Nārada.

**14.6.17**  Thus addressed by the king, Nārada answered the son of Avikṣit, O great king, reviving him, as it were, with his words.

**14.6.18**  O king, there is a son of Aṅgiras, Saṃvarta by name, a righteous man, who roams about in every direction, clad only in the sky, bewildering the folk.

**14.6.19**  Go to him, if Bṛhaspati does not wish to be your priest; pleased with you, O great king, Saṃvarta will perform your sacrifice.

**14.6.20**  Marutta said: I am brought back to life by these words of yours, O Nārada. Where might I find Saṃvarta? Tell me, O best of speakers.

**14.6.21**  And how should I conduct myself toward him, so that he may not forsake me? For if he too rejects me, I shall have no will to live.

**14.6.22**  Nārada said: Wearing the guise of a madman, he wanders about at his ease, but he frequents the city of Vārāṇasī again and again.

**14.6.23**  Going to the gate of that city, set down a corpse somewhere; he who, on seeing it, turns back—that, O lord of the earth, is Saṃvarta.

**14.6.24**  Follow him from behind wherever that mighty one goes; coming upon him in a solitary place, with joined palms take refuge with him.

**14.6.25**  If he should ask you, 'By whom was I made known to you?'—then answer, 'By Nārada,' as one stricken with grief, O slayer of foes.

**14.6.26**  And if he should question you out of a desire to come to me, then tell him without hesitation that I have entered the fire.

**14.6.27**  Vyāsa said: Promising 'So be it,' and honouring Nārada, and taking his leave, the royal sage set out for the city of Vārāṇasī.

**14.6.28**  Coming there, that man of great renown, mindful of Nārada's words, set down a corpse at the gate of the city, just as he had been told.

**14.6.29**  At that very moment the brāhmaṇa entered the city gate; then, on seeing the corpse, he turned back at once.

**14.6.30**  Marking that he had turned back, the king, son of Avikṣit, followed him from behind with joined palms, to win over Saṃvarta.

**14.6.31**  Seeing the king in a deserted place, he pelted him with dust and mud and phlegm and spittle.

**14.6.32**  Yet though thus tormented by Saṃvarta, the lord of the earth still followed that sage, with joined palms, seeking to win his grace.

**14.6.33**  Then Saṃvarta, having turned and grown weary, sat down, coming to a many-branched banyan tree of cool shade.

**14.7.1**  Saṃvarta said: How came you to know me, and by whom was I made known to you? Tell me this truly, if you desire my favour.

**14.7.2**  If you speak the truth, all your heart's desires shall be fulfilled; but if you speak falsely, your head shall burst into seven pieces.

**14.7.3**  Marutta said: It was by Nārada, as he wandered upon the road, that you were made known to me—'He is the son of your preceptor'—and therefore my joy in you is the greatest.

**14.7.4**  Saṃvarta said: This is true that you say; he knows me as a performer of sacrifices. Tell me this one thing: where is Nārada now?

**14.7.5**  Marutta said: That best of divine sages, having told me of you, then gave me his leave and entered the bearer of oblations, the fire.

**14.7.6**  Vyāsa said: Hearing this from the king, Saṃvarta, with the highest delight, then said, 'So much for him could I also do.'

**14.7.7**  Then, like a madman, the brāhmaṇa, as if rebuking Marutta with harsh words, O king, spoke to him again and again.

**14.7.8**  Wind-mad as I am, swayed only by my own whim, and thus disfigured in form—how do you wish me to serve as your priest?

**14.7.9**  My brother is competent and honoured by Vāsava, and he stands ready in the priestly office; have your rites performed by him.

**14.7.10**  My own house, and all my patrons, and the household deities, my elder brother has seized; this body alone is left to me, cast off.

**14.7.11**  Without his leave, O son of Avikṣit, never in any way could I serve as your priest, for he is most worthy of my reverence.

**14.7.12**  So go you to Bṛhaspati, and having obtained his leave, return; then I will officiate for you, if you wish to perform the sacrifice.

**14.7.13**  Marutta said: I went to Bṛhaspati already, O Saṃvarta—hear this: he does not wish me for his patron, being forbidden by Vāsava.

**14.7.14**  Having found in me an immortal as your sacrificer, O seer, do not perform the rites for a mortal — for Marutta, who is bound by the law of mortals and afflicted.

**14.7.15**  That king is forever contending with me, O brahmin. And when your brother, the slayer of Bala and Vṛtra, was thus addressed, he answered, "So be it."

**14.7.16**  Though I came to him with affection, he does not wish to treat me as his sacrificer, having taken refuge with the king of the gods — know this, O bull among sages.

**14.7.17**  Therefore I wish to have you officiate at my sacrifice, even at the cost of all I possess. I long to surpass Vāsava through the merits you would bring about.

**14.7.18**  For my mind is not inclined, O brahmin, to go to Bṛhaspati: I was rejected by him, though I had done him no wrong.

**14.7.19**  Saṃvarta said: All this you desire to accomplish at will rests firmly upon you — if, O king, you carry out every one of my intentions.

**14.7.20**  For when I officiate at your sacrifice, both Bṛhaspati and Purandara will hate you, inflamed with wrath. To this one thing you must steel yourself.

**14.7.21**  How will you remain steadfast in this? Make yourself free of doubt — lest, enraged, I now reduce you to ashes together with your kinsmen.

**14.7.22**  Marutta said: As long as the thousand-rayed sun shall burn, and as long as the mountains shall stand — for that long may I gain no worlds, if ever I abandon our compact.

**14.7.23**  And may I never attain a wholesome understanding here, whether in true knowledge or in worldly matters, if ever I abandon our compact.

**14.7.24**  Saṃvarta said: O son of Avikṣit, let an auspicious resolve settle upon your undertakings, for my own intent to officiate for you, O king, stands likewise firm.

**14.7.25**  And I shall provide for you, O king, an inexhaustible store of the finest wealth, by which you shall overcome the gods together with the gandharvas, and Śakra as well.

**14.7.26**  My mind, however, is not set upon wealth, nor again upon those for whom I officiate; rather, I wish to do what displeases both my brother and Indra.

**14.7.27**  And I shall surely bring you to equality even with Indra, and I shall do what pleases you — this I tell you in truth.

**14.8.1**  Saṃvarta said: On the back of Mount Himavat there is a mountain named Muñjavān, where the blessed lord, the consort of Umā, ever practises his austerities.

**14.8.2**  Among the roots of the great trees, in the chasms and on the peaks, in the caverns of that king of mountains, he dwells as he wills and at his ease.

**14.8.3**  There the blessed Maheśvara, bearer of the trident, of mighty splendour, abides forever with Umā for his companion, surrounded by hosts of varied beings.

**14.8.4**  There are the Rudras and the Sādhyas, the Viśvedevas and the Vasus too, and Yama and Varuṇa, and Kubera with his retinue.

**14.8.5**  And there are the spirits and the piśācas, the Nāsatyas, the two Aśvins, the gandharvas and apsarases, the yakṣas, and the divine seers as well.

**14.8.6**  The Ādityas and the Maruts, and the yātudhānas in their every kind, all wait upon the great-souled, many-formed consort of Umā.

**14.8.7**  There the blessed lord takes his delight among the followers of Kubera — deformed beings of misshapen aspect, sporting about him, O lord of earth — and blazing with glory he is seen, radiant as the rising sun.

**14.8.8**  His form cannot be seen, nor in any way his shape made out or described by any ordinary creatures whose eyes are but of flesh.

**14.8.9**  There is no heat there nor cold, no wind nor sun, no old age, neither hunger nor thirst, no death and no fear, O king.

**14.8.10**  On all the slopes of that mountain, O best of conquerors, are veins of gold, gleaming like the rays of the sun.

**14.8.11**  These are guarded by Kubera's companions with weapons upraised, who wish, O king, to do what pleases the great-souled Kubera.

**14.8.12**  Having made obeisance to that blessed one — to Śarva the ordainer, to Rudra, to the blue-throated, to the fair-formed, to the radiant —

**14.8.13**  to the wearer of matted locks, to the terrible, to the tawny-eyed and the granter of boons, to the three-eyed, to the breaker of Pūṣan's teeth, to the dwarfish and to Śiva,

**14.8.14**  to the deathly one, to him whose hair is unrevealed, to the well-conducted and to Śaṃkara, to the giver of peace, to the green-eyed, to the unmoving pillar and to the Person,

**14.8.15**  to the tawny-haired, to the shaven-headed, to the lean one and to the deliverer, to the sun-like, to the holy ford, to the god of gods, to the swift one,

**14.8.16**  to the turbaned, to the fair-faced, to the thousand-eyed, to the bountiful, to the mountain-dweller, to the tranquil, to the ascetic clad in rags,

**14.8.17**  to him whose staff is of bilva-wood, to the perfected, to the bearer of every rod of chastisement, to the great hunter of beasts, to the wielder of the bow, and to Bhava,

**14.8.18**  to the excellent, to the gentle-faced, to him whose hand holds the sacrificial beast, to the rainer of blessings, to the golden-armed, O king, to the fierce, to the lord of the quarters,

**14.8.19**  and to the lord of creatures, to the lord of beings likewise, to the bull, to the devotee of the Mothers, to the leader of armies and to the one in the midst,

**14.8.20**  to him whose hand holds the sacrificial ladle, to the lord, to the bowman and to Bhārgava, to the unborn, to the dark-eyed, and to the one of unsymmetrical eyes,

**14.8.21**  to the sharp-fanged, to the keen, to him whose mouth is the universal fire, to the greatly resplendent, to the bodiless, to the all-bodied, to the lord of progeny,

**14.8.22**  and to the lord of the seed of life, to the broad one, to him clad in a hide, to the wearer of the garland of skulls, and ever to the gold-crowned one,

**14.8.23**  to Mahādeva, to the dark one, to the three-eyed and to the sinless, to the wrathful, to the cruel, to the gentle, to the strong-armed,

**14.8.24**  to the rod-bearer, to the practiser of fierce austerity and likewise to him of cruel deeds, to the thousand-headed and to the thousand-footed — obeisance to him whose form is the svadhā offering, to the many-formed, to the fanged one.

**14.8.25**  To the bearer of the Pināka bow, to Mahādeva, to the great yogin, to the imperishable, to him whose hand holds the trident, to the granter of boons, to the three-eyed, to the lord of the world,

**14.8.26**  to the destroyer of the three cities, to the three-eyed, to the lord of the three worlds, to the mighty in vigour, to the source of all beings, to the sustainer, to the upholder of the earth,

**14.8.27**  to Īśāna, to Śaṃkara, to the All, to Śiva, to the lord of the universe, to Bhava, to the consort of Umā, to the lord of creatures, to the all-formed, to Maheśvara,

**14.8.28**  to the one of unsymmetrical eyes, to the ten-armed, to him whose banner bears the bull, to the fierce, to the unmoving, to Śiva, to the terrible, to Śarva, to the lord of Gaurī, to the Lord,

**14.8.29**  to the blue-throated, to the unborn, to the bright, to the broad, to the mighty remover, to Hara, to the all-formed, to the one of unsymmetrical eyes, to the many-formed consort of Umā —

**14.8.30**  having bowed your head to the god, to Hara who burned away the body of the Bodiless One, go for refuge to the refuge-giving Mahādeva, the four-faced.

**14.8.31**  Having thus made obeisance to that swift and great-souled Mahādeva, O lord of the earth, you shall obtain that gold. Let your men go there to fetch the gold.

**14.8.32**  Vyāsa said: Thus addressed, the son of Kāraṃdhama did as he was bidden. Then he made every preparation for the sacrifice on a superhuman scale, and the craftsmen there fashioned vessels of gold.

**14.8.33**  But Bṛhaspati, hearing of the prosperity of King Marutta, which surpassed even that of the gods, was sorely tormented.

**14.8.34**  Tormented thus, he grew utterly pale and wasted, thinking, "Saṃvarta, grown rich, will become my rival."

**14.8.35**  Hearing that Bṛhaspati was grievously distressed, the king of the gods, surrounded by the immortals, went to him and then spoke these words.

**14.9.1**  Indra said: Do you sleep in comfort, O Bṛhaspati? Are your attendants agreeable to you? Do you wish well to the gods, O brahmin? And do the gods watch over you?

**14.9.2**  Bṛhaspati said: I lie in comfort upon my couch, O great Indra; my attendants are indeed agreeable to me; I wish well to the gods, O Śakra; and the gods guard me right closely.

**14.9.3**  Indra said: Whence then this grief of mind or of body? Why are you today so pale and wan? Tell me of it, O brahmin, that I may slay all those who cause you this sorrow.

**14.9.4**  Bṛhaspati said: They say, O Maghavan, that Marutta is about to sacrifice with a great rite of bountiful fees; and I have heard that Saṃvarta is to officiate for him. This I wish: that he should not officiate for him.

**14.9.5**  Indra said: You have been granted all your desires, O brahmin, you who counsel the gods as their chaplain; and both birth and death have been transcended by you. What, then, can Saṃvarta do to you today, O brahmin?

**14.9.6**  Bṛhaspati said: Even now, having driven back the asuras with the gods, you still seek to slay them along with their kindred. Wherever you behold one grown prosperous, there is the sorrow of it — that a rival should flourish.

**14.9.7**  For this reason, O lord of the gods, my color has faded: my rival grows in power. Hearing this, O Maghavan, restrain by every means either Saṃvarta or King Marutta.

**14.9.8**  Indra said: Come, go forth as my envoy, O Jātavedas, to deliver Bṛhaspati over to Marutta, saying: "This Bṛhaspati shall officiate at your sacrifice, and shall likewise make you immortal."

**14.9.9**  Agni said: Here I go today as your envoy, O Śakra, to deliver Bṛhaspati over to Marutta — to make the word of the much-invoked one come true, and wishing to do honor to Bṛhaspati.

**14.9.10**  Vyāsa said: Then the great-souled, smoke-bannered one set forth, crushing the great trees and the creepers, moving at will about the edge of the snow, swifter than the wind that outstrips the very wood, and roaring.

**14.9.11**  Marutta said: A marvel I behold today — Fire come hither in bodily form! Bring forth a seat, water, an offering for the feet, and a cow, O sage.

**14.9.12**  Agni said: I welcome your seat, your water, your offering for the feet, O sinless one; but know that I have come as an envoy, dispatched by Indra.

**14.9.13**  Marutta said: Is the glorious king of the gods well and content? Is he pleased with us, O smoke-bannered one? Are the gods duly under his sway? Tell me all of it in full, O god.

**14.9.14**  Agni said: Śakra is most exceedingly content, O lord of kings, and he desires an undying friendship with you. All the gods, O king, are obedient to him. Now hear from me the message of the king of the gods.

**14.9.15**  For this purpose he sent me into your presence — to deliver Bṛhaspati over to Marutta: let this preceptor officiate at your sacrifice, O king, and though you are mortal, let him make you immortal.

**14.9.16**  Marutta said: This brahmin Saṃvarta is the one who shall officiate for me; and to Bṛhaspati here are my joined palms in salutation. Having served as priest to the god, the great Indra, it would ill become him today to officiate for one who is mortal.

**14.9.17**  Agni said: Those great worlds that are in the realm of the gods, you shall attain through the favor of the king of the gods, if Bṛhaspati should officiate for you; surely you would win heaven, crowned with renown.

**14.9.18**  Likewise the worlds of men and those that are divine, and the great worlds of Prajāpati too — all these would be won by you, and the whole sovereignty of the gods, if Bṛhaspati should officiate for you, O lord of men.

**14.9.19**  Saṃvarta said: Do not come to us again like this, in any wise, to deliver Bṛhaspati over to Marutta — lest I burn you with my dreadful eye when I am enraged, O Pāvaka. Mark this well.

**14.9.20**  Vyāsa said: Then the smoke-bannered one went off to the gods, afraid of being burned, trembling like a leaf of the aśvattha tree. Beholding him, the great-souled Śakra spoke to the bearer of oblations in the presence of Bṛhaspati.

**14.9.21**  You who were sent and went forth, O Jātavedas, to deliver Bṛhaspati over to Marutta — what did that king, about to sacrifice, say to you? Does he accept the message, or not?

**14.9.22**  Agni said: Marutta does not relish your word; he offered joined palms in salutation to Bṛhaspati, saying again and again, "Saṃvarta is to officiate for me" — though I urged him repeatedly.

**14.9.23**  He said this with conviction: "Though I might gain the worlds of men and those that are divine, and the great worlds of Prajāpati, by making a compact with him — even then I would not wish it."

**14.9.24**  Indra said: Go once more to that king and convey my word, charged with what is to his own good. If, told again, he will not do my bidding, then I shall hurl the thunderbolt against him.

**14.9.25**  Agni said: Let this king of the gandharvas go there as the envoy; I am afraid to go there, O Vāsava. Saṃvarta, of fierce wrath, his vow of chastity fulfilled, spoke to me in fury these words:

**14.9.26**  "If you should come again in any wise like this, to deliver Bṛhaspati over to Marutta, I would burn you with my dreadful eye in my rage." Understand this, O Śakra.

**14.9.27**  Indra said: You yourself are the one who burns others, O Jātavedas, for none other than you is found to reduce things to ashes; the whole world fears your touch. What you say is past belief, O bearer of oblations.

**14.9.28**  Agni said: Heaven, O king of the gods, and all the earth you could enfold by your own might alone, O Śakra. Yet, you being of such a kind, how was it that Vṛtra once carried off the triple heaven?

**14.9.29**  Indra said: I am no slayer of the small and helpless, no creeping thing nor she-elephant; I do not drink mere water in place of soma, O Fire, nor do I loose the thunderbolt upon the weak. What mortal could strike at me to my hurt?

**14.9.30**  I drove the Kālakeyas into exile upon the earth; I dragged the dānavas down from the firmament; I brought Prahrāda from heaven to his final end. What mortal could strike at me to my hurt?

**14.9.31**  Agni said: When Cyavana, about to officiate for Śaryāti, took the soma single-handed together with the Aśvins, you in your anger sought to forbid it of old — recall that sacrifice of Śaryāti, O great Indra.

**14.9.32**  And you, Puraṃdara, took up the thunderbolt and hurled it most terribly at Cyavana; but that brahman, his wrath born of austerity, seized your arm together with the very thunderbolt and held it fast.

**14.9.33**  Then out of fury he brought into being a rival for you, dreadful of form on every side, an asura named Mada of all-pervading shape, at the mere sight of whom you shut your eyes.

**14.9.34**  One jaw of that mighty dānava rested upon the earth, the other reached to heaven; a thousand teeth he had, each a hundred yojanas long, exceedingly sharp, of dreadful aspect.

**14.9.35**  His four tusks were round and massive, the color of pillars of silver, two hundred yojanas in length; gnashing his teeth he rushed upon you, raising a terrible spear in his desire to slay.

**14.9.36**  You could not bear to look upon that one of dreadful form, though all the others gazed upon him as upon a thing of wonder; and out of fear of him you came, slayer of dānavas, with palms joined, to take refuge with the great sage.

**14.9.37**  Thus the power of the brahman is mightier than that of the kṣatriya; there is nothing whatever mightier than the brahman. Knowing as I do the splendor of the brahman aright, I have no wish, O Śakra, to go against Saṃvarta.

**14.10.1**  Indra said: True it is that the power of the brahman is mightier, and that nothing whatever is mightier than the brahman; yet the might of Āvikṣita's son I will not endure—upon him I shall hurl the terrible thunderbolt.

**14.10.2**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra, go as my envoy and say to Marutta, who stands together with Saṃvarta: O king, accept Bṛhaspati for your priest, or else I shall hurl the terrible thunderbolt upon you.

**14.10.3**  Vyāsa said: Then the gandharva Dhṛtarāṣṭra went and spoke to the king these words of Vāsava: Know me, O king, to be the gandharva Dhṛtarāṣṭra, come to you with a message to deliver.

**14.10.4**  Hear from me, lion among kings, the word of Indra which the great-souled lord of the worlds has spoken: Choose Bṛhaspati for your officiating priest, or else I shall hurl the terrible thunderbolt upon you; if you will not do this my bidding—so much has he of inconceivable deeds declared.

**14.10.5**  Marutta said: You yourself know this, and Puraṃdara too, the Viśvedevas, the Vasus, and the two Aśvins—that for treachery to a friend there is no expiation; such has ever been the saying in the worlds.

**14.10.6**  Let Bṛhaspati officiate for great Indra, the best of the gods, the foremost of the wielders of the thunderbolt; but Saṃvarta shall officiate for me this day, O king. Neither your word nor his is to my liking.

**14.10.7**  The gandharva said: A terrible roar is heard, lion among kings—the roar of Vāsava thundering in the vault of the sky. Surely great Indra is about to loose the thunderbolt upon you. Think now, O king, of your safety; this is the moment.

**14.10.8**  Vyāsa said: Thus addressed by Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and hearing the roar of the thundering Vāsava, the king made known the matter to Saṃvarta—that one ever devoted to austerity, the foremost of those who know the dharma.

**14.10.9**  Marutta said: I behold yonder, floating near, a great mass like a stone, and by it the path ahead is hidden from view. I seek shelter, foremost of brahmans, from you; grant me, best of brahmans, refuge from that peril.

**14.10.10**  Here comes the wielder of the thunderbolt, lighting up the ten directions; by his dreadful, more-than-human form our priests at the sacrifice are stricken with terror.

**14.10.11**  Saṃvarta said: Let your fear of Śakra depart, lion among kings; this most dreadful terror I shall straightway drive away by the spell of immobilizing power. Be not afraid of this, and be of good cheer.

**14.10.12**  I will hold them fast and bound; fear not Śakra, O king. By me the weapons of all the gods can be made of no avail.

**14.10.13**  Let the thunderbolt wander off to the quarters of the sky, let the wind come, let it fall as rain upon the forests; let the waters drift idly in the air, let the lightning be seen flashing—but you, fear not.

**14.10.14**  Or let fire guard you on every side; let Vāsava rain his rain as he pleases; and let the wind hold fast the thunderbolt, most dreadful as it floats amid floods of water.

**14.10.15**  Marutta said: A terrible sound is heard, a mighty roar—this is the thunderbolt joined with the wind; my heart trembles again and again, and no ease comes to me this day, O brahman.

**14.10.16**  Saṃvarta said: Let your fear of the fierce thunderbolt depart this day; becoming the wind myself, O king, I strike down the thunderbolt. Set fear aside and choose some other boon: what desire of yours shall I accomplish by my austerity?

**14.10.17**  Marutta said: Let Indra himself come forth at once, O brahman, and accept the oblation at the sacrifice; let the gods each take their own seats and receive the pressed soma.

**14.10.18**  Saṃvarta said: Here comes Indra with his bay steeds, O king, the soma-drinker, together with all the gods, summoned by my mantras to this sacrifice this day; behold him, his body drawn hither by the power of the spell.

**14.10.19**  Vyāsa said: Then the king of the gods, attended by the gods, having yoked to his chariot those bay horses, the foremost of steeds, came to the sacrifice of the immeasurable son of Āvikṣita, thirsting for the soma.

**14.10.20**  As he came accompanied by the hosts of gods, Marutta with his priest went forth to meet him, and offered foremost worship to the king of the gods, according to scripture and rite, with gladness of heart.

**14.10.21**  Saṃvarta said: Welcome to you here, O learned Puruhūta; this sacrifice shines forth the more now that you are present, O Indra, slayer of Bala and Vṛtra. Drink the soma, pressed and held ready by me.

**14.10.22**  Marutta said: Look upon me with favor, and homage be to you; my sacrifice is achieved, my life is fruitful. This one performs my sacrifice for me, O lord of the gods—he who is younger than Bṛhaspati by birth.

**14.10.23**  Indra said: I know this teacher of yours, rich in austerity, the younger brother of Bṛhaspati, of keen splendor, at whose summons I have come, O king. My displeasure toward you is now gone; my favor rests upon you this day.

**14.10.24**  Saṃvarta said: If you are indeed pleased, O king of the gods, then ordain yourself the rite at this sacrifice; arrange yourself all its courses, O lord of the gods, that all the world may know it, O god.

**14.10.25**  Vyāsa said: Thus addressed by the son of Aṅgiras, Śakra himself gave command to the gods: Let assembly halls be made, and chief dwellings by the thousand, rich and with floors of varied splendor.

**14.10.26**  With pillars set in place, make ascents straightway for the gandharvas and the apsarases, where the apsarases by the thousand may dance; let the sacrificial enclosure be made to seem a very image of heaven.

**14.10.27**  Thus bidden, the dwellers in heaven did all this swiftly and gladly at Śakra's word, O king; then Indra, well pleased, spoke to the king, honoring Marutta.

**14.10.28**  Here am I come together with you, O king, and the other kings, your forefathers, are here as well; let all the other deities too, well pleased, accept the oblation from you, O king.

**14.10.29**  Let them sacrifice the red beast for Agni, and the many-formed offering for the Viśvedevas, O resplendent one; let the foremost of brahmans offer up the dark, pure bull of vigorous member that I have appointed.

**14.10.30**  Then that king's sacrifice grew great, wherein the gods themselves took up the food, and wherein Śakra, lord of the gods and master of the bay steeds, honored by the brahmans, sat as a member of the assembly.

**14.10.31**  Then the great-souled Saṃvarta, mounted upon the altar like a second blazing fire, summoning aloud the hosts of gods, poured the oblations into the flame with the proper mantras, full of confidence.

**14.10.32**  Then the slayer of Bala, having drunk the foremost soma, and the other soma-drinking dwellers of heaven as well, all of them, sated to their content and well pleased, took their leave of the king and departed.

**14.10.33**  Then the king, rejoicing, had heaps of gold piled up at every step, and bestowing abundant wealth upon the twice-born, that slayer of foes shone like the lord of riches himself.

**14.10.34**  Then, having gathered wealth of many kinds and filled his treasury to his utmost, and having taken his leave with his teacher's consent, he returned and ruled the whole earth to the bounds of the sea.

**14.10.35**  Such of qualities was that king, at whose sacrifice gold was so abundant. Take you that wealth, O king, and offer sacrifice, gladdening the gods with due observances.

**14.10.36**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then the Pāṇḍava king, glad of countenance, hearing the words of the son of Satyavatī, set his mind upon sacrificing with that wealth; and thereupon he took counsel again with his ministers.

**14.11.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When the king had been thus addressed by Vyāsa of wondrous deeds, then Vāsudeva of great splendor took up the discourse.

**14.11.2**  That king, downcast in mind, his kinsmen and friends slain, was like the sun in eclipse, like a fire wrapped in smoke.

**14.11.3**  Knowing the son of Pṛthā to be despondent in mind, the upholder of the Vṛṣṇi line, consoling the son of Dharma, began to speak.

**14.11.4**  Vāsudeva said: All that is crooked is the abode of death; uprightness is the abode of Brahman. Such, and no more, is the whole province of knowledge. What will idle lamenting avail?

**14.11.5**  Your work is not yet finished, nor are your enemies conquered. How is it that you do not perceive the enemy that dwells within your own body—your own self?

**14.11.6**  On this I will relate to you, according to dharma and according to what I have heard, how the battle of Indra with Vṛtra came to pass.

**14.11.7**  Of old, O king, the earth was pervaded by Vṛtra, so it is told. Seeing the earth thus pervaded, and the province of smell carried off, there arose a foul stench—the province that came of the earth's being seized.

**14.11.8**  Then the lord of a hundred sacrifices grew wroth at the carrying off of the province of smell, and in his anger he let loose the terrible thunderbolt upon Vṛtra.

**14.11.9**  Being smitten by the thunderbolt of vast power, he straightway entered into the waters, abandoning the earth, and seized then the province of the waters.

**14.11.10**  When the waters had been pervaded by Vṛtra, and the province of taste carried off, the lord of a hundred sacrifices, sore angered, loosed the thunderbolt upon them.

**14.11.11**  Being smitten in the water by the thunderbolt of vast power, he straightway entered into light, and seized then the province of light.

**14.11.12**  When light had been pervaded by Vṛtra, and the province of form carried off, the lord of a hundred sacrifices, sore angered, loosed the thunderbolt there.

**14.11.13**  Being smitten hard by the thunderbolt of vast power, he straightway entered into the wind, and seized then the province of wind.

**14.11.14**  When the wind had been pervaded by Vṛtra, and the province of touch carried off, the lord of a hundred sacrifices, sore angered, loosed the thunderbolt there.

**14.11.15**  Being smitten therein by the thunderbolt of measureless power, he rushed into the ether, and seized then its province.

**14.11.16**  When the ether had become Vṛtra, and the province of sound carried off, the lord of a hundred sacrifices, sore angered, loosed the thunderbolt there.

**14.11.17**  Being smitten therein by the thunderbolt of measureless power, he straightway entered into Śakra himself, and seized then his province.

**14.11.18**  As he was thus seized by Vṛtra, a great bewilderment came over him; but Vasiṣṭha, my son, roused him again to consciousness with the Rathaṃtara hymn.

**14.11.19**  Then the lord of a hundred sacrifices slew Vṛtra, who dwelt within his body, O bull of the Bharatas, with an unseen thunderbolt—so have we heard it here.

**14.11.20**  This secret of dharma was declared by Śakra among the great sages, and by the seers it was told to me; learn it now, O king.

**14.12.1**  Vāsudeva said: Disease arises of two kinds, of the body and of the mind; the one is born of the other, and neither is found to arise wholly apart from its fellow.

**14.12.2**  The disease of the body is born in the body, of that there is no doubt; and the disease of the mind, it is determined, is born in the mind alone.

**14.12.3**  Cold, heat, and wind, O king, are the qualities born of the body; when these qualities are in balance, that they call the mark of health. Cold is subdued by heat, and heat is subdued by cold.

**14.12.4**  Goodness, passion, and darkness—these three are reckoned the qualities of the soul; when these qualities are in balance, that they call the mark of health. And when any one of them rises in excess, a remedy is prescribed for it.

**14.12.5**  Sorrow is subdued by joy, and joy is subdued by sorrow; one man, dwelling in misery, wishes to remember his happiness, while another, dwelling in happiness, wishes to remember his misery.

**14.12.6**  But you, O son of Kuntī, being neither now in misery would recall your misery, nor in deep happiness would recall your happiness—for fate is the stronger force.

**14.12.7**  Or perhaps this is your very nature, by which you are drawn down, O Pārtha; for when you saw Kṛṣṇā brought into the assembly hall, in a single garment, in her courses, before the very eyes of the Pāṇḍavas—that you do not wish to remember.

**14.12.8**  The banishment from the city, the sending forth clad in deerskins, and the dwelling in the great forest—of that you do not wish to remember.

**14.12.9**  The affliction at the hands of Jaṭāsura, the combat with Citrasena, and the affliction from the king of the Sindhus—of that you do not wish to remember.

**14.12.10**  And again, during the time of concealment, the kicking of Yājñasenī by Kīcaka—of that too, O Pārtha, you do not wish to remember.

**14.12.11**  The war you waged against Droṇa and Bhīṣma, O subduer of foes—even such a war is now at hand for you, to be fought with the mind alone; therefore you must take it up for battle, O bull of the Bharatas.

**14.12.12**  It is the highest war, against that whose form is unmanifest, won by setting aside the highest through one's own deeds—wherein there is no need of arrows, nor of servants, nor of kinsmen; it must be fought with the self alone, and that war is now at hand for you.

**14.12.13**  Should that war remain unwon, to what state will you come? But once you have understood this, O son of Kuntī, you will have accomplished your purpose.

**14.12.14**  Having resolved upon this wisdom—the coming and the going of all beings—rule the kingdom fittingly, in the way of your fathers and forefathers.

**14.13.1**  Vāsudeva said: Not by casting off outward possessions does perfection come, O Bhārata; by casting off the body's own substance, perfection may come—or it may not.

**14.13.2**  For one who has renounced outward possessions yet still hankers after things of the body—whatever his dharma, whatever his happiness, let that be the lot of our enemies.

**14.13.3**  Of two syllables is death, of three the eternal Brahman: 'mine' (mama), of two syllables, is death; 'not mine' (na mama) is the eternal.

**14.13.4**  Brahman and death, O king—both of these are established within the self alone; unseen, they set all beings to war with one another, beyond all doubt.

**14.13.5**  If, O Bhārata, the imperishability of this living essence is settled and certain, then in cleaving the body of creatures one in truth attains to non-injury.

**14.13.6**  Even having gained the whole earth, with all that is unmoving and moving upon it, what will he do with it, in whom there is no sense of "mine"?

**14.13.7**  Or again, O Pārtha, though a man dwell in the forest and live on its wild produce, if the sense of "mine" toward possessions remains in him, he abides within the very jaws of death.

**14.13.8**  Look, O Bhārata, upon the nature of one's enemies, outer and inner alike; the being who does not perceive that nature is freed from the great fear.

**14.13.9**  In the world men do not praise the one who is wholly given over to desire; yet without desire there is no undertaking at all. For giving, the study of the Veda, austerity, and the rites prescribed in the Veda — these are all set in motion by desire.

**14.13.10**  There is no man who, having understood, undertakes vows, sacrifices, observances, and the practice of meditation otherwise than through desire. Whatever this desire seeks, that becomes the duty pursued; for it is not duty, but restraint of desire, that is the root of such striving.

**14.13.11**  On this theme the knowers of old recite verses sung by Desire itself; hear them, O Yudhiṣṭhira, recited now in their entirety.

**14.13.12**  "I cannot be slain by any creature through means unfit for the task. Whoever, knowing where strength lies, strives to slay me by striking — in that very striking I, Desire, arise again within him."

**14.13.13**  "Whoever strives to slay me by sacrifices with their manifold fees, in him, as one whose self is bent on action, I arise again, as it were among moving things."

**14.13.14**  "Whoever strives to slay me by the Vedas and the disciplines of the Vedānta, in him, his self grown calm, I arise again, as it were among unmoving things."

**14.13.15**  "Whoever, with truth for his valor, strives to slay me by steadfast resolve — of him I become the very inclination of his heart, and he does not perceive me."

**14.13.16**  "Whoever, his vows made keen, strives to slay me by austerity, then in that very austerity of his I arise again."

**14.13.17**  "Whoever, a learned man, strives to slay me by taking his stand upon liberation — at him, fixed in the joy of release, I dance and I laugh; for I alone am eternal, not to be slain by any being."

**14.13.18**  Therefore do you too, O great king, by sacrifices with their manifold fees, turn that very desire into duty; there it shall serve your purpose.

**14.13.19**  Offer the Horse Sacrifice according to the rule, with its due fees, and other manifold sacrifices besides, abundant and complete in their gifts.

**14.13.20**  Let there be no anguish in you as you gaze again and again upon your slain kinsmen; those who fell on this field of battle can never be seen again.

**14.13.21**  You, then, having worshipped with great sacrifices, abundant and complete in their gifts, will win the highest renown in the world and attain the supreme goal.

**14.14.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus, by manifold sayings of those sages rich in austerity, was the royal sage Yudhiṣṭhira, his kinsmen slain, comforted and consoled.

**14.14.2**  That sovereign was won over by the blessed Viṣṭaraśravas himself, by Dvaipāyana, by Kṛṣṇa, and by Devasthāna.

**14.14.3**  The king was consoled too by Nārada, by Bhīma, by Nakula, by Kṛṣṇā, by Sahadeva, and by the wise Vijaya.

**14.14.4**  And by other tigers among men, by brāhmaṇas whose insight rested on the scriptures, he cast off the grief born of sorrow and the burning anguish of his mind.

**14.14.5**  Yudhiṣṭhira honored the gods and the brāhmaṇas, and having performed the funeral rites for his kinsmen, that king, righteous of soul, once more ruled the earth girdled by the sea.

**14.14.6**  His mind grown calm, the Kaurava king, having gained his undivided kingdom, spoke to Vyāsa and Nārada and those others.

**14.14.7**  "I have been consoled by you, elders and bulls among sages; not even the slightest trace of false grief remains in me now."

**14.14.8**  "Great wealth has been won, by which I shall worship the gods; placing you here at the head, we shall bring the sacrifice to completion."

**14.14.9**  "Guarded by you, grandsire, we shall journey to the Himavat; for that land, O best of the twice-born, is said to be full of wonders."

**14.14.10**  Likewise much that was wondrous and salutary was spoken by the blessed one, by the divine sage Nārada, and by Devasthāna.

**14.14.11**  No man devoid of good fortune ever obtains, when calamity befalls him, such teachers as these — friends esteemed by the good.

**14.14.12**  Thus addressed by the king, all those great sages, having taken their leave of him and of both Kṛṣṇa and Phalguna, vanished from sight then and there, before the eyes of all.

**14.14.13**  Then the king, the son of Dharma, the lord, sat down there; and thus no very long time passed for them.

**14.14.14**  As they performed the rites of purification at the death of Bhīṣma, bestowing great gifts upon the brāhmaṇas as offerings for the departed,

**14.14.15**  for the Kurus led by Bhīṣma and Karṇa, O delight of the Kurus, the king, together with Dhṛtarāṣṭra, bestowed the offerings for the departed.

**14.14.16**  Then, having given much wealth to the brāhmaṇas, the bull of the Pāṇḍavas, placing Dhṛtarāṣṭra at the head, entered the city named for the elephant.

**14.14.17**  Having consoled his father, the lord whose sight was wisdom, that righteous-souled one ruled the earth together with his brothers.

**14.15.1**  Janamejaya said: When the realm had been won by the Pāṇḍavas and brought to peace, O best of the twice-born, what did the two heroes Vāsudeva and Dhanaṃjaya then do?

**14.15.2**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When the realm had been won by the Pāṇḍavas and brought to peace, O lord of peoples, Vāsudeva and Dhanaṃjaya grew glad of heart.

**14.15.3**  Joined in joy, those two disported themselves like the two lords of the gods in heaven, in lovely forests and upon the slopes of mountains,

**14.15.4**  upon delightful crags, by pools and by rivers, wandering full of delight, like the two Aśvins in the Nandana grove.

**14.15.5**  In Indraprastha the two great souls, Kṛṣṇa and the Pāṇḍava, took their pleasure, and entering that lovely assembly hall they disported themselves, O Bhārata.

**14.15.6**  There, O king, in the course of one tale and another, they recounted at that time the wondrous stories of the war and the hardships they had borne.

**14.15.7**  The two great souls, ancient and best of sages, taking delight in one another, then rehearsed the lineages of the seers and of the gods.

**14.15.8**  And Keśava, knower of all certainties, recounted to Pārtha tales sweet and wondrous, settled in subtle meaning and well-chosen word.

**14.15.9**  With his tales, the descendant of Śūra, Janārdana, soothed Pārtha, who was scorched by grief for his son and for kinsmen by the thousand.

**14.15.10**  Having duly consoled him, the Sātvata, knower of the right ordering and great in austere power, lifted as it were the burden from his own heart and took his rest.

**14.15.11**  Then at the close of their talk Govinda spoke to Guḍākeśa, soothing him with gentle speech, these words full of sound reason.

**14.15.12**  "This whole earth has been conquered, O Savyasācin, scorcher of foes, by the king, the son of Dharma, leaning upon the strength of your arms."

**14.15.13**  "Yudhiṣṭhira, the king of righteousness, enjoys the earth free of all rivals, O best of men, by the might of Bhīmasena and of the twins."

**14.15.14**  "By righteousness, O knower of righteousness, the king has gained a kingdom without a thorn; by righteousness too was King Suyodhana slain in battle."

**14.15.15**  "The sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra — delighting in unrighteousness, greedy, ever speaking hateful words, evil of soul — have been laid low together with all their followers."

**14.15.16**  "The whole earth brought to peace, O Pārtha, the lord of the earth, King Dharma's son, enjoys, guarded by you, O foremost of the Kurus."

**14.15.17**  "I take delight in your company even in the wild forests, O Pāṇḍava — how much more here, where these our people are, and Pṛthā too, O wearer-down of foes."

**14.15.18**  "Where King Dharma's son is, where Bhīma of mighty strength is, where the two sons of Mādravatī are — there is my highest delight."

**14.15.19**  "And so it is in these assembly halls like very heaven, O Bhārata, delightful and holy, when I am here together with you, O sinless one."

**14.15.20**  "But a long time has now passed for me without sight of the son of Śūra, and of Baladeva, O Kaurava, and of the other bulls among the Vṛṣṇis."

**14.15.21**  "I therefore wish to journey toward the city of Dvāravatī; let my going be approved by you also, O bull among men."

**14.15.22**  "King Yudhiṣṭhira has been counseled in many ways, time and again, by Bhīṣma — and by us too, when he was overcome by grief."

**14.15.23**  "Yudhiṣṭhira the Pāṇḍava, though himself a teacher, was instructed by us, and that counsel was rightly received by that great soul."

**14.15.24**  "For in Dharma's son — knower of righteousness, grateful, speaker of truth — there is truth, there is righteousness, there is supreme understanding, and a steadfastness forever firm."

**14.15.25**  "Therefore, Arjuna, if it please you, go to that great soul, the lord of men, and speak to him the word concerning our departure."

**14.15.26**  "For I would do nothing displeasing to him, even were the loss of life at hand — how much less would I go toward the city of Dvāravatī against his wish, O mighty-armed one."

**14.15.27**  "All this, O Pārtha, I speak out of desire for your joy and your good; it is the truth, O Kaurava, and in no way is it false."

**14.15.28**  "And the purpose of my dwelling here is accomplished, Arjuna: the king, son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, has been slain, together with his army and his followers."

**14.15.29**  "And the earth, my friend, sea-girt, with its mountains, woods, and forests, is set beneath the sway of the wise son of Dharma, heaped with jewels of many kinds — the earth of the Kuru king, O Pāṇḍava."

**14.15.30**  "By righteousness let the king, the knower of righteousness, protect all the bounteous earth — attended by many perfected great souls and ever praised by the bards, O bull of the Bharatas."

**14.15.31**  "So come with me now and ask leave of the king, the increaser of the Kurus, O tiger of the Kurus, for the journey toward Dvārakā."

**14.15.32**  "This body, and whatever wealth is in my house, has always, O Pārtha, been dedicated to Yudhiṣṭhira; for Yudhiṣṭhira, the high-minded lord of the Kurus, is ever dear to me and honored by me."

**14.15.33**  "And for dwelling here I have no purpose left, O mighty-armed, save you alone; for the earth, O Pārtha, abides under the rule of your venerable, well-conducted elder, Yudhiṣṭhira."

**14.15.34**  Thus addressed by the great soul Janārdana, Arjuna of measureless valor, O king, uttered with difficulty, as it were, the word "So be it," and paid full honor to Janārdana.

**14.16.1**  Janamejaya said: As the two great souls, Keśava and Arjuna, dwelt in that hall, their foes slain, what discourse, O brāhmaṇa, then arose between them?

**14.16.2**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Together with Kṛṣṇa, Pārtha, having gained his undivided kingdom, disported himself with joy in that lovely hall.

**14.16.3**  Then, O king, those two, glad of heart and surrounded by their own people, came by chance to a certain quarter of the hall, like some quarter of heaven.

**14.16.4**  Then the Pāṇḍava Arjuna, content, together with Kṛṣṇa, gazing upon that lovely hall, spoke these words.

**14.16.5**  "Your greatness, O mighty-armed son of Devakī, became known to me when the battle was upon us, and that sovereign form of yours as well."

**14.16.6**  "But that which you then declared, O Keśava, out of friendship — all of it, O tiger among men, is lost to me, for my mind was overthrown."

**14.16.7**  "Yet I have a longing to hear those matters once again, O lord; and you, O Mādhava, will before long be setting out for Dvārakā."

**14.16.8**  Thus addressed, Kṛṣṇa of great splendor, the best of speakers, embraced Phalguna and answered him with these words.

**14.16.9**  "By me you were made to hear the secret, O Pārtha, and were made to know the eternal Dharma in its own true nature, and all the everlasting worlds."

**14.16.10**  "That you did not grasp it, failing to understand, is a very great grief to me; surely you are wanting in faith, and dull of wit, O Pāṇḍava."

**14.16.11**  "For that Dharma is fully sufficient for the knowing of the abode of Brahman; yet I cannot again declare it just so, in its entirety."

**14.16.12**  "For the supreme Brahman was then declared by me, joined as I was in yoga; but now I shall recount an ancient tale bearing upon that very matter."

**14.16.13**  How, taking your stand upon that understanding, you shall attain the supreme goal — hear it all from me as I speak, O best of those who uphold the Law.

**14.16.14**  A certain brāhmaṇa once came, O subduer of foes, from the world of heaven and from the world of Brahmā — one hard to assail — and by us he was duly honored.

**14.16.15**  And being questioned by us, what he declared, O bull of the Bharatas, by a divine method, O Pārtha — hear that with an undoubting mind.

**14.16.16**  The brāhmaṇa said: That which you ask of me, O Kṛṣṇa, resting upon the Law of liberation — for the sake of compassion toward beings, that which cuts away delusion, O Lord —

**14.16.17**  that I shall fully declare to you just as it is, O slayer of Madhu; listen attentively as I speak, O Mādhava.

**14.16.18**  A certain learned brāhmaṇa, Kāśyapa, devoted to austerity and most versed in the Law, approached a certain twice-born who had mastered the traditions of dharma,

**14.16.19**  one who had come and gone many times, who had crossed to the far shore of knowledge and discernment, skilled in the truth and meaning of the worlds, a knower of pleasure and pain,

**14.16.20**  a knower of the truth of birth and death, expert in merit and demerit, a seer of the high and low destinies that embodied beings reach by their deeds,

**14.16.21**  who wandered like one already liberated, a perfected one, serene, his senses restrained, blazing with the splendor of Brahman and moving everywhere at will,

**14.16.22**  and one who knew the art of vanishing — so Kāśyapa truly learned — moving along with hidden perfected ones who bore the discus,

**14.16.23**  conversing with them apart and sitting together with them, and going wherever he wished, unattached like the wind.

**14.16.24**  Approaching him, that wise and most excellent twice-born, an ascetic eager for the Law, well-composed, did him reverence at his feet as is proper, filled with the highest devotion.

**14.16.25**  And amazed at seeing this marvel, Kāśyapa gratified that most excellent twice-born, his learned teacher, with great and attentive service.

**14.16.26**  With a glad heart, fit and endowed with learning and good conduct, that scorcher of foes pleased him sincerely with the service due to a teacher.

**14.16.27**  Pleased and gracious toward that pupil, the teacher then spoke, looking to the highest perfection — hear it from me, O Janārdana.

**14.16.28**  By manifold deeds, my son, and by purely meritorious practices, mortals reach their destiny here and a dwelling even in the world of the gods.

**14.16.29**  Nowhere is there utter happiness, nowhere an everlasting station; and there is a fall, again and again, from a high place won with pain.

**14.16.30**  Evil and grievous destinies have I reached through the pursuit of sin, overcome by desire and wrath and deluded by craving.

**14.16.31**  Again and again death, and birth again and again; manifold foods have I eaten, and many kinds of breasts have I suckled.

**14.16.32**  Manifold mothers have I seen, and fathers of many kinds, and varied joys and sorrows, O sinless one.

**14.16.33**  Often parting from those I loved and dwelling together with those I disliked; and loss of wealth came upon me after I had won that wealth with pain.

**14.16.34**  And grievous humiliations, from strangers and likewise from my own kin, and agonies of body and mind, exceedingly cruel.

**14.16.35**  Fierce indignities have come upon me, and terrible slayings and bondages, and a fall into hell, and torments in the realm of Yama.

**14.16.36**  Old age and diseases without end, and lingering distresses in abundance, born of the pairs of opposites, have all been keenly suffered by me in this world.

**14.16.37**  Then at last, out of revulsion, out of humiliations and treacheries, the whole machinery of worldly life was wholly abandoned by me, sorely afflicted; and so this perfection was attained by me through the grace of my own self.

**14.16.38**  No more shall I come hither; I look upon the worlds from above. Up to perfection, up to the creation of beings, blessed is the course of my self.

**14.16.39**  This highest perfection has been won, O best of the twice-born; from here I shall go beyond, and beyond that again to the untroubled station of Brahman — let there be no doubt of this in you.

**14.16.40**  No more shall I come again to the world of mortals, O scorcher of foes. I am pleased with you, O greatly wise one; say what I may do for you.

**14.16.41**  For whatever you have come seeking, the time for it has now arrived; and I know full well the purpose for which you have come to me. But soon I shall depart, by that path toward which I have urged you.

**14.16.42**  Greatly am I pleased with your conduct, O discerning one; ask, while I may speak to you whatever you desire.

**14.16.43**  I esteem your understanding highly and honor it greatly, for by you I have been rightly understood; for you are wise, O Kāśyapa.

**14.17.1**  Vāsudeva said: Then, clasping his feet, he asked questions exceedingly hard to answer; and that best of upholders of the Law declared them all.

**14.17.2**  Kāśyapa said: How does the body fall away, and how does it come into being? And how, wandering through grievous transmigration, is one set free?

**14.17.3**  And how, having yoked the self, does it release that body? And freed from the body, how does it pass on to another?

**14.17.4**  And how does this man enjoy the fruits of the good and evil deeds he himself has done? And where does the deed abide for one who is without a body?

**14.17.5**  The brāhmaṇa said: Thus prompted, the perfected one answered those questions in due order, O Vārṣṇeya; hear that word of mine as it was.

**14.17.6**  The perfected one said: Those deeds that promote life and renown, which one performs here at the taking of a particular body — when these are wholly exhausted,

**14.17.7**  his self beset by the wasting of life, he engages in things contrary to his good, and his understanding turns awry as destruction draws near.

**14.17.8**  Not knowing his own constitution, his strength, and the season, the man without self-mastery eats unseasonably things harmful to them.

**14.17.9**  When he indulges in all manner of exceedingly harmful things, eats far too much, or at times eats not at all,

**14.17.10**  and partakes of spoiled food, ill-balanced food, and food mutually incompatible, or heavy food along with too much, or again before what was eaten is digested,

**14.17.11**  or indulges in excessive exercise and in sexual intercourse, or out of greed for action constantly suppresses the natural urges as they arise,

**14.17.12**  or resorts to food too rich in flavors and to sleeping by day — while it is yet undigested, before the time has come — he himself stirs up the bodily humors,

**14.17.13**  and from the inflaming of his own humors he contracts a disease that ends in death; or else, beset, he resolves upon hanging and the like.

**14.17.14**  By such causes the life of that creature departs from the body — as I tell it, mark it well, just as it is.

**14.17.15**  The bodily heat, inflamed and driven by a fierce wind, courses through the body and blocks all the vital airs.

**14.17.16**  That heat, grown exceedingly strong and inflamed within the body, splits open the seats of life — know these to be the vital points.

**14.17.17**  Then the life, full of anguish, at once flows out and departs; and the creature, its self beset by agonies as the vital points are riven, quits the body — know this, O best of the twice-born.

**14.17.18**  Forever agitated by birth and death, all creatures are seen abandoning their bodies, O bull among the twice-born.

**14.17.19**  And again, in the passage into the womb, when the vital points are pierced through, a man undergoes just such an agony once more.

**14.17.20**  His joints loosened, that man then takes on moisture from the waters; thus, inflamed by cold within the body and driven by a fierce wind, he enters into union with the five elements.

**14.17.21**  That breath which, amid the five elements, is established in the inward and outward airs — that upward-moving wind departs, releasing the embodied one with difficulty.

**14.17.22**  It quits the body indeed, and breathless does the body appear: without warmth, without breath, without luster, its consciousness gone.

**14.17.23**  Forsaken by Brahman, the man is called dead; by those channels through which the embodied one perceives the objects of the senses, by those very channels he no longer perceives the breath that arises from food.

**14.17.24**  That eternal life-principle which dwells in this very body — whatever among these humors is fittingly joined in confluence, here and there, know that to be a vital point, for so it is seen in the scriptures.

**14.17.25**  When those vital points are riven, it surges up, and entering the heart of the creature it swiftly blocks the inner sense; then the conscious creature can perceive nothing at all.

**14.17.26**  His knowledge veiled by darkness, the vital points now closed over, that life-principle, left without a seat, is shaken loose by the wind.

**14.17.27**  Then, drawing that great breath, exhaling fiercely and terribly, as it goes forth it swiftly sets that senseless body trembling.

**14.17.28**  That life-principle, fallen from the body, wrapped about by its own deeds, marked by its own meritorious good or by its evil, comes to a new birth.

**14.17.29**  Brāhmaṇas endowed with knowledge, whose certainties rest duly upon the sacred lore, recognize by signs the one who has done meritorious deeds and the other as well.

**14.17.30**  As those who have eyes behold a firefly flitting and merging this way and that in the darkness, so do those whose eye is knowledge behold it.

**14.17.31**  Perfected ones of such a kind behold with the divine eye the life-principle passing away, being born, and entering into a womb.

**14.17.32**  Its abodes are seen here to be of three kinds, according to the scriptures: this earth is the field of action, where creatures abide.

**14.17.33**  Having done good and evil, all embodied beings then obtain — even here — high and low enjoyments, which they win by their own deeds.

**14.17.34**  But here itself the man of evil deeds, gone to hell by his deeds, that sinful man is grievously cooked headlong in hell; therefore liberation is hard indeed to win, and the self must be guarded with the utmost care.

**14.17.35**  But the stations on high to which creatures go and where they abide — as they are recounted, learn them here from me in their truth; hearing that, you would attain a settled understanding through certainty about action.

**14.17.36**  All the forms of the stars, and this orb of the moon, and that orb of the sun which shines in the world by its own radiance — know these to be the abodes of men of meritorious deeds.

**14.17.37**  And at the exhaustion of their deeds all of them fall away, again and again; and there too, in heaven, there is a distinction of low, high, and middling.

**14.17.38**  Nor even there is there contentment, on beholding a yet more dazzling splendor. Thus all these destinies have been set forth in their separateness.

**14.17.39**  But the coming-to-be of the embryo I shall now declare hereafter; as I recount it duly, listen attentively, O twice-born one.

**14.18.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: For deeds, whether good or evil, there is no destruction here; meeting field upon field, in this way and that, they ripen.

**14.18.2**  As a fruit-bearing tree, coming to fruition, yields fruit in plenty, so abundant is the merit that is wrought with a pure mind.

**14.18.3**  And sin too would be just the same, wrought with a sinful mind; for here, setting the mind in front, the self engages in action.

**14.18.4**  How, as ordained by his deeds, beset by desire and wrath, a man enters the womb — hear that too in what follows.

**14.18.5**  The seed, mingled with blood and entered into the womb of the woman, obtains a field born of its deeds, whether good or evil.

**14.18.6**  By reason of its subtlety and unmanifest nature, it nowhere clings, having attained the body of Brahman; therefore that Brahman is eternal. It is the seed of all beings, and by it the creatures live.

**14.18.7**  That life-principle, entering all the limbs of the embryo part by part, at once sustains them by the mind, abiding in the seats of the vital airs; then the embryo, endowed with consciousness, sets its limbs astir.
