**14.18.8**  For as molten iron, poured out, takes on the form of the mold, even so understand the entering of the life-principle into the embryo.

**14.18.9**  As fire enters a ball of iron, heating it through, so too understand the coming-to-be of the life-principle within the embryo.

**14.18.10**  And as a blazing lamp lights up a dwelling, even so does consciousness light up the bodies.

**14.18.11**  Whatever deed one performs, whether good or evil, all that was done in a former body must of necessity be enjoyed as its fruit.

**14.18.12**  Then that karma is worn away, and yet again another is heaped up, so long as one does not come to understand the dharma that rests upon the discipline of liberation.

**14.18.13**  On this I shall now declare the dharma by which one becomes happy, O most excellent, even while revolving through births, one after another.

**14.18.14**  Giving, observance of vows, chastity, the keeping of vows as enjoined, self-restraint, tranquillity, and compassion toward living beings;

**14.18.15**  control of the senses and absence of cruelty, refraining from seizing what belongs to another, the doing of no harm to living beings—where this exists upon the earth;

**14.18.16**  service to mother and father, honoring of the gods and guests, reverence for the teacher, tenderness, purity, and constant restraint of the senses;

**14.18.17**  and the setting in motion of all that is auspicious—this is called the conduct of the good. From it springs forth the dharma that protects creatures everlastingly.

**14.18.18**  Thus one should ever look upon the good, for in them is this firm foundation. Conduct reveals the dharma, that wherein the good are established.

**14.18.19**  In them is this dharma laid up as a deposit, and it is the eternal dharma. Whoever attains to it shall not come to an evil destiny.

**14.18.20**  By this the world is governed, even when bewildered upon the paths of dharma. But he who is a yogin and one who is freed surpasses all of these.

**14.18.21**  For the man who conducts himself according to dharma, in whatever measure, the crossing over of the round of rebirth comes to pass, though only after a great length of time.

**14.18.22**  Thus every creature partakes of the karma done before; all that is the cause whereby, cast down, it has come hither into this world.

**14.18.23**  And by whom was the taking on of a body first ordained for it?—such is the doubt that arises in the world; that too I shall declare hereafter.

**14.18.24**  Having made a body for himself, Brahmā, the grandsire of all beings, created the three worlds entire, with all that moves and stands still.

**14.18.25**  Then he created Pradhāna, that consciousness of embodied beings, by which all this is pervaded, and which in the world they know as supreme.

**14.18.26**  Here that is called the perishable, but the supreme is the immortal and imperishable. Of the three there is a coupling, and yet each one stands apart, severally.

**14.18.27**  Prajāpati, himself first created, created all living beings and the things that stand fixed—such is the ancient sacred lore.

**14.18.28**  For him the grandsire fixed the measure of time, and among beings their revolving and their returning again and again.

**14.18.29**  As here some wise man, who has beheld the self in a former birth—all that I am about to declare comes to pass for him exactly as I say.

**14.18.30**  He who ever rightly perceives pleasure and pain as impermanent, and the body as a heap of impurity, doomed to destruction and bound up with karma—

**14.18.31**  and who, remembering that whatever pleasure there is, all of it is pain, shall cross over the dreadful ocean of saṃsāra, hard indeed to cross.

**14.18.32**  Beset by birth, death, and disease, the knower of Pradhāna beholds in all sentient beings their consciousness as the same.

**14.18.33**  Then he turns away in disgust from the whole of it, seeking the supreme state. Of that I shall give you the teaching truly, O most excellent.

**14.18.34**  Now the supreme knowledge of that eternal, undecaying state, as it is declared by me, O brāhmaṇa—mark it well, in its entirety.

**14.19.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: He who is absorbed in the one goal, silent, thinking on nothing, abandoning each former object one by one, becomes free from all undertaking.

**14.19.2**  Friend to all, enduring all, equal-tempered, his senses conquered, with fear and wrath gone from him, the man who has slain desire is set free.

**14.19.3**  He who, restrained and pure, conducts himself toward all beings as toward himself, free of pride and of self-conceit, is wholly liberated.

**14.19.4**  He who is the same toward both life and death, toward pleasure and pain alike, toward gain and loss, toward the loved and the hated—he too is set free.

**14.19.5**  He who longs for nothing whatever, who despises nothing at all, free from the pairs of opposites, his self purged of passion, is wholly liberated.

**14.19.6**  He who is without an enemy, without a kinsman, and without offspring anywhere, who has renounced dharma, wealth, and desire, and is free of all craving, is set free.

**14.19.7**  He who is neither virtuous nor sinful, who destroys what was heaped up before, whose self is stilled by the wasting away of the bodily elements, free of the pairs of opposites, is wholly released.

**14.19.8**  Free of action and free of longing, beholding the world as impermanent, unwell, ever without mastery of itself, bewildered by the round of birth—

**14.19.9**  his mind ever set on dispassion, mindful of the fault that is suffering, he accomplishes the release of the self from bondage before long.

**14.19.10**  Beholding the self as without smell, taste, touch, or sound, beyond possession, without form, and not to be known by the senses, he is liberated.

**14.19.11**  He who beholds it as devoid of the qualities of the five elements, formless and untainted, itself without qualities yet the enjoyer of qualities—he is set free.

**14.19.12**  Casting off by the intellect all imaginings, both of body and of mind, he gradually attains extinction, like a fire that is without fuel.

**14.19.13**  Released from all formations, he then attains the eternal Brahman, the supreme—wholly at peace, unmoving, divine, and imperishable.

**14.19.14**  Beyond this I shall now declare the unsurpassed teaching of yoga, knowing which the yogins behold the self perfected within the world.

**14.19.15**  That teaching I perceive aright; mark it from me—the gates through which one, ever roaming, beholds the self within the self.

**14.19.16**  Withdrawing the senses, one should hold the mind upon the self; having first practiced fierce austerity, one should then set about the yoking.

**14.19.17**  The ascetic, having abandoned imaginings, free of deceit and of egotism, the wise brāhmaṇa beholds with the mind the self within the self.

**14.19.18**  If this holy man is able to yoke the self within the self, then, devoted to solitude, he beholds the self within the self.

**14.19.19**  Restrained, ever disciplined, self-possessed, his senses conquered, thus, well yoked, he beholds the self through the self.

**14.19.20**  For just as a man in a dream, having seen, perceives 'that is he,' so the man well yoked beholds the self as if it had taken on a form.

**14.19.21**  And as one might draw forth the reed-stalk from the muñja grass and display it, so the yogin perceives the self drawn forth from within the body.

**14.19.22**  The body they call the muñja, and the self that rests within it the reed: this is the unsurpassed illustration proclaimed by those who know yoga.

**14.19.23**  For when the embodied one rightly beholds the self well yoked, then no one rules over him, not even the lord of the three worlds.

**14.19.24**  He takes on at will body after body, one after another; having turned back old age and death, he neither rejoices nor grieves.

**14.19.25**  The yoked one, having mastery, makes even the gods exercise their godhood; and abandoning the impermanent body, he attains the undecaying Brahman.

**14.19.26**  Even when the worlds are perishing, no fear arises in him; though beings are tormented, he is tormented by nothing whatever.

**14.19.27**  By the dreadful agitations made of grief and sorrow, born of attachment and affection, the disciplined self is not shaken—free of longing, his mind at peace.

**14.19.28**  Weapons do not pierce him, nor does death exist for him; than this there is nothing happier to be found anywhere in the world.

**14.19.29**  When, having rightly yoked the self, he beholds it within the self alone, then he no longer longs even for the station of Indra himself, lord of the hundred sacrifices.

**14.19.30**  But weariness must in no wise be allowed to one who is practicing yoga. Hear now how the man devoted to solitude should join himself to yoga.

**14.19.31**  Calling to mind the quarter he has seen before, in whatever city he dwells, within that city his mind is to be set roaming, not outside it.

**14.19.32**  Abiding within that city, in whatever dwelling he lodges, in that dwelling the mind, together with its outer and inner parts, is to be held fast.

**14.19.33**  Having contemplated the whole dwelling, in whatever body he abides, in that body the mind is to be made to roam, in no wise outside it.

**14.19.34**  Curbing the host of the senses, in a forest soundless and unpeopled, one should with single-minded focus contemplate the whole body within.

**14.19.35**  The teeth, the palate, the tongue, the throat, and likewise the neck; and he should contemplate too the heart, and the heart's binding-cord.

**14.19.36**  Thus addressed by me, that wise disciple, O slayer of Madhu, asked once again about this dharma of liberation, so hard to put into words.

**14.19.37**  How is this food, once eaten and eaten, digested in the belly? How does it pass into chyle, and how is blood produced? And how does it nourish flesh, and fat, and sinews, and bones?

**14.19.38**  How do all these bodily parts of embodied beings grow, and how, as one grows, does his strength increase? And how comes the passing out of the spent vital fluids, and of the several impurities, each apart?

**14.19.39**  From what does this one breathe out, and again breathe in? And, resting upon what place, does this self abide within the self?

**14.19.40**  If the living soul bears the body, setting the frame in motion, of what color and what manner of being is it that lodges the mind within? Truly, O blessed and sinless one, you are worthy to tell me this.

**14.19.41**  Thus thoroughly questioned by that brāhmaṇa, O Mādhava, I made answer, O mighty-armed subduer of foes, according to what I had heard.

**14.19.42**  As a man, having stored away the vessels in his own storeroom, fixes his mind upon that store, so, having cast the mind into his own body through the unwavering gates, one should there seek out the self, shunning all heedlessness.

**14.19.43**  Thus ever striving, his self made glad, before long he reaches that Brahman, beholding which one becomes the knower of Pradhāna.

**14.19.44**  Yet it is not to be grasped by the eye, nor even by all the senses together; by the mind alone, as by a lamp, the Great One is beheld within the self.

**14.19.45**  With hands and feet on every side, with eyes, heads, and faces on every side—the living soul fully beholds that self gone forth out of the body.

**14.19.46**  Casting off that body of his and holding fast to Brahman alone, he gazes upon the self with the mind, as it were laughing in joy.

**14.19.47**  This whole mystery has been declared by me to you, O most excellent of the twice-born. I take my leave and shall depart; go, disciple, as it pleases you.

**14.19.48**  Thus addressed by me then, O Kṛṣṇa, that disciple of great austerity, the brāhmaṇa, his doubts now severed, departed as he wished.

**14.19.49**  Vāsudeva said: Having spoken these words to me then, O Pārtha, that bull among the twice-born, who had rightly taken his stand upon the dharma of liberation, vanished then and there.

**14.19.50**  Have you, O Pārtha, heard this with a single-pointed mind? For even then, standing upon your chariot, you had heard this very thing.

**14.19.51**  This, O Pārtha, is not easily understood by a man of distracted mind—so I hold—by one whose perception is unformed, who is shallow and whose self is unperfected.

**14.19.52**  This great secret of the gods has been declared, O bull of the Bharatas. Surely, O Pārtha, this has not been heard by any other mortal?

**14.19.53**  For no other man than you is worthy to hear this, O sinless one; nor is it now easily understood by one whose inner self is confused.

**14.19.54**  For by those given to ritual works, O son of Kuntī, the world of the gods is filled; and the gods do not wish this turning back from form to be granted to mortals.

**14.19.55**  For that, O Pārtha, is the supreme goal, that which is the eternal Brahman, where one attains immortality and, having cast off sorrow, is happy forever.

**14.19.56**  For taking their stand thus upon this dharma, even those who may be of base birth—women, vaiśyas, and likewise śūdras—they too attain the supreme goal.

**14.19.57**  How much more so, O Pārtha, the brāhmaṇas, or the kṣatriyas deeply learned, ever devoted to their own dharma and intent upon the world of Brahman.

**14.19.58**  This has been set forth with its reasons, and the means for accomplishing it: the fruit of perfection, liberation, and the ending of sorrow. What other happiness could be higher than this, O bull of the Bharatas?

**14.19.59**  The mortal who is learned, full of faith, and strenuous in effort, O son of Pāṇḍu, who casts off this worthless web of worldly affairs — by these means he would swiftly attain the supreme goal.

**14.19.60**  This much alone is to be said; beyond this there is nothing more. For one who is constantly disciplined, O Pārtha, within six months yoga comes to fruition.

**14.20.1**  Vāsudeva said: On this, too, they recount this ancient tale, O Pārtha — a dialogue between a husband and wife, called by name the discourse of Abhaya.

**14.20.2**  A brāhmaṇa's wife, seeing a certain brāhmaṇa, one who had reached the far shore of knowledge and discernment, seated in solitude, addressed her husband thus.

**14.20.3**  To what world shall I go, having taken refuge in you as my husband — you who sit having laid aside all action, niggardly and unperceiving?

**14.20.4**  We have heard that wives attain the worlds won by their husbands. Having gained you as my husband, to what destination shall I go?

**14.20.5**  Thus addressed, that serene-souled one, as if smiling, said to her: O fortunate one, O blameless lady, I take no offense at this speech of yours.

**14.20.6**  Whatever can be grasped, seen, and heard — this is what is reckoned as action; this alone the doers of works hold to, declaring 'this is action, this is action.'

**14.20.7**  By action, those bereft of knowledge only bind themselves to delusion. Yet freedom from action is not found in this world in any embodied form.

**14.20.8**  By deed, by mind, by speech, whether good or evil — action operates among beings of every form, from birth onward through their distinctions.

**14.20.9**  Amid actions whose visible objects are destroyed, as if slain by demons, by that very Self I have seen the abode that rests within the Self.

**14.20.10**  There is that Brahman, free of all dualities; there Soma together with Agni perpetually performs his work, the wise one sustaining all beings.

**14.20.11**  There the disciplined ones, Brahmā and the rest, worship that Imperishable — the wise of firm vows, the serene-souled who have conquered their senses.

**14.20.12**  It cannot be smelled by the nose, nor tasted by the tongue, nor touched by contact; by the mind alone is it reached.

**14.20.13**  It cannot be borne by the eye, that which is beyond all hearing — odorless, without taste or touch, formless, soundless, imperishable.

**14.20.14**  From it the whole fabric proceeds, and in it comes to rest: prāṇa, apāna, samāna, vyāna, and udāna as well.

**14.20.15**  From it alone they issue forth, and into it alone they enter again. Between samāna and vyāna, prāṇa and apāna move to and fro.

**14.20.16**  When that one sleeps, samāna and vyāna dissolve away; between apāna and prāṇa, udāna pervades and abides. Therefore prāṇa and apāna do not abandon the sleeping man.

**14.20.17**  That by which the vital breaths are drawn together they call udāna. Therefore the expounders of Brahman undertake the austerity that arises from it.

**14.20.18**  Among all these that course through the body, feeding upon one another, the Vaiśvānara fire is set within, distributed sevenfold.

**14.20.19**  Nose, tongue, eye, skin, and hearing as the fifth, with mind and intellect — these seven are the tongues of the Vaiśvānara flame.

**14.20.20**  The smellable, the drinkable, the visible, the touchable, the hearable, the thinkable, and the knowable — these are my seven kinds of fuel.

**14.20.21**  The smeller, the eater, the seer, the toucher, the hearer as the fifth, the thinker, and the knower — these seven are the supreme officiating priests.

**14.20.22**  Into the smellable, the drinkable, the visible, the touchable, and the hearable, the seven priests, casting the oblations rightly sevenfold, seven into seven fires, the wise generate them in their own wombs of origin.

**14.20.23**  Earth, wind, ether, water, and light as the fifth, with mind and intellect — these seven are designated as the wombs of origin.

**14.20.24**  Become oblations, all the qualities enter the mouth born of fire; and having dwelt there within for a season, they are born again in their own wombs; and there too they are dissolved, in the dissolution that produces beings anew.

**14.20.25**  From that arises smell, from that arises taste, from that arises form, and from that touch comes into being.

**14.20.26**  From that arises sound, and therein arises doubt; from that arises certainty. This birth they know to be sevenfold.

**14.20.27**  In this very manner it was grasped by the ancients; filled by full oblations, they are filled, and they fill all with their splendor.

**14.21.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: On this, too, they recount this ancient tale. Learn now the manner of the rite of the ten priests, such as it is here.

**14.21.2**  Here all is to be understood: the mind beholds knowledge; the seed is in the body of the body-bearer, but the knower is the body-bearer itself.

**14.21.3**  The body-bearer is the gārhapatya fire; from it another is carried forth, and that is the āhavanīya; into it the oblation is cast.

**14.21.4**  Then the lord of speech was born; the samāna oversees all. Form becomes manifest, and after it the mind runs.

**14.21.5**  The brāhmaṇa's wife said: Why did speech come to be first, and why did the mind come to be after — when the utterance, conceived by the mind, only then comes forth?

**14.21.6**  By what union of understanding does thought rest established in the mind? Drawn upward, why did it not reach its goal, and who is it that holds it back?

**14.21.7**  The brāhmaṇa said: The apāna, becoming its lord, sends that thought into the downward breath; that thought they call the mind's own, and therefore the mind beholds it.

**14.21.8**  Since you question me concerning speech and mind, I shall therefore relate to you the very contest between those two.

**14.21.9**  Both speech and mind, going to the Soul of beings, asked him: Declare which of us two is the greater; cut away our doubt, O lord.

**14.21.10**  'The mind,' the lord then declared to Sarasvatī; whereupon Speech said to him: 'But it is I who am the wish-granting cow for you.'

**14.21.11**  Know that there are two kinds of mind, the unmoving and the moving: the unmoving abides with me, the moving lies within your domain.

**14.21.12**  Whatever passes into your domain — a sacred formula, a syllable, or a tone — that mind is called the moving one; therefore you are the weightier.

**14.21.13**  And because, O lovely one, you came of your own accord and spoke 'I am she,' therefore, O Sarasvatī, upon reaching the out-breath you shall not speak.

**14.21.14**  The goddess Speech ever abides between prāṇa and apāna; when impelled, O greatly fortunate one, but bereft of prāṇa and sinking into apāna, she ran to Prajāpati, crying, 'Be gracious, O lord!'

**14.21.15**  Then prāṇa appeared, nourishing Speech once more. Therefore, upon reaching the out-breath, Speech never speaks at all.

**14.21.16**  Speech is of two kinds — the sounded and the soundless — and ever proceeds. And of these two, the sounded and the soundless, the soundless is the weightier.

**14.21.17**  Like a cow she streams forth her sap, this most excellent one; ever she flows, eternal, the proclaimer of Brahman.

**14.21.18**  By a power both divine and not divine, O bright-smiling one, the cow that is Speech flows on; behold the difference between these two subtle, streaming ones.

**14.21.19**  When utterances had not yet arisen, impelled by the wish to bring them forth, which first, then, did the goddess Sarasvatī speak?

**14.21.20**  She who comes to be in the body through prāṇa, and from prāṇa passes into apāna, and, becoming udāna, releases the body, and through vyāna pervades the whole heaven —

**14.21.21**  and then comes to rest here in samāna — thus indeed she spoke first of all. Therefore the mind, by its unmoving nature, is distinguished; and so too the goddess Speech, by her moving nature, is distinguished.

**14.22.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: On this, too, they recount this ancient tale, O fortunate one — the manner of the rite of the seven priests, such as it is here.

**14.22.2**  Nose, eye, tongue, skin, and hearing as the fifth, with mind and intellect — these seven priests, each resting apart in its own place.

**14.22.3**  Dwelling in a subtle space, they do not perceive one another. Know these seven priests, O lovely one, each by its own nature.

**14.22.4**  The brāhmaṇa's wife said: Dwelling in a subtle space, how is it they do not perceive one another? What is the nature of each, O lord? Declare this to me, O mighty one.

**14.22.5**  The brāhmaṇa said: Ignorance of the qualities is non-cognition; knowledge of the qualities is true discernment. These never know one another's qualities at all.

**14.22.6**  Tongue, eye, hearing, skin, mind, and intellect do not apprehend odors; the nose alone apprehends them.

**14.22.7**  Nose, eye, hearing, skin, mind, and intellect do not apprehend tastes; the tongue alone apprehends them.

**14.22.8**  Nose, tongue, hearing, skin, mind, and intellect do not apprehend forms; the eye alone apprehends them.

**14.22.9**  Nose, tongue, eye, hearing, intellect, and mind do not apprehend touches; the skin alone apprehends them.

**14.22.10**  Nose, tongue, eye, skin, mind, and intellect do not apprehend sounds; hearing alone apprehends them.

**14.22.11**  Nose, tongue, eye, skin, hearing, and intellect do not apprehend doubts; the mind alone apprehends them.

**14.22.12**  Nose, tongue, eye, skin, hearing, and mind do not apprehend certainty; the intellect alone apprehends it.

**14.22.13**  On this, too, they recount this ancient tale, O beautiful one — a dialogue between the senses and the mind.

**14.22.14**  The mind said: Without me the nose does not smell, the tongue does not perceive taste, the eye does not grasp form, the skin does not register touch.

**14.22.15**  Hearing, bereft of me, in no way perceives sound. I am the foremost of all beings, the eternal one.

**14.22.16**  Like empty houses, like fires whose flames have died, the senses, bereft of me, never shine at all.

**14.22.17**  Like logs of wood, whether wet or dry, even with the senses striving, all creatures fail to attain the objects of sense without me.

**14.22.18**  The senses said: This would be true, just as you think it, only if, without us, you could enjoy those enjoyments that are our objects.

**14.22.19**  If, when we are dissolved away, your sustenance and the upholding of life endure — if you can enjoy the enjoyments and savor the tastes just as you suppose —

**14.22.20**  or else, if when we are dissolved while the objects remain present, you can enjoy the enjoyments truly by mere act of will —

**14.22.21**  and if you believe you have constant mastery over our domains, then seize form with the nose, seize taste with the eye,

**14.22.22**  seize odor with the ear, seize certainty with the tongue, seize sound with the skin, and seize touch with the intellect.

**14.22.23**  For the strong are without constraint; constraints belong to the weaker. Seize enjoyments that are wholly your own; you ought not to eat what is left over by others.

**14.22.24**  Just as a pupil runs to his teacher for the meaning of scripture, and then, having received the teaching, abides by the meaning he has heard,

**14.22.25**  so it is the objects shown to you by us that you lay claim to — things to come and things past alike, in dream and in waking.

**14.22.26**  And even in creatures of little understanding, fallen into despondency of mind, when the task pertaining to our objects is done, the upholding of life is seen to continue.

**14.22.27**  For though you frame many resolves and dwell upon dreams, when oppressed by hunger you run after the objects of sense alone.

**14.22.28**  As if entering a house without a door, the enjoyment that is mere resolve, finding no objects, ever comes to rest when the breath fails — just as a blazing fire is quenched when its fuel is spent.

**14.22.29**  Granted, we are each attached to our own qualities, and granted, we do not perceive one another's qualities. Yet without us there is no perception for you, and without you no joy could attend upon us either.

**14.23.1**  The Brāhmaṇa said: On this point too they recite an ancient tale, O fortunate one, concerning what the disposition of the five oblation-priests is here.

**14.23.2**  The prāṇa and the apāna, the udāna, the samāna, and the vyāna—these the wise know as the five oblation-priests and as the supreme state of being.

**14.23.3**  The Brāhmaṇī said: Your earlier opinion held that by nature there are seven oblation-priests. Now tell me how the five oblation-priests are the supreme state of being.

**14.23.4**  The Brāhmaṇa said: The vital air gathered up by the prāṇa is born as the apāna; the air gathered up in the apāna gives rise then to the vyāna.

**14.23.5**  The air gathered up by the vyāna gives rise then to the udāna; the air gathered up in the udāna brings the samāna fully into play.

**14.23.6**  Of old they went and asked Prajāpati, the firstborn: 'Tell us which of us is the eldest; he shall be our foremost.'

**14.23.7**  Brahmā said: He upon whose dissolution all the vital breaths in the bodies of breathing creatures go to dissolution, and upon whose moving forth they move once more—he indeed is the foremost. Go now wherever you please.

**14.23.8**  The prāṇa said: When I dissolve, all the vital breaths in the bodies of breathing creatures go to dissolution, and when I move forth they move once more. I am the foremost—behold me as I dissolve.

**14.23.9**  The Brāhmaṇa said: The prāṇa then dissolved and again moved forth; and the samāna and the udāna then spoke these words, O fair one.

**14.23.10**  'You do not stand here pervading all this as we do; you are not our foremost, O prāṇa, for the apāna is in your power.' Again the prāṇa moved forth, and the apāna addressed it.

**14.23.11**  'When I dissolve, all the vital breaths in the bodies of breathing creatures go to dissolution, and when I move forth they move once more. I am the foremost—behold me as I dissolve.'

**14.23.12**  As the apāna was speaking, the vyāna and the udāna then said to it: 'O apāna, you are not the foremost, for the prāṇa is subject to you.'

**14.23.13**  The apāna then moved forth, and the vyāna in turn declared: 'I am the foremost of all—hear the reason why.'

**14.23.14**  'When I dissolve, all the vital breaths in the bodies of breathing creatures go to dissolution, and when I move forth they move once more. I am the foremost—behold me as I dissolve.'

**14.23.15**  The vyāna then dissolved and again moved forth; and the prāṇa, the apāna, the udāna, and the samāna said to it: 'You are not our foremost, O vyāna, for the samāna is in your power.'

**14.23.16**  Again the vyāna moved forth, and the samāna in turn declared: 'I am the foremost of all—hear the reason why.'

**14.23.17**  'When I dissolve, all the vital breaths in the bodies of breathing creatures go to dissolution, and when I move forth they move once more. I am the foremost—behold me as I dissolve.'

**14.23.18**  Then the samāna dissolved and again moved forth; and the prāṇa, the apāna, the udāna, and the vyāna said to it: 'O samāna, you are not the foremost, for the vyāna alone is in your power.'

**14.23.19**  The samāna then moved forth, and the udāna said to it: 'I am the foremost of all—hear the reason why.'

**14.23.20**  'When I dissolve, all the vital breaths in the bodies of breathing creatures go to dissolution, and when I move forth they move once more. I am the foremost—behold me as I dissolve.'

**14.23.21**  Then the udāna dissolved and again moved forth; and the prāṇa, the apāna, the samāna, and the vyāna said to it: 'O udāna, you are not the foremost, for the vyāna alone is in your power.'

**14.23.22**  Then Brahmā the Prajāpati said to them as they stood gathered: 'You are all foremost, or none of you is foremost; all of you sustain one another's functions; each is foremost in its own domain, and all are guardians of one another.'

**14.23.23**  'There is one, both steady and unsteady; by distinction there are five winds. There is but one Self, my own, though it is built up in manifold ways.'

**14.23.24**  'Being friends to one another, nourishing one another—go in peace, blessings upon you, and uphold one another.'

**14.24.1**  The Brāhmaṇa said: On this point too they recite an ancient tale, the dialogue of Nārada and the seer Devamata.

**14.24.2**  Devamata said: When a creature is coming into being, which arises first—the prāṇa, the apāna, the samāna, the vyāna, or indeed the udāna?

**14.24.3**  Nārada said: That by which this creature is brought forth—another goes before it. And the pairing of the breaths is to be understood, both that which moves crosswise and that which moves upward.

**14.24.4**  Devamata said: By what is this creature brought forth, and which other goes before it? Tell me with certainty about the pairing of the breaths, the crosswise and the upward.

**14.24.5**  Nārada said: From intention joy is born, and from sound too it is born; it arises from taste as well, and from form too it is born.

**14.24.6**  It arises from touch as well, and from smell too it is born. This is the form of the udāna: joy, which springs from union.

**14.24.7**  From desire the seed arises, from desire the essence arises; the seed and the blood, held in common, are produced by the samāna and the vyāna.

**14.24.8**  From the seed mingled with the blood, the prāṇa first comes into play; and when the seed has been transformed by the prāṇa, the apāna then comes into play.

**14.24.9**  The prāṇa and the apāna are this pair, going downward and upward; the vyāna and the samāna, both together, are called the crosswise pair.

**14.24.10**  'Agni is verily all the deities'—such is the teaching of the Veda; and among brāhmaṇas there arises knowledge attended by discernment.

**14.24.11**  Of that fire the smoke is the form of tamas, and its ash is rajas; from that fire of fine seed sattva arises, there where the oblation is cast.

**14.24.12**  The two sprinklings of ghee are the samāna and the vyāna—so those versed in sacrifice know; the prāṇa and the apāna are the two portions of clarified butter, and between them is the devourer of oblations. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.24.13**  As for that which is free of all pairs of opposites, hear it now as I declare it.

**14.24.14**  Day and night are this pair, and between them is the devourer of oblations. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.24.15**  The two solstitial courses of the sun are a pair, and between them is the devourer of oblations. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.24.16**  Truth and falsehood, the two, are a pair, and between them is the devourer of oblations. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.24.17**  The good and the evil, the two, are a pair, and between them is the devourer of oblations. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.24.18**  The existent and the non-existent are that pair, and between them is the devourer of oblations. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.24.19**  First the samāna and the vyāna; by these the work is set in motion. The third part, again, is brought about once more by the samāna.

**14.24.20**  For the sake of peace there is the Vāmadeva hymn, and peace is the eternal Brahman. This the brāhmaṇas know as the supreme form of the udāna.

**14.25.1**  The Brāhmaṇa said: On this point too they recite an ancient tale, concerning the nature of the disposition of the four-priest oblation here.

**14.25.2**  The disposition of all of it shall be taught according to rule. Listen to me as I tell it, O blessed one—this is the highest mystery.

**14.25.3**  The instrument, the action, the agent, and release—O lovely one, these four are the oblation-priests by whom this world is enveloped.

**14.25.4**  And hear too, in full and without remainder, the means of these oblation-priests: smell, the tongue, the eye, the skin, and hearing as the fifth, the mind, and the intellect—these seven are to be known as the causes of the qualities.

**14.25.5**  Smell, taste, form, sound, and touch as the fifth, the object of thought and the object of cognition—these seven are the causes of action.

**14.25.6**  The smeller, the taster, the seer, the toucher, and the hearer as the fifth, the thinker and the knower—these seven are to be known as the causes of the agent.

**14.25.7**  These, possessed of qualities, consume their own object, good and evil; and the thought 'I am here without qualities'—these seven are the causes of release.

**14.25.8**  For the wise who understand, each in its own station according to rule, those qualities, having become deities, continually consume the oblation.

**14.25.9**  The ignorant man, eating his foods, becomes attached to them through the sense of 'mine'; cooking ever for his own sake, he is destroyed through the sense of 'mine'.

**14.25.10**  The eating of what should not be eaten and the drinking of liquor destroy him; he destroys that food, and that food destroys him; having destroyed, the wise man is himself destroyed.

**14.25.11**  The wise lord, the eater, brings forth this food again; and he is born from food—therein lies a subtle distinction.

**14.25.12**  That which is reached by the mind, and that which is uttered by speech, that which is heard by the ear, and that which is seen by the eye,

**14.25.13**  and that which is touched by touch, and that which is smelled by smell—restraining all these wholly, with the mind as the sixth, as oblations.

**14.25.14**  For me the purifying fire, the bearer of oblations, blazes endowed with qualities; the sacrifice of yoga is set in motion for me, born of the knowledge of Brahman in the mind, with the prāṇa for its hymn of praise, the apāna for its recited verse, and the renunciation of all things for its rich priestly fee.

**14.25.15**  The one who sanctions the act is my Brahman-priest, the agent is my Adhvaryu, the deed itself the hymn of praise, the doer the director, and that scripture; and final release is the priestly fee of this sacrifice.

**14.25.16**  And here too the people learned in Nārāyaṇa recite verses about how of old they bound the sacrificial victims for the god Nārāyaṇa.

**14.25.17**  There they sing the Sāman chants, and these they declare to be the demonstration. Know from me, O timid one, the god Nārāyaṇa, the Self of all.

**14.26.1**  The Brāhmaṇa said: There is one Ruler; there is no second ruler. As I am appointed, so do I act. This Person, dwelling in the heart, the Ruler, rules; impelled by him alone I move, like water running down a slope.

**14.26.2**  There is one Teacher; there is no second besides him. Him who dwells in the heart I now proclaim. By that Teacher, ever instructed, the Dānavas were one and all overcome.

**14.26.3**  There is one Kinsman; there is no second besides him. Him who dwells in the heart I now proclaim. By him instructed, kinsmen rich in kindred, the Seven Sages, shine sevenfold in the sky.

**14.26.4**  There is one Hearer; there is no second besides him. Him who dwells in the heart I now proclaim. Having dwelt his term of pupilage under that Teacher, Śakra attained immortality over all the worlds.

**14.26.5**  There is one Hater; there is no second besides him. Him who dwells in the heart I now proclaim. By that Teacher, ever instructed, the serpents were one and all made hateful to the world.

**14.26.6**  On this point too they recite an ancient tale, the conference of the serpents and the divine seers in the presence of Prajāpati.

**14.26.7**  The divine seers, the Nāgas, and the Asuras, sitting in attendance, asked Prajāpati: 'Let what is good for us be declared.'

**14.26.8**  To them as they inquired after the good, the Blessed One spoke the single syllable Om, which is Brahman; and having heard it they ran off in all directions.

**14.26.9**  As they ran off, each taking the instruction for himself, in the serpents the disposition toward biting came forth first of all.

**14.26.10**  In the Asuras the disposition toward arrogance, born of their nature, came forth; the gods resolved upon giving, and the great seers upon self-restraint.

**14.26.11**  Approaching one Ruler, refined by one and the same sound, the serpents, divine seers, and Dānavas all resolved upon diverse things.

**14.26.12**  This one hears what is being told and grasps it just as it is; and when he asks again and again, another, the inner Teacher, gives his assent.

**14.26.13**  By his assent the action thereafter comes into play. He is Teacher, Knower, Foe, and Hater, dwelling within the heart.

**14.26.14**  Moving through the world by evil, this one becomes a doer of evil; moving through the world by good, he becomes a doer of good.

**14.26.15**  He who delights in the pleasures of the senses is, through desire, one who acts at his own will; but he who ever delights in the conquest of the senses is one who keeps to his vow.

**14.26.16**  But he who has left behind vows and rites and has taken refuge solely in Brahman, moving through the world as one who has become Brahman, is a true student of the sacred—a brahmacārin.

**14.26.17**  For him the kindling-sticks are Brahman, the fire is Brahman, the sacrificial grass is Brahman, the waters are Brahman, the teacher is Brahman; he is wholly concentrated in Brahman.

**14.26.18**  Such, and so subtle, is the holy life of Brahman that the wise know; and having known it they attained it, beholding through the Knower of the Field.

**14.27.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: Its gnats and mosquitoes are the cravings of the will; its cold and heat are grief and elation; its blinding gloom is delusion; its creeping serpents are the snakes of greed —

**14.27.2**  its road that must be crossed leads through the objects of sense alone; its hostile barriers are desire and anger. Having passed through that great and trackless waste, I have entered a vast forest.

**14.27.3**  The brāhmaṇa's wife said: Where is that forest, O man of great wisdom? What are its trees, what its streams, what its hills and mountains, and at what distance does that forest lie?

**14.27.4**  The brāhmaṇa said: In the realm of separateness there is nothing else equal to it; in the realm of non-separateness there is nothing more remote than it.

**14.27.5**  There is nothing shorter than it, and nothing greater than it; there is nothing more sorrowful than it, and no joy that equals it.

**14.27.6**  Having entered it, the twice-born neither grieve nor rejoice; they fear nothing from any quarter, and there are those who do not fear them.

**14.27.7**  In that forest are seven great trees, seven fruits, seven guests, seven hermitages, seven kinds of meditation, and seven consecrations — such is the form of that wilderness.

**14.27.8**  Trees putting forth divine flowers and fruits of five colors stand pervading that forest.

**14.27.9**  Trees putting forth flowers and fruits of fair hue and of two colors stand pervading that forest.

**14.27.10**  Trees putting forth divine flowers and fruits of four colors stand pervading that forest.

**14.27.11**  Trees putting forth auspicious flowers and fruits of three colors stand pervading that forest.

**14.27.12**  Trees putting forth fragrant flowers and fruits of a single color stand pervading that forest.

**14.27.13**  And two great trees, putting forth many flowers and fruits of indistinct color, stand pervading that forest.

**14.27.14**  Here is one fire, the gladdened brāhmaṇa; here are the five senses for its kindling-sticks; from these arise the seven releases that are the consecrations; the qualities are the fruits, and the guests are those who long for the fruits.

**14.27.15**  There the seven great seers accept the hospitality; and when they, being honored, have dissolved away, another forest shines forth.

**14.27.16**  There stands the tree of resolve, fruitless, attended by the shade of peace, the refuge of knowledge, watered by contentment, and lit within by the sun that is the Knower of the field.

**14.27.17**  For the good who attain that, there is no further fear; above, below, and across, its limit cannot be reached.

**14.27.18**  Seven women dwell there ever-present, downward-faced, radiant, and life-bearing; they bestow upon creatures the upward essences of all things, and likewise all impermanence.

**14.27.19**  There abide and from there again arise the seven groups of seven seers, the perfected ones, with Vasiṣṭha at their head.

**14.27.20**  Glory, splendor, fortune, victory, accomplishment, and brilliance — even so do seven lights attend upon the sun.

**14.27.21**  There too, in brief, are hills and mountains, and rivers and streams bearing waters sprung from Brahman.

**14.27.22**  There, in a secret place, is the confluence of the rivers, the sacred fire; from there those who are content in the Self, the self-restrained, go directly to the Grandfather.

**14.27.23**  With desires made lean and hopes turned to good vows, their sins burnt away by austerity, they merge the self in the Self and worship Brahman.

**14.27.24**  Here too the people learned in the forest of knowledge recite a hymn that, intending that wilderness, arose in measure with steadfastness.

**14.27.25**  Such, of this nature, is the divine wilderness that the brāhmaṇas know; and having known it, as shown to them by the Knower of the field, they have lived in accordance with it.

**14.28.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: I do not smell odors, I do not taste flavors, I do not see form, nor do I touch, nor do I hear the manifold sounds, nor do I entertain any willful thought at all.

**14.28.2**  It is one's own nature that desires the wished-for objects, one's own nature that hates all that is hateful; desire and hatred spring up from nature, having lodged the in-breath and out-breath within the bodies of creatures.

**14.28.3**  From these and other states, impermanent within them, I would mark out the elemental self within the body; and dwelling in that, I cannot in any way be assailed by desire and anger, by old age, or by death.

**14.28.4**  For one who craves none of the objects of desire and hates none of the faults, no stains cling to me amid the modes of nature, as a drop of water does not cling to the lotus leaves.

**14.28.5**  For this eternal one, who beholds the many modes of nature, the eternal things remain; the net of enjoyment does not cling to him in his actions, as in the sky the net of the sun's rays does not cling.

**14.28.6**  On this they recite an ancient tale, the dialogue of the adhvaryu priest and the ascetic; mark it well, O illustrious lady.

**14.28.7**  Seeing the sacrificial victim being sprinkled in the rite, the seated ascetic spoke to the adhvaryu, condemning it: 'This is violence.'

**14.28.8**  To him the adhvaryu replied: This goat does not perish; the creature will be joined to a higher good, if this Vedic teaching is true.

**14.28.9**  For that part of it which is earthly will go to the earth, and whatever in it is born of water returns to the waters.

**14.28.10**  Its eye goes to the sun, its hearing to the quarters, its breath to the very sky; for me, who act in keeping with sacred tradition, there is no fault at all.

**14.28.11**  The ascetic said: If you foresee a higher good for the goat in its parting from life, then the sacrifice exists for the goat's sake — what use is it to you?

**14.28.12**  Let its mother, father, brother, and friend give you their consent; take counsel with them, having first brought up the creature — especially since it belongs to others.

**14.28.13**  You ought to ask those who would thus give consent; and having heard their assent, the deliberation can be carried out.

**14.28.14**  As for the life-breaths of this goat, they have been led back to their own sources; only the body remains, motionless — so it is my view.

**14.28.15**  But for those who wish to be free of violence, the body, devoid of consciousness and like mere fuel, is fuel that goes by the name of victim.

**14.28.16**  'Non-violence is the highest of all duties' — such is the teaching of the elders; and we hold that whatever action is free of harm is the action to be done.

**14.28.17**  'Non-violence' must be the vow declared; and if I were to speak further than this, much could be said in many ways to fault your performance.

**14.28.18**  Non-violence toward all beings is what ever pleases us; we accomplish it before our very eyes — we do not serve what is unseen.

**14.28.19**  The adhvaryu said: You enjoy the quality of smell that belongs to earth, you drink the watery flavors, you see the form that belongs to the luminaries, you touch the qualities born of air,

**14.28.20**  you hear the sound born of space, and with the mind you conceive thought; and you regard all these elements as living beings.

**14.28.21**  You are constantly taking life away, and so you live in violence; there is no activity without harm. Or what do you think, O twice-born?

**14.28.22**  The ascetic said: The imperishable and the perishable — this is the twofold being of the Self; therein the imperishable is true being, while one's own nature is called the perishable.

**14.28.23**  Breath, tongue, mind, and goodness — this nature, together with passion: for one freed from these states, free of the pairs of opposites and of all craving,

**14.28.24**  who is equal toward all beings, free of possessiveness, self-conquered, and wholly liberated, no fear exists anywhere.

**14.28.25**  The adhvaryu said: Surely fellowship is to be kept here only with the good, O best of the wise; for, having heard your view, my own understanding grows clear.

**14.28.26**  Reverend one, awakened by your reverend understanding, I declare: in holding this view and in performing the sacrifice, there is no offense in me, O twice-born.

**14.28.27**  The brāhmaṇa said: Thereafter the ascetic, content with this reasoning, abode in silence; and the adhvaryu too, his delusion gone, proceeded with the great sacrifice.

**14.28.28**  Thus do the brāhmaṇas know this most subtle liberation; and having known it, they live by it, as shown by the Knower of the field.

**14.29.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: On this too they recite an ancient tale, the dialogue of Kārtavīrya and the Ocean, O lovely lady.

**14.29.2**  There was a king named Kārtavīrya Arjuna, possessed of a thousand arms, by whom, with his bow, the earth to its ocean-shores was conquered.

**14.29.3**  Once, roaming along the ocean's edge, proud of his strength, he showered the sea with hundreds of arrows — so we have heard.

**14.29.4**  The Ocean bowed to him and, with hands joined in reverence, said: Do not loose your iron shafts, O hero; tell me, what shall I do for you?

**14.29.5**  The creatures that take refuge in me are being slain by your mighty arrows, O tiger among kings; grant them safety, O lord.

**14.29.6**  Arjuna said: If anywhere there exists a bearer of the bow equal to me in battle, point him out to me — one who could stand against me in the fray.

**14.29.7**  The Ocean said: If, O king, the great seer Jamadagni is known to you, his son is able to offer you fitting hospitality.

**14.29.8**  Then the king set out, wrapped in great wrath; and coming to that hermitage, he sought out Rāma himself.

**14.29.9**  Together with his kinsmen he did deeds hostile to Rāma, and he brought distress upon the great-souled Rāma.

**14.29.10**  Then the fiery power of Rāma of boundless splendor blazed forth, consuming the armies of the foe, O lotus-eyed one.

**14.29.11**  Then, taking up his axe, Rāma swiftly cut down that thousand-armed king, as one lops a tree of its branching boughs.

**14.29.12**  Seeing him slain and fallen, all his kinsmen gathered, and taking up their swords and spears, surrounded the descendant of Bhṛgu.

**14.29.13**  Rāma too, taking up his bow and swiftly mounting his chariot, loosed showers of arrows and scattered the host of those kings.

**14.29.14**  Then certain warriors, having slain Jamadagni, fled into the mountain fastnesses, like deer harried by a lion.

**14.29.15**  Out of that fear they no longer performed the duties ordained for them, and their offspring, for want of the sight of brāhmaṇas, fell to the condition of outcastes.

**14.29.16**  These are the Draviḍas, the Kāśas, and the Puṇḍras, together with the Śabaras, who through their lapse from the warrior's duty came to the state of outcastes.

**14.29.17**  Then, when the warrior race had been stripped of its heroes again and again, the warriors begotten anew upon them by the brāhmaṇas were cut down by the son of Jamadagni.

**14.29.18**  At the end of the twenty-first slaughter, a bodiless voice, divine and sweet, heard throughout all the worlds, spoke to Rāma:

**14.29.19**  Rāma, Rāma, desist! What merit, dear son, do you see in robbing these wretched warriors of their lives again and again?

**14.29.20**  Likewise the forefathers, with Ṛcīka at their head, then said to that great-souled one, O blessed lady: Desist!

**14.29.21**  But Rāma, unable to bear his father's slaying, said to those seers: Your honors have no right to restrain me here.

**14.29.22**  The forefathers said: You ought not to slay these warriors, O best of the victorious; for it is not fitting that you, a true brāhmaṇa, should kill kings.

**14.30.1**  The forefathers said: On this too they recite an ancient tale; having heard it, you must act accordingly, O best of the twice-born.

**14.30.2**  There was a royal seer named Alarka, of mighty austerity, knower of duty, true to his word, great-souled, and firm in great vows.

**14.30.3**  Having conquered this earth to its ocean-shores with his bow, and having done that most arduous deed, he fixed his mind upon the subtle.

**14.30.4**  As he sat at the foot of a tree, this thought arose in him — having renounced his vast kingdom — concerning the subtle, O great-minded one:

**14.30.5**  Alarka said: The power of my mind has grown great; once the mind is conquered, victory is assured. Beset by foes, I shall loose my arrows elsewhere.

**14.30.6**  Since out of its fickleness the embodied mind desires to do all this, I shall loose arrows of keenest point against the mind itself.

**14.30.7**  The mind said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and with your vitals pierced you shall die.

**14.30.8**  Look rather for other arrows by which you might destroy me. Hearing this, he reflected and then spoke these words.

**14.30.9**  Alarka said: Having smelled the manifold scents, it craves after those very scents; therefore I shall loose my sharpened shafts against the sense of smell.

**14.30.10**  The sense of smell said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and with your vitals pierced you shall die.

**14.30.11**  Look rather for other arrows by which you might destroy me. Hearing this, he reflected and then spoke these words.

**14.30.12**  Alarka said: Having enjoyed the sweet tastes, it craves after those very tastes; therefore I shall loose my sharpened shafts against the tongue.

**14.30.13**  The tongue said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and with your vitals pierced you shall die.

**14.30.14**  Look rather for other arrows by which you might destroy me. Hearing this, he reflected and then spoke these words.

**14.30.15**  Alarka said: Having touched the manifold textures, the skin craves after those very contacts; therefore I shall rend the skin with my many heron-feathered shafts.

**14.30.16**  The skin said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and with your vitals pierced you shall die.

**14.30.17**  Look rather for other arrows by which you might destroy me. Hearing this, he reflected and then spoke these words.

**14.30.18**  Alarka said: Having heard the manifold sounds, it craves after those very sounds; therefore I shall loose my sharpened shafts against the sense of hearing.

**14.30.19**  The ear said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and thereby you shall give up your life.

**14.30.20**  Look rather for other arrows by which you might destroy me. Hearing this, he reflected and then spoke these words.

**14.30.21**  Alarka said: Having seen the manifold forms, it craves after those very objects; therefore I shall loose my sharpened shafts against the eye.

**14.30.22**  The eye said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and with your vitals pierced you shall die.

**14.30.23**  Look rather for other arrows by which you might destroy me. Hearing this, he reflected and then spoke these words.

**14.30.24**  Alarka said: This faculty of resolve, manifold in kind, deliberates through the understanding; therefore I shall loose my sharpened shafts against the intellect.

**14.30.25**  The intellect said: These arrows of yours, Alarka, shall in no way pass through me; they will only pierce your own vital parts, and with your vitals pierced you shall die.

**14.30.26**  The fathers said: Then Alarka, taking up a terrible and most arduous penance, could find with all his might no arrow to use against these seven; and so, with his mind fully composed, the lord pondered.

**14.30.27**  Having reflected for a long while, O best of the twice-born, Alarka, foremost of the wise, found no higher good than yoga.

**14.30.28**  Making his mind one-pointed and unwavering, he took his stand in yoga; the hero swiftly slew the senses with a single arrow, and entering into the Self through yoga he attained the highest perfection.

**14.30.29**  And the royal sage, marveling, uttered this verse: Alas, how grievous that we formerly devoted ourselves to kingship! Only afterward did I learn that there is no happiness higher than yoga.

**14.30.30**  Know this too, O Rāma: do not slay the Kṣatriyas. Undertake a terrible penance, and thereby you shall attain the supreme good.

**14.30.31**  The brāhmaṇa said: Thus addressed by his ancestors, the son of Jamadagni, exceedingly blessed, took up a terrible penance and attained a perfection hard to reach.

**14.31.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: In the world there are three foes, held to be nine by their qualities. Elation, stubbornness, and conceit—these three are the sāttvika qualities.

**14.31.2**  Grief, anger, and excessive impetuosity—these are reckoned the rājasa qualities. Sleep, drowsiness, and delusion—these three are the tāmasa qualities.

**14.31.3**  Having cut these down with volleys of arrows, the steadfast and tireless man, his self serene and his senses mastered, is able to conquer his foes.

**14.31.4**  On this matter those who know the ancient lore recite verses, those which were sung by King Ambarīṣa as he governed his kingdom.

**14.31.5**  When the vices had risen up and the good were being slain, Ambarīṣa seized the kingdom by main force—so runs the tradition.

**14.31.6**  Having subdued the great vices and duly honored the good, he attained a high perfection and uttered this verse.

**14.31.7**  For the most part my vices are conquered and all my enemies slain; but one vice remains, which deserves to be killed, and which I have not yet killed.

**14.31.8**  Possessed by it, this creature never attains freedom from craving; tormented by thirst, running toward the low places, he does not come to his senses.

**14.31.9**  That greed which, driving a man, makes him pursue even what ought not to be done—cut it down, even as it cuts you down, with sharp swords.

**14.31.10**  For from greed is born craving, and from that anxiety follows; the man who lusts to acquire gathers in abundance the rājasa qualities.

**14.31.11**  Bound in the bondage of the body by those qualities, he is born again and again and toils at action; when his life ends his body is broken and scattered, and in his next birth he goes once more to death.

**14.31.12**  Therefore, having rightly perceived this greed and subdued it with firmness, let a man seek sovereignty over himself. Let him know that this is the true kingdom and there is no other; for here within, that one king has been vanquished by me.

**14.31.13**  Thus was this verse sung by the illustrious King Ambarīṣa, who, setting supreme sovereignty before him, cut down greed alone.

**14.32.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: On this too, O fair lady, they recite this ancient story, the dialogue between a brāhmaṇa and Janaka.

**14.32.2**  To a brāhmaṇa who had settled there on the strength of some scriptural authority, King Janaka said, by way of discipline: You must not dwell within my domain.

**14.32.3**  Thus addressed, the brāhmaṇa answered that best of kings: Tell me, O king, how far the domain extends that stands within your power.

**14.32.4**  I wish to dwell, O mighty one, within the domain of another king; I wish to obey your word in accord with the scriptures, O lord of the earth.

**14.32.5**  Thus addressed by the illustrious brāhmaṇa, the king then sighed hot sighs again and again, and made him no answer.

**14.32.6**  As that king of boundless might sat there absorbed in thought, a faintness suddenly came over him, as an eclipse overtakes the radiant sun.

**14.32.7**  Then the king, recovering himself when the faintness had passed, after a moment spoke these words to the brāhmaṇa.

**14.32.8**  Though the kingdom of my fathers and forefathers is mine, and the people obedient, yet, searching this whole earth, I can find no domain that is truly my own.

**14.32.9**  When I searched the earth I did not find it; when I searched within Mithilā I did not find it; when I sought my own subjects therein, I did not find it.

**14.32.10**  When I could not find it among them, then this faintness came upon me; but when the faintness ended, my understanding returned to me.

**14.32.11**  By that understanding I judge that no domain is mine, or else the whole world is my domain; even this self is not mine, or else the whole earth is mine. Dwell here as long as you have the will, and enjoy as much as you desire.

**14.32.12**  Though the kingdom of your fathers and forefathers is yours, and the people obedient, tell me—resting on what conviction have you renounced the sense of 'mine'?

**14.32.13**  Or having settled on what conviction is it that all becomes your domain—the conviction by which you do not regard any as your domain, and yet all becomes your domain?

**14.32.14**  Janaka said: In all undertakings here the works are known to be finite; therefore I could find nothing of which I might say, 'This is mine.'

**14.32.15**  'Whose is this?' 'Whose is the property?'—so run the words of the Veda; reasoning thus with my intellect, I could find nothing of which I might say, 'This is mine.'

**14.32.16**  Having settled on this conviction, I have renounced the sense of 'mine'. Now hear the conviction by knowing which all things everywhere become my domain.

**14.32.17**  I do not desire even the scents that reach the nose for my own sake; therefore earth is conquered by me and abides forever in my power.

**14.32.18**  I do not desire even the tastes present in my mouth for my own sake; therefore the waters are conquered by me and abide forever in my power.

**14.32.19**  I do not desire form and light through the eye for my own sake; therefore light is conquered by me and abides forever in my power.

**14.32.20**  I do not desire for my own sake the contacts that reach the skin; therefore the wind is conquered by me and abides forever in my power.

**14.32.21**  I do not desire even the sounds that reach the ear for my own sake; therefore sounds are conquered by me and abide forever in my power.

**14.32.22**  I do not desire the mind for my own sake, ever fixed within the inner mind; therefore the mind is conquered by me and abides forever in my power.

**14.32.23**  For the gods, for the fathers, for created beings, together with guests—it is for this purpose that all these undertakings exist.

**14.32.24**  Then, smiling, the brāhmaṇa spoke again to Janaka: Know that I have come here today, in the guise of dharma itself, to test you.

**14.32.25**  You alone are the turner of this wheel whose hub is Brahman, whose spokes are the intellect, which never turns back, and which is held firm by the rim of sattva.

**14.33.1**  The brāhmaṇa said: I do not move in the world, O timid one, in the way you suppose me by your own reasoning. I am a brāhmaṇa, I am liberated, I am a forest-dweller; I am also a householder by duty, and a celibate student as well.

**14.33.2**  I am not, O fair one, as you behold me with your eye; by me is pervaded all this, whatever exists upon the earth.

**14.33.3**  Whatever creatures there are in the world, moving and unmoving, know me to be their ender, as fire is the ender of pieces of wood.

**14.33.4**  Sovereignty over all the earth, or even over heaven itself—this intellect knows it for what it is; the intellect alone is my wealth.

**14.33.5**  One is the path of the brāhmaṇas, by which those who know it go—in homes, in forest dwellings, in the houses of teachers, among mendicants; through many tokens, unconfused, the one intellect is worshiped.

**14.33.6**  Those of diverse tokens and stations of life, whose intellect is of the nature of tranquility, arrive at one and the same state, as rivers come to the sea.

**14.33.7**  This path is traversed by the intellect; it is not traversed by the body. Actions have a beginning and an end, and the body is the bondage of action.

**14.33.8**  Therefore, O blessed one, you have no fear concerning the world beyond; devoted to dwelling in my being, you shall come to my very Self.

**14.34.1**  The brāhmaṇa woman said: This cannot be understood by one of little soul, nor by one whose self is unperfected. My understanding finds it at once vast and slight, condensed and yet bewildering.

**14.34.2**  But tell me the means by which this understanding is attained; that I deem the chief cause from which it arises.

**14.34.3**  The brāhmaṇa said: Know the lower fire-stick to be the brāhmaṇa-power, and the teacher to be its upper stick; when austerity and sacred learning churn them together, from this is born the fire of knowledge.

**14.34.4**  The brāhmaṇa woman said: This token of Brahman that is named the knower of the field—by what mark can it be grasped, and where indeed is that mark of it?

**14.34.5**  The brāhmaṇa said: It is without token and without quality, and no cause of it is known. Yet I shall tell the means by which it may be grasped, or may not.

**14.34.6**  Even when rightly taught, it is but dimly perceived, as if through swarming bees; the mind bent on action, through want of true insight, seems to take refuge in the mere tokens of knowledge.

**14.34.7**  'This is to be done, this is not'—such is not taught in the paths of liberation; rather, it lies in those things wherein, as one sees and hears, the understanding of the Self is born.

**14.34.8**  As many portions as can here be conceived, so many let one frame—manifest and unmanifest in form, by hundreds and by thousands.

**14.34.9**  When all things marked by diversity, and all whose causes are perceptible, are seen to be such that beyond them nothing further exists—then realization comes through sustained practice.

**14.34.10**  Vāsudeva said: Then the understanding of that Brāhmaṇī, upon the dissolution of the Knower of the Field, perceived that beyond the Knower of the Field a higher, other Knower of the Field arises.

**14.34.11**  Arjuna said: Where, O Kṛṣṇa, is that Brāhmaṇī, and where that bull among Brāhmaṇas, by whom this perfection was attained? Tell me of them both, O undecaying one.

**14.34.12**  Vāsudeva said: Know my mind to be the Brāhmaṇa, and know my intellect to be the Brāhmaṇī; and he who was called the Knower of the Field—that, O Dhanaṃjaya, is none but myself.

**14.35.1**  Arjuna said: That supreme Brahman which is to be known—this you ought to expound to me; for by your grace my mind takes delight in things subtle.

**14.35.2**  Vāsudeva said: On this too they recount an ancient tale—a dialogue concerning liberation between a disciple and his teacher.

**14.35.3**  A certain wise disciple, O scorcher of foes, questioned a Brāhmaṇa teacher of firm vows, who sat at ease: 'What, indeed, is the highest good?'

**14.35.4**  'I have taken refuge in you, my lord, intent upon the supreme good. I beseech you with bowed head, O Brāhmaṇa: explain to me that of which I would ask.'

**14.35.5**  To the disciple who spoke thus, O Pārtha, the teacher replied: 'Speak forth, twice-born one; I shall declare to you that whereon your doubt lies.'

**14.35.6**  Thus addressed by the teacher, O best of the Kurus, that disciple, devoted to his guru, with joined palms put his question—hear it, O great-minded one.

**14.35.7**  The disciple said: Whence am I, and whence are you? Tell me that highest truth. Whence have beings come to be, the standing and the moving alike?

**14.35.8**  By what do beings live, and of what nature is their life-span? What is truth, what is austerity, O Brāhmaṇa? What virtues are praised by the good? Which are the auspicious paths? What is happiness, and what is wrongdoing?

**14.35.9**  These my questions, O blessed and most excellent one, O sage among Brāhmaṇas, you ought to answer here truly, exactly, and in accordance with reality.

**14.35.10**  Vāsudeva said: To that disciple—well-disposed, questioning fitly, endowed with virtues, tranquil, obedient to his guru, become as his very shadow, self-restrained, a striver, a chaste student—

**14.35.11**  to those questions, O Pārtha, the wise teacher of steadfast vows replied—all of them rightly, O best of the Kuru line, O subduer of foes.

**14.35.12**  The teacher said: This Law was proclaimed by Brahmā, cultivated by the foremost seers, to be attained through knowledge of the Vedas, the contemplation of the truly real.

**14.35.13**  It settles the matters of duty, desire, and gain concerning past, present, future, and the rest; it is fully known to the hosts of the perfected; it belongs to the ancient ordinance and is eternal.

**14.35.14**  I shall declare to you today, O greatly wise one, the supreme station, by understanding which the thoughtful become perfected here.

**14.35.15**  Of old the seers, drawing near and seeking knowledge from one another—Bṛhaspati and Bharadvāja, Gautama and the Bhārgava as well,

**14.35.16**  Vasiṣṭha and Kāśyapa, and Viśvāmitra and Atri too—having traversed all the paths and grown weary through their own works,

**14.35.17**  those twice-born ones, placing the aged seer of the Aṅgiras line before them, beheld in the abode of Brahmā the spotless Brahmā himself.

**14.35.18**  Bowing to that great-souled one as he sat at ease, the great seers, full of humility, asked of him this supreme highest good:

**14.35.19**  'How may action be well performed? How may one be freed from sin? Which are our auspicious paths? What is truth, and what is wrongdoing?'

**14.35.20**  'By what are the two paths of action traversed? By what does one win greatness? And whence the dissolution and final release, the arising and ceasing of beings?'

**14.35.21**  Thus questioned by those foremost sages, what the Great-grandsire said—that I shall fully declare to you. Hear it, O disciple, as it has come down.

**14.35.22**  Brahmā said: From truth are beings born, the standing and the moving alike; by austerity they live. Know this, O you of good vows.

**14.35.23**  Returning again to their own source, they continue on by their own deeds; for truth, joined with the virtues, is fixed and has five marks.

**14.35.24**  Brahman is truth, austerity is truth, and Prajāpati too is truth; from truth are beings born, and this great creation is made of truth.

**14.35.25**  Therefore the Brāhmaṇas, having truth for their refuge, ever devoted to yoga, beyond anger and torment, self-controlled, are the very bridges of the Law.

**14.35.26**  Of these learned ones, restrained toward one another, who set in motion the bridges of the Law, eternal and sustaining the worlds—of them I shall fully tell.

**14.35.27**  The fourfold knowledge, the four social orders, and the four stages of life severally—these the wise call the one eternal Law with its four supports.

**14.35.28**  I shall declare to you, O twice-born, the auspicious path that brings security, ordained for the attainment of the state of Brahman, trodden of old by the wise.

**14.35.29**  As I now speak of that supreme path, so hard to know, attend, O highly fortunate ones, in its entirety to the supreme station.

**14.35.30**  They call the stage of the chaste student the first station; the householder's life would be the second; thereafter comes the forest-dweller's; and beyond that is to be known the spiritual life, the supreme station.

**14.35.31**  Light, ether, the Sun, the Wind, Indra, Prajāpati—so long as one does not approach the spiritual life, so long does one not behold these. The means thereto I shall declare beforehand; attend to it.

**14.35.32**  For sages who feed on fruit, root, and air, dwelling in the forest, the forest-dweller's mode of life is enjoined for the three twice-born orders.

**14.35.33**  For all the social orders alike the householder's life is ordained; thus the steadfast declare that Law, whose mark is faith.

**14.35.34**  Thus have these paths leading to the gods been proclaimed to you—trodden by the good and the steadfast, by their works the bridges of the Law.

**14.35.35**  Whoever, of firm vow, severally follows the Law of these stages, in due time ever beholds the arising and the ceasing of beings.

**14.35.36**  Now I shall declare the principles truly and with their causes—all of them abiding in their spheres, operating each in its own portion.

**14.35.37**  The Great Self, the Unmanifest, and likewise the I-maker; the senses, ten and one besides; and the five great elements;

**14.35.38**  and the distinctive qualities of the five elements—such is the Vedic teaching. This count of four-and-twenty principles has been declared to you.

**14.35.39**  Now whoever knows the arising and ceasing of all the principles—that steadfast one falls into no delusion amid any of the beings.

**14.35.40**  Whoever truly knows the principles, and all the qualities, and all the deities entire—he, his sin shaken off and his bondage cast away, attains to all the stainless worlds.

**14.36.1**  Brahmā said: That Unmanifest, unagitated, all-pervading, fixed, and steadfast—one should know it as a city of nine gates, of three strands, of five elements.

**14.36.2**  Encompassed by eleven, with mind as its analyzing power and intellect as its lord—this would be that supreme eleventh.

**14.36.3**  The three streams that swell within it again and again—these very three channels, made of the strands, are what flow forth.

**14.36.4**  Tamas, rajas, and sattva—these they call the strands. All are paired with one another, and likewise all live upon one another.

**14.36.5**  They lean upon one another, and follow upon one another, and are interwoven with one another—the three strands and the five elements.

**14.36.6**  The mate of tamas is sattva, the mate of sattva is rajas; and of rajas too the mate may be sattva, and the mate of sattva is tamas.

**14.36.7**  Where tamas is restrained, there rajas comes into play; where rajas is restrained, there sattva comes into play.

**14.36.8**  One should know tamas to be of the nature of night, threefold, called delusion, marked by unrighteousness, and fixed upon evil deeds.

**14.36.9**  Rajas they declare to be of the nature of activity, bringing things about in turn; engaged in all beings, it is seen, marked by origination.

**14.36.10**  Illumination in all beings, lightness, faithfulness—such is the form of sattva; and this lightness is esteemed by the good.

**14.36.11**  The true nature of these strands shall be told with reasons and counter-reasons; with their parts combined and analyzed, know them from me in their reality.

**14.36.12**  Bewilderment, ignorance, non-renunciation, indecision in deeds; sleep, stupor, fear, greed, grief, the spoiling of good works;

**14.36.13**  forgetfulness, immaturity of judgment, unbelief, inconstancy of conduct, lack of discernment, blindness, the practice of the basest qualities;

**14.36.14**  thinking a thing done that is undone, fancying oneself knowing where there is no knowledge, unfriendliness, a perverted disposition, faithlessness, deluded imagining;

**14.36.15**  crookedness, want of consciousness, sinful action, insentience, heaviness, a state of collapse, darkness, a downward course;

**14.36.16**  all these qualities, O Brāhmaṇas, are declared to belong to tamas, together with whatever other settled states in this world are called delusion.

**14.36.17**  In this case and that, all those qualities of tamas are reckoned, as is the constant speech that reviles the gods, the Brāhmaṇas, and the Vedas.

**14.36.18**  Non-renunciation, conceit, delusion, wrath, and intolerance, and envy toward beings—such conduct is held to be of tamas.

**14.36.19**  Whatever undertakings are vain, and gifts that are vain, and vain eating—this conduct is held to be of tamas.

**14.36.20**  Excessive speech, intolerance, jealousy, overweening pride, and faithlessness—such conduct is held to be of tamas.

**14.36.21**  Whatever men of such kind there are in this world, doers of evil who have broken the bounds of decorum—all those are people of tamas.

**14.36.22**  I shall declare the appointed wombs of those doers of evil, who go downward into hell and to the lot of beasts and of hell:

**14.36.23**  unmoving beings, cattle, beasts of burden, flesh-eaters, biting serpents, worms, insects, and birds;

**14.36.24**  egg-born creatures and all four-footed beings; the mad, the deaf, the dumb, and whatever others are afflicted by sinful diseases.

**14.36.25**  Sunk in darkness, ill-conducted, marked by what their own deeds have made them—flowing downward—these, the men of tamas, lie sunk in darkness.

**14.36.26**  Their rise and ascent I shall now declare hereafter—how those doers of meritorious deeds attain the worlds of the well-doing.

**14.36.27**  But those who have set out otherwise and grown great in their works—of Brāhmaṇas devoted to their own duties and seeking the good—

**14.36.28**  by their sacraments they rise upward, striving toward fellowship of world; they go to the heaven of the gods—such is the Vedic teaching.

**14.36.29**  But those who have set out otherwise, grown great in their own works, subject to the law of return, are born here again as men.

**14.36.30**  Having fallen into a sinful womb—as outcastes, the dumb, the deformed—they nevertheless attain the social orders in turn, each higher than the last.

**14.36.31**  Passing beyond the womb of the Śūdra, those and the other qualities of tamas, gathered midway in the stream, abide within the strand of tamas.

**14.36.32**  Attachment to objects of desire is remembered as the Great Delusion; here even seers, sages, and gods, longing for pleasure, fall into delusion.

**14.36.33**  Tamas, delusion, the great delusion, darkness called wrath—and death is the blinding darkness; darkness is said to be wrath.

**14.36.34**  By its states, by its qualities, and by the wombs it leads to, in truth—all this concerning tamas, O Brāhmaṇas, has been duly proclaimed to you.

**14.36.35**  Who indeed truly understands this, who indeed truly sees it—who, perceiving the real amid the unreal, discerns the true mark of tamas?

**14.36.36**  The qualities of tamas have been proclaimed to you in their many forms, and tamas, higher and lower, has been duly told; for the man who ever knows these qualities is released from all the qualities of tamas.

**14.37.1**  Brahmā said: I shall now declare to you rajas as it truly is, O most excellent ones; give heed, O greatly fortunate, to the working of this quality in all its forms.

**14.37.2**  Aggregation, form, exertion, pleasure and pain, cold and heat, lordship, conflict, treaty-making, disputation, discontent, forbearance —

**14.37.3**  strength, valour, intoxication, fury, toil and quarrel as well, envy, craving, slander, war, possessiveness, protection,

**14.37.4**  killing, binding, and torment, buying and selling too, and the cutting at another's vital points with cries of 'cut, slash, split!' —

**14.37.5**  the cruel and the harsh, abuse, the directing of others' wealth, worldly anxiety and brooding, spite, and reproachful speech,

**14.37.6**  false speech and false giving, doubt and contention, blame, praise and eulogy, fierce splendour, gratification,

**14.37.7**  attendance and the wish to serve, service, thirst, dependence, marshalling of forces, misrule, heedlessness, affliction, and acquisition,

**14.37.8**  and whatever rites and dispositions arise in this world, severally, among men, among women, among creatures, in goods, and in refuges,

**14.37.9**  distress and distrust, and those vows and observances, and the giving joined with the prayer 'let this ever be mine' —

**14.37.10**  the utterance of svadhā, salutation, the utterance of svāhā, the vaṣaṭ-rite, and both the officiating at sacrifice and the teaching of it, and likewise acceptance of fees — these too they call rajas.

**14.37.11**  'Let this be mine, let this be mine' — attachment born of the qualities, and treachery, and illusion, deceit, and pride as well,
