**14.37.12**  theft, injury, calumny, anguish, sleeplessness, stubbornness and hypocrisy, and passion, devotion, fondness, and merriment,

**14.37.13**  gambling and gossip, and the ties contracted through women, dancing, instrumental music, and song, and whatever attachments there be — all these, O brāhmaṇas, are proclaimed to be qualities of rajas.

**14.37.14**  The strivings on earth after things past, present, and to come, ever bent upon the threefold aim — dharma, artha, and kāma —

**14.37.15**  those who follow desire rejoice in the abundance of every desire; these are the downward-flowing, the fiery ones, enveloped by rajas.

**14.37.16**  In this world they take their joy, being born again and again; they strive both for the fruit that follows death and for what belongs to this world; they give and receive, they mutter prayers and they pour oblations.

**14.37.17**  The qualities of rajas have been recounted to you in their many forms, and the working of the quality told as it is; for the man who ever knows these qualities is freed from all the qualities of rajas.

**14.38.1**  Brahmā said: Next I shall declare the third and highest quality, the good of all beings in the world, the blameless law of the virtuous.

**14.38.2**  Bliss, delight, exaltation, luminous clarity, and happiness too, generosity, freedom from agitation, contentment, faithfulness,

**14.38.3**  forbearance, steadfastness, and non-injury, evenness, truth, uprightness, freedom from anger and from envy, purity, skill, and prowess —

**14.38.4**  knowledge held free of self-interest, conduct free of it, service free of it, toil free of it — he who is thus disciplined in duty attains the infinite in the world beyond.

**14.38.5**  Free of 'mine,' free of ego, free of expectation, even-minded toward all, unstricken by desire — this is the eternal law of the virtuous.

**14.38.6**  Trust, modesty, and endurance, renunciation, purity, vigilance, kindliness, freedom from delusion, compassion toward beings, freedom from slander,

**14.38.7**  gladness, satisfaction, and wonder, humility, good conduct, works of peace and inner cleansing, an auspicious mind, and deliverance,

**14.38.8**  equanimity and chastity, and renunciation utter and complete, freedom from 'mine,' freedom from expectation, the practice of duty unbought —

**14.38.9**  giving free of self-interest, sacrifice free of it, study free of it, vow free of it, acceptance of gifts free of it, duty free of it, austerity free of it.

**14.38.10**  Whoever in this world walk thus, taking their stand in sattva — those brāhmaṇas, abiding in the source that is Brahman, are the steadfast, the seers of the good.

**14.38.11**  Having cast off all sins, free of sorrow, ageless and deathless, those steadfast ones, attaining heaven, then work their will here and there.

**14.38.12**  Sovereignty and mastery and the swiftness of mind — these the great-souled ones bring forth at will, like the gods who roam the triple heaven.

**14.38.13**  These are remembered as the upward-flowing, the gods born of modification; having reached heaven, they transform things by their very nature, here and there; whatever they desire, all that they enjoy and apportion.

**14.38.14**  Thus has the conduct of sattva been told to you, O bulls among the twice-born; knowing this duly, one obtains whatever he desires.

**14.38.15**  The qualities of sattva have been proclaimed in detail, and the working of the quality told as it is; the man who ever knows these qualities enjoys the qualities, and is not himself devoured by them.

**14.39.1**  Brahmā said: The qualities cannot be told here as wholly distinct from one another; rajas, sattva, and tamas are seen unbroken, blent together.

**14.39.2**  They cleave to one another, they live upon one another, all of them lean upon one another and follow after one another.

**14.39.3**  As far as sattva extends, so far tamas is present — of this there is no doubt; and as far as tamas and sattva reach, so far, it is said here, reaches rajas.

**14.39.4**  Joined together they make their journey, traveling in company; for these act as an aggregate and proceed by causes and non-causes.

**14.39.5**  Among these that abide in one another, in their predominance and their excess, the relation holds throughout, now lesser, now greater.

**14.39.6**  Where tamas is in excess, sinking to the state of the lower creatures, there rajas is to be known as scant, and sattva scantier still.

**14.39.7**  And where rajas is in excess, moving in the middle current, there tamas is to be known as scant, and sattva scantier still.

**14.39.8**  And when sattva is in excess, flowing in the upward current, there rajas is to be known as scant, and tamas scantier still.

**14.39.9**  Sattva is the source of modification, the illuminator of the senses; no other state whatever is ordained as higher than sattva.

**14.39.10**  Those grounded in sattva go upward, those of rajas remain in the middle, and the men of tamas, yoked to the lowest quality, go downward.

**14.39.11**  Tamas in the śūdra, rajas in the kṣatriya, and supreme sattva in the brāhmaṇa — thus do the three qualities revolve among the three classes.

**14.39.12**  Even from afar they are seen together, traveling in company — tamas, sattva, and rajas; we have never heard of their being apart.

**14.39.13**  At the sight of the rising sun, fear comes to wicked thieves, while travelers are scorched, afflicted with thirst and overtaken by distress.

**14.39.14**  The sun is taken to be sattva, the base thieves are as tamas, and the scorching of the travelers is called the quality of rajas.

**14.39.15**  In the sun, its radiance is sattva, its burning the quality of rajas; but its eclipse, at the conjunctions, is to be known as the tāmasa quality.

**14.39.16**  Thus in all the heavenly lights the three qualities revolve, and they hold sway by turns, in this place and that, in this way and that.

**14.39.17**  And in immobile beings, tamas, sunk to the state of the lower order, and rajas qualities revolve there too; but the oily, nurturing essence is sāttvika.

**14.39.18**  The day is to be known as threefold, and the night is ordained as threefold; so too months, fortnights, and years, the seasons, and the junctures of time.

**14.39.19**  Threefold are the gifts that are given, threefold the sacrifice proceeds; threefold the worlds, threefold the Vedas, threefold the sciences, threefold the course of beings.

**14.39.20**  Past, present, and to come; dharma, artha, and kāma as well; the prāṇa, the apāna, and the udāna — these too are the three qualities.

**14.39.21**  Whatever there is in this world, all of it rests in these three; the three qualities, ever unmanifest, hold sway — sattva, rajas, and tamas, the eternal creation of the qualities.

**14.39.22**  Tamas is the unmanifest, the auspicious, the eternal, the unborn, the everlasting womb — Nature, modification, dissolution, the primal matter, origin and end.

**14.39.23**  Without excess and without lack, unshaking, unmoving, fixed, both being and non-being — all this unmanifest is remembered as having the three qualities; these are the names to be known by men who ponder the inner self.

**14.39.24**  He who truly knows the names of the unmanifest and the qualities, and all the courses of beings in their pure essence — freed from the body, knowing the truth of their distinction — he, made whole, is released from all the qualities.

**14.40.1**  Brahmā said: From the unmanifest there arose first the Great Self, of mighty intellect, the beginning of all the qualities; it is called the first creation.

**14.40.2**  The Great Self, Mind, Viṣṇu, the All, the mighty Śaṃbhu, intellect, wisdom, perception, and likewise fame, steadfastness, memory —

**14.40.3**  by these synonymous names the Great Self is made manifest; the learned brāhmaṇa who knows it falls not into delusion.

**14.40.4**  With hands and feet on every side, with eyes, heads, and faces on every side, with ears on every side, he stands in the world, pervading all.

**14.40.5**  The Person, whose flame is of mighty splendour, lodged in the heart of all — minuteness, lightness, attainment, the Lord, the imperishable light.

**14.40.6**  There are the worlds of the wise, of those devoted to renunciation, the meditators, ever-yoked, true to their word, their senses conquered,

**14.40.7**  and whoever are endowed with knowledge, free of greed, their wrath subdued, of tranquil mind, steadfast, free of 'mine,' free of ego, all liberated — these indeed attain to greatness.

**14.40.8**  He who knows the holy and supreme course of the Great Self, that steadfast one comes to no delusion in any of the worlds; for in the primal creations it is Viṣṇu who becomes the self-existent Lord.

**14.40.9**  The man who thus knows the Lord seated in the secret heart, the ancient Person of cosmic form, golden, the supreme goal of the wise — that wise one, transcending the intellect, abides beyond it.

**14.41.1**  Brahmā said: That Great One who arose first is then called the I-maker; coming into being as 'I,' it is called the second creation.

**14.41.2**  The I-maker, the origin of the elements, is remembered as the modifying principle; it is the conscious essence of energy, the begetter of creatures, the Lord of beings.

**14.41.3**  It is the source of the gods, the god, and of the mind; the maker of the three worlds; it is called the one that conceives all this as 'I.'

**14.41.4**  For the sages ever devoted to the knowledge of the inner self, their souls refined, perfected through study and sacrifice, this is the eternal world.

**14.41.5**  Drawing these qualities together by the I-maker, the origin of the elements thus creates, the maker of beings; the modifying principle sets all this in motion, and so colours the world with its own radiance.

**14.42.1**  Brahmā said: From the I-maker were born the five great elements — earth, air, ether, water, and fire as the fifth.

**14.42.2**  In these five great elements creatures are bewildered — in sound, touch, and form, and in taste, smell, and action.

**14.42.3**  When the destruction of the great elements draws near and dissolution is at hand, a mighty terror arises in all that bears the breath of life; but the steadfast remain unmoved.

**14.42.4**  If a being is born from this, into that it again dissolves; they dissolve in reverse order and are born each from the one before.

**14.42.5**  Then, when all beings, the moving and the unmoving, have dissolved away, the steadfast, possessed of true memory, are never dissolved at all.

**14.42.6**  Sound, touch, and form, taste, and smell as the fifth, joined with action and cause, are impermanent and known as the marks of delusion.

**14.42.7**  Bound up with greed and procreation, without distinction, owning nothing, mere aggregates of flesh and blood, living upon one another —

**14.42.8**  these are the outer self, wretched, of pitiable conduct; the prāṇa and apāna, the udāna, the samāna, and the vyāna as well —

**14.42.9**  these are the inner self, the five fixed breaths; together with these — speech, mind, and intellect — the world is of eightfold nature.

**14.42.10**  He whose skin, nose, ears, and eyes, whose tongue and speech are restrained, whose mind is wholly pure and whose intellect never strays —

**14.42.11**  He in whom these eight fires never burn the mind—he attains that auspicious Brahman, beyond which nothing further is found.

**14.42.12**  And the eleven that they call, in particular, the sense-organs—born of egoism—those I shall now expound, O twice-born ones.

**14.42.13**  The ear, the skin, the two eyes, the tongue, and the nose as the fifth; the two feet, the anus, and the organ of generation, the two hands, and speech as the tenth.

**14.42.14**  This is called the aggregate of the senses, with the mind as the eleventh. Let one first conquer this aggregate; thereupon Brahman shines forth.

**14.42.15**  Five they call the organs of knowledge, and five the organs of action; the ear and the rest, five in number, they declare to be in truth conjoined with intellect.

**14.42.16**  And the others, undistinguished, are those conjoined with action; in both groups the mind is to be known as present, and intellect would be the twelfth.

**14.42.17**  Thus have these eleven senses been declared by me in order; knowing these, the wise consider their task accomplished.

**14.42.18**  Three are the abodes of beings; a fourth does not arise—dry land, the waters, and the sky; and birth, too, is of four kinds.

**14.42.19**  Egg-born, sprout-born, sweat-born, and womb-born—thus the birth of the aggregate of beings is observed to be of four kinds.

**14.42.20**  Even the immobile beings, and likewise those that move in the sky, one should know to be egg-born, and so too all the creeping things.

**14.42.21**  Worms and creatures of like kind are declared to be sweat-born; this second mode of birth is said to be the lowliest.

**14.42.22**  Those beings which, bursting through the earth, are born as time rolls on—these they call sprout-born, O most excellent of the twice-born.

**14.42.23**  Beings two-footed and many-footed, and those that move on a slanting course—know these too to be womb-born, O most excellent ones.

**14.42.24**  The eternal source that is Brahman is here to be known as twofold: austerity, and meritorious action—such is the doctrine of the wise.

**14.42.25**  Action is to be known as twofold: sacrifice, and the giving of gifts at the rite. The study of one who has been born is meritorious—such is the teaching of the elders.

**14.42.26**  He who knows this duly would be liberated, O bulls among the twice-born; and know him, too, to be freed from all sins.

**14.42.27**  Ether is the first element; the ear is called its relation to the self; sound is its relation to beings; and the quarters of space are therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.28**  The second element is wind; the skin is renowned as its relation to the self; the tangible is its relation to beings; and lightning is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.29**  The third they call light; the eye is said to be its relation to the self; form is then its relation to beings; and the sun is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.30**  The fourth is to be known as the waters; the tongue is held to be its relation to the self; taste is here its relation to beings; and Soma is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.31**  Earth is the fifth element; the nose is held to be its relation to the self; smell is likewise its relation to beings; and Vāyu is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.32**  This fourfold ordering with respect to the five elements has been recounted; hereafter I shall expound the whole threefold reckoning of each sense-organ.

**14.42.33**  The two feet, the brāhmaṇas who perceive the truth declare to be the relation to the self; that which is to be traversed is the relation to beings; and Viṣṇu is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.34**  The downward-moving breath, the apāna whose course is downward—the anus is held to be the relation to the self; excretion is the relation to beings; and Mitra is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.35**  The procreation of all beings—the organ of generation is called the relation to the self; semen, likewise, is the relation to beings; and the divinity is Prajāpati.

**14.42.36**  The two hands, those who know the relation to the self declare to be the relation to the self; acts are the relation to beings; and Śakra is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.37**  Speech, sacred to all the gods and preceded by the mind, is here called the relation to the self; what is to be spoken is the relation to beings; and Agni is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.38**  The mind, follower of the five elements, they call the relation to the self; what is to be thought is the relation to beings; and the moon is the presiding divinity.

**14.42.39**  The intellect, which ranges over the six senses, they call the relation to the self; what is to be discerned is the relation to beings; and Brahmā is therein the presiding divinity.

**14.42.40**  This ordering of the relation to the self has been duly proclaimed to you by me; for the knowledge of it, O knowers of dharma, is attained here by the wise.

**14.42.41**  The senses, the objects of the senses, and the five great elements—gathering all these together with the mind, one should hold them firmly in contemplation.

**14.42.42**  When the entire mind has been dissolved, no happiness rooted in birth is sought; that happiness, in the view of the wise, belongs to those whose being is full of knowledge.

**14.42.43**  Hereafter I shall expound that auspicious cessation toward all beings—producing a subtle state—whether by gentle means or by harsh.

**14.42.44**  Free of the qualities yet endowed with quality, free of attachment, solitary in conduct, uninterrupted—this they call the conduct befitting the brāhmaṇa, the one abode of happiness.

**14.42.45**  The wise man who, like a tortoise drawing in its limbs, withdraws all desires entirely—stainless and freed on every side—that man is forever happy.

**14.42.46**  Restraining his desires within the self, with thirst extinguished and mind composed, a friend to all beings, full of goodwill, he goes to union with Brahman.

**14.42.47**  By the restraint of all the senses that crave their objects, and by the sage's abandonment of inhabited regions, the fire of the inner self is kindled.

**14.42.48**  As fire kindled by fuel shines forth as a great light, so by the restraint of the senses the great Self shines forth.

**14.42.49**  When, with serene self, one beholds all beings within the heart of the self, then, self-originated, one attains the unsurpassed, subtler than the subtle.

**14.42.50**  Fire is form, water is the flowing stream, and wind is touch; earth is the dreadful bearer of mire, and ether is hearing.

**14.42.51**  Pervaded by passion and grief, encompassed by five streams, conjoined with the five elements, having nine gates and two presiding deities—

**14.42.52**  full of rajas, yet unseen, possessed of the three qualities and the three humours, delighting in contact, deluded—such is the conception of the body.

**14.42.53**  Hard to traverse in this world of the living, it rests upon each individual existence; and it is this very thing that turns as the wheel of time in this world.

**14.42.54**  This dreadful, fathomless great ocean named delusion—one may let it loose or draw it in, and rouse to wakefulness the world together with its gods.

**14.42.55**  Desire and anger, fear, delusion, hostility, and falsehood—by the restraint of the senses he forsakes these things so hard to forsake.

**14.42.56**  He by whom these five constituents bearing the three qualities are conquered in the world—for him the supreme, infinite abode is discerned in the firmament.

**14.42.57**  Having crossed the river whose banks are desire, whose far shore is endless, fearful with the current of the mind, and dangerous with its whirlpools, one should conquer both desire and anger.

**14.42.58**  Freed from all faults, he then beholds what is supreme; fixing the mind within the mind, he beholds the Self within the self.

**14.42.59**  All-knowing, in all beings he sees the Self within the self—now as one, now as many—transforming himself this way and that.

**14.42.60**  He surely beholds forms, as a hundred lamps are lit from a single lamp; he indeed is Viṣṇu and Mitra, Varuṇa, Agni, and Prajāpati.

**14.42.61**  For he is the Ordainer and the Disposer; he is the Lord, facing in all directions; as the heart of all beings the great Self shines forth.

**14.42.62**  Him the assemblies of brāhmaṇas, and gods and demons, the yakṣas, the piśācas, the ancestors, the birds, the hosts of rākṣasas, and all the throngs of spirits, and the great seers as well, forever praise.

**14.43.1**  Brahmā said: Among men the foremost is the prince, the kṣatriya, of the middle quality; among beasts of burden, the elephant; and among forest-dwellers, the lion.

**14.43.2**  The ram, among all the small cattle; and the rat, among dwellers in burrows; the bull, among cattle; and among women, the man indeed.

**14.43.3**  The banyan and the jambu tree, the pippala and the śālmali, the śiṃśapā and the meṣaśṛṅga, and likewise the hollow, whistling bamboos—these are the kings among trees in this world; herein is no doubt.

**14.43.4**  Himavān and Pāriyātra, the Sahya, the Vindhya, the Trikūṭa, the Śveta and the Nīla, the Bhāsa, and the mountain rich in timber—

**14.43.5**  the fair-shouldered, and Mahendra, and the mountain Mālyavān—these are the kings among mountains; and among hosts, likewise, the Maruts.

**14.43.6**  The sun is lord of the planets, and the moon of the constellations; Yama is lord of the ancestors, and the ocean of the rivers.

**14.43.7**  Varuṇa is the king of the waters; Mitra is said to be the king of living creatures; the sun is the overlord of hot things, and the moon is called the king of the lights of heaven.

**14.43.8**  Agni is ever the lord of beings; Bṛhaspati is lord of the brāhmaṇas; Soma is lord of the healing herbs; and Viṣṇu is the foremost of the mighty.

**14.43.9**  Tvaṣṭṛ is the sovereign of forms; Śiva is the lord of beasts; the sacrifice, likewise, is lord of priestly fees; and the seers, of the Vedas.

**14.43.10**  Of the quarters, the north; of the brāhmaṇas, the glorious king Soma; Kubera, of all the yakṣas; and Purandara, of the gods. This is the creation beginning with the elements, and Prajāpati is the lord of creatures.

**14.43.11**  Of all beings I am the great one, made of Brahman; no being higher than I, or than Viṣṇu, is found.

**14.43.12**  The king of all kings is Viṣṇu, the great one, made of Brahman; him we know to be the Lord—he is the all-pervading, he is Prajāpati.

**14.43.13**  For he is the Lord of all—of men, kiṃnaras, and yakṣas, of gandharvas, serpents, and rākṣasas, of gods, dānavas, and nāgas.

**14.43.14**  Of all those fair-eyed ones who follow the god Bhaga, the great goddess, consort of Maheśvara, who is called Pārvatī, is proclaimed the highest.

**14.43.15**  Know the goddess Umā to be the most excellent and auspicious of women; among delights, the bounteous earths; and among women, likewise, the apsarases.

**14.43.16**  Kings desire dharma, and brāhmaṇas bear the mark of dharma; therefore let the king strive here in the protection of the twice-born.

**14.43.17**  For those kings in whose realm the virtuous sink into ruin—they, bereft of all their own merits, travel after death by the downward path.

**14.43.18**  But those kings in whose realm the virtuous are well protected—they rejoice in this world, and after death those great-souled ones attain the very state of infinity; know this, O bulls among the twice-born.

**14.43.19**  Hereafter I shall expound the fixed defining mark of dharma: dharma has non-injury as its mark, and injury is the mark of unrighteousness.

**14.43.20**  The gods have radiance as their mark; men have action as their mark; ether has sound as its mark; and wind has touch as its mark.

**14.43.21**  The mark of the luminaries is form, and the waters have taste as their mark; the earth, bearer of all beings, has smell as her mark.

**14.43.22**  Speech, refined with vowels and consonants, has truth as its mark; the mark of the mind is thought; and the intellect, accordingly, is so described by inference.

**14.43.23**  Contemplating objects with the mind, one resolves upon them with the intellect; for the intellect is marked by determination—herein is no doubt.

**14.43.24**  Meditation is the mark of the Great Principle; the unmanifest has the mark of the holy; yoga has the mark of activity, and knowledge has the mark of renunciation.

**14.43.25**  Therefore, placing knowledge foremost, let the wise man here renounce; the renunciant, joined with knowledge, attains the supreme goal—transcending the pairs of opposites, he approaches that which is beyond darkness, death, and old age.

**14.43.26**  That which is joined with the defining marks of dharma has been duly declared to you by me; hereafter I shall rightly expound the apprehension of the qualities.

**14.43.27**  The smell that belongs to earth is here grasped by the nose; and the wind seated in the nose is appointed to the knowledge of smell.

**14.43.28**  The essence that is the constituent of the waters is ever grasped by the tongue; and Soma, seated in the tongue, is appointed to the knowledge of taste.

**14.43.29**  The quality of light is form, and form is grasped by the eye; and the sun, seated within the eye, is ordained for the knowing of form.

**14.43.30**  Touch belongs to the wind, and it is perceived by the skin; and the wind, seated within the skin, is ordained for the knowing of touch.

**14.43.31**  The quality of ether is sound, and it is grasped by the ear; and all the quarters, seated within the ear, are declared the means for knowing sound.

**14.43.32**  The quality of mind is thought, and it is grasped by understanding; and the conscious element seated in the heart is ordained for the knowing of mind.

**14.43.33**  The intellect acts through resolve, and the Great One through meditation; their grasping is ever a determining—the Unmanifest, in this there is no doubt.

**14.43.34**  The Knower of the Field is eternal, grasped by no sign, devoid of qualities in its nature; therefore the Knower of the Field is signless, marked by pure knowledge alone.

**14.43.35**  The Unmanifest is declared the Field, the source and dissolution of the qualities. Ever do I see what lies hidden within; I know it, and I hear it.

**14.43.36**  The Spirit comes to know that Field, and therefore is called the Knower of the Field; the Knower of the Field beholds the whole working of the qualities entire.

**14.43.37**  With beginning, middle, and end as its limits, unconscious and ever being created—the qualities, themselves created again and again, do not know the Self.

**14.43.38**  Indeed none knows the truth; the Knower of the Field alone attains that which is higher than the high, greater than the qualities and what is born of them.

**14.43.39**  Therefore the knower of truth, abandoning here both the qualities and their principle, with his faults worn away and the qualities forsaken, then enters into the Knower of the Field.

**14.43.40**  Free from the pairs of opposites, beyond salutation and beyond the offering-cry, unmoving and homeless—that Knower of the Field is the supreme, all-pervading Lord.

**14.44.1**  Brahmā said: That which has beginning, middle, and end, together with the means of its apprehension, joined with name and mark—all this I shall declare to you in truth.

**14.44.2**  Day comes first, then night; the months are reckoned from the bright fortnight onward; the lunar mansions from Śraviṣṭhā onward, and the seasons from the cool season onward.

**14.44.3**  Earth is the source of scents, and water of tastes; light is the source of forms, and wind is called the origin of touch; ether is the source of sound—such is the quality wrought by the elements.

**14.44.4**  Beyond this I shall now proclaim the highest origin of beings: the sun is the source of lights, and fire is held to be the source of the elements.

**14.44.5**  The Sāvitrī is the source of all branches of knowledge, Prajāpati of the gods; the syllable Oṃ is the source of all the Vedas, and breath of speech; whatever is fixed in this world is all called Sāvitra, born of the Sun.

**14.44.6**  The Gāyatrī is the source of metres; the goat is called the source of cattle; cows are the source of four-footed beasts, and the twice-born of men.

**14.44.7**  The hawk is the source of birds, the oblation the highest of sacrifices; and of all creeping things the serpent is the foremost, O best of the twice-born.

**14.44.8**  The Kṛta is the first of all the ages—in this there is no doubt; gold is the first of all gems, and barley of the healing herbs.

**14.44.9**  Of all things to be chewed and eaten, grain is called supreme; and of all fluids that may be drunk, water is the highest.

**14.44.10**  Among all immovable beings without distinction, the ever-holy plakṣa tree, the field of Brahman, is remembered as the first-born.

**14.44.11**  And I am the first of all the Prajāpatis—in this there is no doubt; Viṣṇu, whose nature is beyond thought, is mine, and he is remembered as the Self-existent.

**14.44.12**  The great Meru is remembered as the eldest-born of all the mountains; and of the quarters and intermediate quarters, the upward direction was born first.

**14.44.13**  So too Gaṅgā, who flows by three paths, is remembered as the eldest-born of rivers; and the ocean is the eldest of all lakes and wells of water.

**14.44.14**  Of gods, demons, and spirits, of piśācas, serpents, and rākṣasas, of men, kiṃnaras, and yakṣas—of all he is the Lord and ruler.

**14.44.15**  Viṣṇu, the mighty one made of Brahman, is the source of the whole universe; no being higher than he exists here in the three worlds.

**14.44.16**  Of all the stages of life, the householder's is the first—in this there is no doubt; the Unmanifest is the source of the worlds, and it too is the end of all.

**14.44.17**  Days end in sunset, and night ends with the rising of the sun; the end of pleasure is ever pain, and the end of pain is ever pleasure.

**14.44.18**  All accumulations end in waste, all risings end in a fall; unions end in separation, and life indeed ends in death.

**14.44.19**  Everything done ends in destruction; for what is born, death is certain; in this world all things, moving and unmoving, are impermanent.

**14.44.20**  Sacrifice offered, gifts given, austerity, study, vows, and observances—all of this ends in destruction; only of knowledge there is no end.

**14.44.21**  Therefore, by pure knowledge, with serene and steadfast self, free from possessiveness and from the sense of 'I,' one is released from all evils.

**14.45.1**  Brahmā said: With intellect for its core, mind for its pillar, the host of the senses for its fastenings, the great elements for its struts, and the blinking of an eye for its rim—

**14.45.2**  beset by old age and grief, coursing with disease and misfortune, ranging over place and time, sounding with toil and exertion—

**14.45.3**  with day and night for its circuit, cold and heat for its circling ring, the torments that end in pleasure and pain, and hunger and thirst for its rivets—

**14.45.4**  streaked with shadow and sunlight, tossed by the closing and opening of eyes, thronged with men of dreadful delusion, ever turning, and unconscious—

**14.45.5**  counted by months and half-months, uneven, ranging through the worlds, with the heaped mire of darkness and driven by the rush of passion—

**14.45.6**  bright with the ornament of goodness, a wheel formed of the mass of the qualities, with quarrel and discord for its nave, revolving with massed griefs—

**14.45.7**  joined with action and its cause, broad with the spread of desire, reckoned by greed and craving, yet the birthplace of discerning knowledge—

**14.45.8**  surrounded by fear and delusion, working the bewilderment of beings, bearing within it joy and delight, and the grasp of desire and anger—

**14.45.9**  reaching from the Great Principle to the particulars, with unattached origin and undecaying, swift as the mind and tireless—thus the wheel of Time turns onward.

**14.45.10**  This wheel of Time, yoked with the pairs of opposites and unconscious, may send forth the world together with its gods, draw it back, and awaken it.

**14.45.11**  But the man who truly and ever knows the turning forth and the turning back of the wheel of Time is not bewildered among created beings.

**14.45.12**  Freed from all torments, the sage who has passed beyond all pairs of opposites, released from all sins, attains the supreme goal.

**14.45.13**  The householder, the celibate student, the forest-dweller, and the mendicant—these four stages of life are declared, all of them rooted in the householder's state.

**14.45.14**  Whatever scripture is proclaimed here in this world, to reach its end is the higher good—this is the eternal renown.

**14.45.15**  First consecrated by the sacraments, having duly fulfilled his vows, born in a family distinguished by virtue, the knower of the Veda should return home from his studentship.

**14.45.16**  Devoted to his own wife, self-controlled, of cultivated conduct, with senses subdued, full of faith, he should worship with the five great sacrifices.

**14.45.17**  Eating what remains after the gods and guests are served, intent upon the rites of the Veda, given to sacrifice and to giving, according to his means and according to the rule.

**14.45.18**  The sage is not restless with hand or foot, not restless with the eye, nor restless in speech or limb—such is the proper sphere of the cultivated man.

**14.45.19**  He should always wear the sacred thread, be clad in white, of pure vows, restrained in self-control and giving, and should ever consort with the cultivated.

**14.45.20**  Having mastered his loins and belly, friendly, settled in cultivated conduct, he should bear a staff of bamboo and a water-pot filled with water.

**14.45.21**  Having studied, he should teach; likewise he should sacrifice and officiate at sacrifice, give and receive—thus he should follow the sixfold livelihood.

**14.45.22**  Of these, three acts are the livelihood of brāhmaṇas: both officiating at sacrifice and teaching, and accepting gifts from a pure source.

**14.45.23**  And the other three acts that remain—giving, study, and sacrifice—those are bound up with sacred duty.

**14.45.24**  In those three acts the knower of duty should be vigilant: self-controlled, friendly, endowed with patience, a sage equal toward all beings.

**14.45.25**  The pure brāhmaṇa, accomplishing all this according to his power—thus yoked, the householder of resolute vows shall win heaven.

**14.46.1**  Brahmā said: Thus, by this path declared before and according to rule, one who has studied to the best of his power and likewise observed celibacy—

**14.46.2**  the learned sage devoted to his own duty, restraining all his senses, intent on what is dear and good to his teacher, given to truth and righteousness, and pure—

**14.46.3**  with his teacher's leave he should eat his food without disparaging it, living on sacrificial fare and on what is begged, regulated in standing, sitting, and movement.

**14.46.4**  Offering into the fire at the two times, having become pure and composed, he should ever bear a staff of bilva wood or of palāśa.

**14.46.5**  The garment of the twice-born, whether of linen, of cotton, or even an antelope's hide, should all be dyed with ochre.

**14.46.6**  His girdle should be of muñja grass; he should wear matted locks, perform his ablutions always, wear the sacred thread, study his lessons, and keep his fixed vows unbroken.

**14.46.7**  The celibate student who, restrained in his disposition, ever makes the offering of water to the gods with purified water, is praised.

**14.46.8**  Thus yoked, with seed drawn upward and composed, he shall win heaven; he wanders no more among births, having reached the supreme abode.

**14.46.9**  Consecrated by all the sacraments and likewise observant of celibacy, the sage, departing from the villages and going forth into the forest, should dwell there.

**14.46.10**  Clad in hide and bark, at dawn he should himself perform his ablutions; ranging always in the forest, he should not enter a village again.

**14.46.11**  Honoring guests in due season, he should also offer them shelter, sustaining himself on fruits, leaves, lowly roots, and wild millet.

**14.46.12**  He should partake, in due order and according to his consecration and unwearied, of flowing water, of air, and of all that the forest yields, down to grass.

**14.46.13**  With roots, fruits, and the alms he has gathered he should honor a guest who comes; whatever be his own food, from that he should ever give alms, unwearied.

**14.46.14**  Serving the gods and guests first, he should always eat with restrained speech, his mind unwavering, eating lightly, his refuge in the gods.

**14.46.15**  Self-controlled, friendly, endowed with patience, wearing his hair and beard, offering oblations, given to the study of scripture, and devoted to truth and righteousness—

**14.46.16**  having renounced care of the body, ever skillful, abiding in the forest and composed—thus yoked, the forest-dweller with senses subdued shall win heaven.

**14.46.17**  And the householder, the celibate student, or again the forest-dweller—whoever wishes to abide in liberation should take up the highest mode of life.

**14.46.18**  Having granted freedom from fear to all beings, he should practice freedom from action—the sage devoted to the welfare of all beings, friendly, restraining all his senses.

**14.46.19**  Unasked, unprepared for, and come by chance—such food he should always partake of, content with the mouthful that comes, free from longing.

**14.46.20**  He should eat only enough to sustain his journey, merely to keep life going; he should eat only what is righteously gained, and not pursue desire.

**14.46.21**  Beyond a mouthful and a covering he should take nothing whatsoever; only as much as he may eat should he accept, and no more.

**14.46.22**  He should accept nothing from others, nor ever give to them; but the wise one, out of compassion for the wretched state of beings, should always share what he has.

**14.46.23**  He should not take what belongs to others, nor accept what was not begged for; and having once enjoyed any object of sense, he should not crave it again.

**14.46.24**  When need arises, he may take earth, water, and stone, and leaves, flowers, and fruits that lie uncovered and freely available here.

**14.46.25**  He should not live by a craftsman's trade, nor desire food twice over; he should be neither a hater nor a teacher, and remain without adornment; he should eat only what has been hallowed by faith, and shun all gain got through omens.

**14.46.26**  Living without contrivance, unattached, holding no covenant with any creature, he should go for alms when the fire is smokeless and the householders have already eaten.

**14.46.27**  When the clatter of the serving-dishes has ceased, the knower of liberation should seek his alms; gaining them, let him not rejoice, and failing to gain them, let him not be downcast.

**14.46.28**  Eating in measure, awaiting the proper hour, composed, he should go for alms; he should not desire a gain shared in common, nor eat as one who has been honored — for the mendicant should shrink from a gain that comes through being honored.

**14.46.29**  While eating he should not savor the sour, the acid, the bitter, the astringent and pungent, nor the sweet flavors either; he should eat only enough to keep going, merely to sustain the breath of life.

**14.46.30**  The knower of liberation should seek his livelihood without obstructing any creature, and while begging he should in no way beg following after another.

**14.46.31**  He should not parade his piety; free of passion, let him dwell in solitude. For shelter he may resort to an empty house, a forest, the foot of a tree, a riverbank, or again a mountain cave.

**14.46.32**  In summer he should pass but a single night in a village; in the rains he may stay in one place. Let his road be the one the sun marks out, and let him roam the earth like an insect.

**14.46.33**  And out of compassion for living things he should look upon the ground as he walks; he should lay up no hoards, and shun any dwelling that breeds attachment.

**14.46.34**  The knower of liberation should always do his needful tasks with purified water; and a man should ever sip and touch with water that has been drawn up out of its source.

**14.46.35**  Non-injury, chastity, truth, and uprightness, freedom from anger, freedom from envy, self-restraint, and a constant abstaining from slander —

**14.46.36**  in these eight vows he should be steadfast, his senses curbed; and he should ever follow a course of conduct sinless, guileless, and straight.

**14.46.37**  Rites bound up with self-seeking prayer, and those bound up with injury, and the dharma performed for the world's regard — these he should neither do himself nor cause others to do.

**14.46.38**  Passing beyond all states of being, with the lightest of burdens let him wander forth, the same toward all creatures, the moving and the unmoving alike.

**14.46.39**  He should alarm no other being, nor take alarm at any; one whom all creatures may trust is called the foremost knower of liberation.

**14.46.40**  He should not brood on what has not yet come, nor dwell in thought on what is past; he should be indifferent to the present, composed, awaiting his hour.

**14.46.41**  Neither with the eye, nor with the mind, nor with speech should he ever do harm; and whether openly or in secret he should commit no evil act.

**14.46.42**  Drawing in his senses on every side, as a tortoise draws in its limbs, with senses, mind, and intellect grown still, let him gaze inward as one beyond the senses.

**14.46.43**  Beyond the pairs of opposites, beyond all salutation, beyond the cry of 'svāhā,' without the sense of 'mine,' without the sense of 'I,' beyond all care for getting and keeping —

**14.46.44**  free of expectation toward all beings, free of clinging, dependent on nothing, all-knowing, freed on every side — such a one is liberated; in this there is no doubt.

**14.46.45**  That Self is without hand or foot or back, without head, without belly, devoid of the qualities and of action, alone, stainless, and unmoving;

**14.46.46**  without smell or taste, without touch, without form or sound; without skin or bone, without marrow, and without flesh as well —

**14.46.47**  those who behold that Self — free of all care, imperishable, eternal, ever seated in the heart and abiding in all beings — they do not die.

**14.46.48**  There the intellect does not reach, nor the senses, nor the gods, nor the Vedas, the sacrifices, and the worlds, nor austerity, nor heroic effort — there, it is taught, the wise attain by a grasping that is without sign or mark.

**14.46.49**  Therefore the knower of dharma, bearing no outward mark, faithful to the vow of dharma, the wise man who has taken to a hidden practice of it, should move through life with his ways unknown.

**14.46.50**  Though clear-minded, he should follow dharma in the guise of a simpleton, doing it no harm, in such a way that others will continually hold him in contempt.

**14.46.51**  So conducting himself, he should follow dharma without sullying the path of the good; for he who is endowed with such conduct is called the foremost of sages.

**14.46.52**  The senses and the objects of sense, the five great elements, the mind and the intellect, the self, the Unmanifest, and likewise the Person —

**14.46.53**  having reckoned up all this, and rightly renounced it, he becomes stainless; then, freed from every bond, he attains the realm beyond.

**14.46.54**  Reckoning up this very truth at the hour of his end, the knower of reality should meditate, abiding in solitude; then, dependent on nothing, he is set free.

**14.46.55**  Released from all attachments, like the wind that moves through space, his sheath worn away and free of fear, he attains the supreme abode.

**14.47.1**  Brahmā said: The elders of settled vision declare that renunciation is austerity; the brāhmaṇas grounded in the source that is Brahman know knowledge to be the supreme Brahman.

**14.47.2**  Not far off lies the supreme Brahman, the resort of the Vedic lore, beyond the pairs of opposites, without quality, eternal, unthinkable, hidden, and highest.

**14.47.3**  Through knowledge and through austerity the steadfast behold that abode, their darkness scoured away, made pure, risen above passion, and stainless.

**14.47.4**  By austerity those who seek the highest tread the safe road — they who delight ever in renunciation, men who are the knowers of Brahman.

**14.47.5**  Austerity, they say, is a lamp, and right conduct accomplishes dharma; but knowledge we know to be supreme, and renunciation the highest austerity.

**14.47.6**  He who, through certainty of the truth, knows the unimpeded knowledge — the Self that abides in all beings — is held to be the one whose course goes everywhere.

**14.47.7**  The wise man who perceives both union and separation, and likewise oneness and manyness, is wholly freed from sorrow.

**14.47.8**  He who desires nothing and disdains nothing — that man, even while dwelling in this world, is fit to become one with Brahman.

**14.47.9**  He who knows the truth of primal Nature and its qualities, who understands the ordering of all beings, without the sense of 'mine' and without the sense of 'I,' is liberated; in this there is no doubt.

**14.47.10**  Beyond the pairs of opposites, beyond all salutation, beyond the cry of 'svadhā,' by tranquility alone he reaches the quality-less, the eternal, the one beyond all duality.

**14.47.11**  Forsaking all action made of the qualities, good and ill alike, and forsaking both truth and falsehood, the creature is liberated; in this there is no doubt.

**14.47.12**  Sprung from the seed of the Unmanifest, mighty, its trunk made of the intellect, with the great 'I'-principle for its boughs and the inner senses for its hollows;

**14.47.13**  with the great elements for its great branches and the particulars for its lesser branches, ever in leaf, ever in flower, putting forth fruits both fair and foul — such is the eternal tree of Brahman, the sustenance of all beings.

**14.47.14**  Cutting and cleaving this tree with the supreme sword of knowledge, and forsaking it, and gaining deathlessness, one should cast off death and birth; without the sense of 'mine' and without the sense of 'I,' he is liberated; in this there is no doubt.

**14.47.15**  These two birds are eternal companions, and both are without consciousness; but he who is held to be the conscious one is higher than these two.

**14.47.16**  The one without consciousness is yoked to the aggregate of sattva; higher than sattva, the inner Self gives consciousness. He is the Knower of the Field, whose understanding works through the aggregate of sattva, and passing beyond the qualities, he is freed from the noose of death.

**14.48.1**  Brahmā said: Some hold the supreme to be the Brahman-tree, some the great Principle made of Brahman, some the Person, some the Unmanifest, some that which is beyond and free of all affliction; yet all of this springs from the Unmanifest and is imperishable.

**14.48.2**  If a man, even for the space of a single breath at the hour of his end, becomes evenminded, then having drawn near to the Self he is fit for immortality.

**14.48.3**  If, even for the space of a wink, he restrains the self within the Self, then by the grace of the Self he reaches the imperishable attainment of the wise.

**14.48.4**  Restraining the breaths again and again by exercises of breath-control — by ten or by twelve, and then beyond that by twenty-four —

**14.48.5**  thus, his self first made serene, he gains whatever he desires; and the sattva that has risen up out of the Unmanifest is fit to attain immortality.

**14.48.6**  Those who know it praise nothing higher in this world than sattva; by inference we know the Person who has sattva for his refuge — and otherwise, O twiceborn, that Person cannot be reached.

**14.48.7**  Patience, steadfastness, non-injury, evenness, truth, uprightness, knowledge, relinquishment, and renunciation — this is held to be the conduct that springs from sattva.

**14.48.8**  By this very inference the wise then hold that sattva and the Person are one; in this there is no debate.

**14.48.9**  But certain learned men, well established in knowledge, say: this oneness of the Knower of the Field and of sattva does not hold.

**14.48.10**  That the one is forever distinct from the other — this is held beyond dispute; yet their distinctness must in truth be understood as also being innate and shared together.

**14.48.11**  And so the reasoning of the wise admits both oneness and manyness; for in the gnat and the fig there is seen both a union and a separateness.

**14.48.12**  As the fish is a thing apart within the waters, so is the conjunction of these two; their bond is like that of waterdrops upon the leaf of the red lotus.

**14.48.13**  The teacher said: When the Grandsire of the world had spoken thus, those brāhmaṇas, those best of the twiceborn, fell again into doubt and put their questions to him.

**14.48.14**  The seers said: Which among the dharmas, then, is held in this world the most worthy to be practiced? For we behold the manifold course of dharma as if it were at war with itself.

**14.48.15**  Some say there is a state above the body; others say it is not so. Some hold that all is a matter of doubt; others, that it is beyond doubt.

**14.48.16**  Some say it is impermanent, others permanent; some that it is not, others that it is. Some say it is of a single form, others twofold, others a blending of both; some say it is one, others that it is separate, and others still that it is many.

**14.48.17**  Thus think the brāhmaṇas, wise men who behold the truth of things. Some wear matted locks and skins, others are shaven, and some go uncovered.

**14.48.18**  Some choose to forgo bathing, others to bathe; some choose to take food, while others delight in fasting.

**14.48.19**  Some praise action, others tranquility; some praise both place and time, while others say there is no such thing. Some extol liberation, others the enjoyments of their various kinds.

**14.48.20**  Some desire riches, others poverty; some hold that worship is the means, while others say it is not so.

**14.48.21**  Some are devoted to non-injury, others bent upon injury; some say that fame comes through merit, while others say it is not so.

**14.48.22**  Some are intent on the good and the real, others remain in a state of doubt; some hold to sorrow, some to happiness, and others rest in meditation.

**14.48.23**  Some of the steadfast say it is sacrifice, others that it is giving; some praise all of these, while others say not all of them are good.

**14.48.24**  Others praise austerity, and some men the study of the sacred texts; some say it is knowledge and renunciation, while those who ponder the elements say it is inherent nature.

**14.48.25**  Thus, when dharma is raised up and goes racing off in so many directions, we cannot reach certainty, and are bewildered, O best of the gods.

**14.48.26**  'This is best, this is best' — so the people set forth; for whoever delights in a given dharma forever exalts that one.

**14.48.27**  At this our understanding is confounded and our mind drawn many ways; we wish you to declare this to us — what, O best one, is the highest good?

**14.48.28**  And what is yet more secret than this, that you should be pleased to tell us: by what cause is there a bond between sattva and the Knower of the Field?

**14.48.29**  Thus addressed by those brāhmaṇas, the blessed Lord, the world's creator, righteous of soul and wise, declared it to them according to the very truth.

**14.49.1**  Brahmā said: Come now, I shall declare to you, O most excellent ones, that which you ask of me; hear it all here, and lay it rightly to heart.

**14.49.2**  Non-injury toward all beings — this is held the most worthy thing to do; this is the untroubled state, the highest mark of dharma.

**14.49.3**  The elders who perceive things with certainty declare that knowledge is the highest good; therefore by pure knowledge a man is freed from all his sins.

**14.49.4**  Those in the world who are devoted to violence, those whose conduct is that of unbelievers, and those bound by greed and delusion—these surely go the way to hell.

**14.49.5**  But those who untiringly perform actions joined to desire for reward rejoice in this world, being born again and again.

**14.49.6**  But those wise and faithful men who perform their actions joined to a discipline free of craving—they are the steadfast ones, seers of the good.

**14.49.7**  Now beyond this I shall declare how the union and the separation of primal matter and the Knower of the Field come about; learn it well, O best of men.

**14.49.8**  This is called the relation here: that of the object and of objecthood. The Spirit is ever the experiencing subject, while matter is held to be the object.

**14.49.9**  This has been explained before by the earlier illustration, as of the gnat and the fig: matter, being unconscious, never knows that it is being enjoyed; but there is one who knows, who enjoys, and who is enjoyed.

**14.49.10**  They say matter is impermanent, bound to the pairs of opposites, and made up of the strands of nature; but the Knower of the Field is beyond the pairs, indivisible, eternal, and free of the strands.

**14.49.11**  When, having attained this awareness, he is seen as the same everywhere, he ever enjoys matter as the lotus leaf bears the water upon it.

**14.49.12**  Though joined with all the strands of nature, the wise man is not stained by them; just as a trembling drop of water rests upon a lotus leaf, even so, beyond doubt, the Spirit remains unattached.

**14.49.13**  The certainty is that matter is merely the material substance belonging to the Spirit; as there is substance and an agent, so too there is the union of these two.

**14.49.14**  As a man takes up a lamp and walks through the darkness, so by the lamp of pure matter the seekers of the supreme make their way.

**14.49.15**  As long as the quality of the fuel lasts, so long the lamp shines forth; when the quality of the fuel is exhausted, the light passes into extinction.

**14.49.16**  Thus the quality of matter is manifest, while the Spirit is held to be unmanifest. Understand this, O brāhmaṇas; come, I shall tell you yet more.

**14.49.17**  Even with a thousand portions the dull-witted man attains no growth, whereas with but a quarter portion the wise man prospers with ease.

**14.49.18**  In like manner one should understand the accomplishment of righteousness through right means; for the intelligent man who knows the means enjoys boundless happiness.

**14.49.19**  As a man without provisions who sets out upon a road somewhere travels on with great hardship, or even perishes along the way,

**14.49.20**  so too in the matter of works it must be understood that the fruit may come or may not come; the highest good lies in the Self, which reveals the auspicious and the inauspicious.

**14.49.21**  And as one suddenly sets out on foot alone upon a long road never seen before, bereft of any vision of the truth,

**14.49.22**  and as one might traverse that same road by a swift-going chariot yoked with horses—such is the course of the wise.

**14.49.23**  Having climbed a high mountain, the charioteer should not look down at the one upon the ground, the dull man toiling along, whom he beholds from his own chariot.

**14.49.24**  As far as there is a road fit for the chariot, so far he goes by chariot; but when the chariot-road comes to an end, the wise man abandons the chariot and proceeds on foot.

**14.49.25**  Thus the intelligent man advances, knowing the rule of yoga and the truth; the man of great wisdom, having fully grasped each higher stage, passes from the higher to the still higher.

**14.49.26**  As a man without a boat who, out of delusion, plunges into the terrible great ocean with his arms alone undoubtedly meets his death,

**14.49.27**  so the wise man who knows the distinctions, plunging unwearied into the water by a boat fitted for crossing, surely crosses over quickly.

**14.49.28**  Having crossed, free of the sense of mine, he abandons the boat and goes on to the farther shore. This has been explained by the earlier illustration, as of the chariot-rider and the man on foot.

**14.49.29**  As the ferryman who out of attachment has fallen into delusion remains there upon his boat, so the man overcome by the sense of mine keeps turning about in that same place.

**14.49.30**  Having boarded a boat, one cannot move about upon dry land; even so, having mounted a chariot, no passage over the waters is ordained.

**14.49.31**  Thus the various deeds that are done, each set within its own sphere of objects, bear their fruit severally; as a deed is done in the world, so it comes to pass.

**14.49.32**  That which has neither smell nor taste, neither form nor touch nor sound—this the sages perceive with their understanding and declare to be the Primordial Nature.

**14.49.33**  Therein the Primordial Nature is the Unmanifest; the evolute of the Unmanifest is the Great Principle; and of the Great, become the foundation, the evolute is the I-sense itself.

**14.49.34**  Of the I-sense, become the foundation, the evolute is what produces the great elements; for the sense-objects of the elements are severally held to be their qualities.

**14.49.35**  As the Unmanifest has the nature of a seed, so too it has the nature of bringing forth. We have heard that the Great Self has the nature of a seed and is also a bringer-forth.

**14.49.36**  The I-sense too has the nature of a seed and brings forth again and again; and the five great elements have the nature both of seed and of bringing-forth.

**14.49.37**  The particularized sense-objects of the five elements they call seed-natured, yet these do not bring forth further; know that they are but the distinguishing marks of those elements.

**14.49.38**  Therein ether has one quality, air is said to have two, fire they call possessed of three qualities, and water of four.

**14.49.39**  Earth is to be known as having five qualities, teeming with things moving and unmoving, the goddess who fashions all beings, the revealer of the auspicious and the inauspicious.

**14.49.40**  Sound, touch, and form, taste, and smell as the fifth—these five are to be known as the qualities of earth, O best of the twice-born.

**14.49.41**  Smell is ever earthly, and smell is held to be of many kinds; of that smell I shall declare in detail its many qualities.

**14.49.42**  Pleasant and unpleasant in smell, sweet, sour, and pungent, pervading, compact, oily, dry, and clear as well—thus earthly smell is to be known as of ten kinds.

**14.49.43**  Sound, touch, and form, and taste are held to be the qualities of water; but I shall declare the knowledge of taste, for taste is held to be of many kinds.

**14.49.44**  Sweet, sour, pungent, bitter, astringent, and salty—thus the taste born of water is held to be sixfold in its extent.

**14.49.45**  Sound, touch, and form—fire is said to have three qualities; and the quality of fire is form, and form is held to be of many kinds.

**14.49.46**  White, black, and red, blue, yellow, and tawny; short, long, and thick, square, minute, and round—

**14.49.47**  thus the form belonging to fire is said to be twelvefold in its extent, to be known ever by brāhmaṇas who know the law and speak the truth.

**14.49.48**  Sound and touch are to be known as the two qualities by which air is said to be twofold; and the quality of air is touch, and touch is held to be of many kinds.

**14.49.49**  Hot, cold, pleasant, painful, oily, and clear, hard, greasy, smooth, slimy, harsh, and soft—

**14.49.50**  thus the quality belonging to air is said to be twelvefold in its extent, to be known duly by perfected brāhmaṇas who know the law and perceive the truth.

**14.49.51**  Therein ether has one quality, and that is held to be sound; of that sound I shall declare in detail its many qualities.

**14.49.52**  Ṣaḍja and Ṛṣabha, Gāndhāra, Madhyama, and Pañcama, and beyond these are to be known Niṣāda and Dhaivata.

**14.49.53**  Pleasant and unpleasant in tone, combined and divided—thus sound, which arises from ether, is to be known as of many kinds.

**14.49.54**  Ether is the highest of the elements; beyond it is the I-sense; higher than the I-sense is the intellect; and the Self is higher than the intellect.

**14.49.55**  Higher than that is the Unmanifest, and higher than the Unmanifest is the Spirit, the knower of what is high and low among beings, on attaining whom one enjoys the Infinite.

**14.50.1**  Brahmā said: Now of these five elements the mind is the lord, both in restraint and in release; the mind is the very self of the elements.

**14.50.2**  The mind is ever the overseer of the great elements; the intellect proclaims their sovereignty; and the Knower of the Field is said to be the All.

**14.50.3**  The mind yokes the senses as a charioteer yokes good horses; and the Knower of the Field ever yokes the senses, the mind, and the intellect.

**14.50.4**  The chariot is joined with the great elements and curbed by the intellect; having mounted it, the embodied self runs about on every side.

**14.50.5**  Joined with the host of the senses, having the mind for its charioteer and ever reined in by the intellect, the great chariot is made of Brahman.

**14.50.6**  The wise man who thus ever knows this chariot made of Brahman—that steadfast one falls into no delusion in any of the worlds.

**14.50.7**  It begins with the Unmanifest and ends in the particulars; it teems with things moving and unmoving; it is a world lit by the radiance of moon and sun, adorned with planets and stars,

**14.50.8**  everywhere graced with a network of rivers and mountains, and ever embellished with manifold waters,

**14.50.9**  the livelihood of all beings, the resort of all that bears the breath of life. This is the eternal forest of Brahman, in which the Knower of the Field roams.

**14.50.10**  Whatever beings there are in this world, unmoving and moving, these dissolve first; afterward the qualities produced by the elements; from the qualities come the five elements—such is the upbuilding of beings.

**14.50.11**  Gods, men, and gandharvas, piśācas, asuras, and rākṣasas—all are created by their own inherent nature, not from rites and not from any other cause.

**14.50.12**  These makers of the universe, O brāhmaṇas, are born again and again; brought forth from them, they dissolve back into those very five great elements in due time, as waves dissolve into the sea.

**14.50.13**  From the world-creating elements one passes to the great elements; and freed from the five elements, one may go on to Prajāpati.

**14.50.14**  The Lord Prajāpati created this whole world by austerity alone; and even so the seers attained the Vedas through austerity.

**14.50.15**  And by the gradual practice of austerity, living on fruits and roots, the perfected ones, with concentrated minds, behold the triple world here through their austerity.

**14.50.16**  Herbs, antidotes, and the like, and the manifold sciences in every form, are perfected by austerity alone; for the means has austerity for its root.

**14.50.17**  Whatever is hard to attain, hard to learn, hard to assail, hard to compass—all that may be accomplished by austerity; for austerity cannot be surpassed.

**14.50.18**  The drinker of liquor, the slayer of a brāhmaṇa, the thief, the killer of an embryo, the violator of the teacher's bed—by austerity well practiced they are freed from their sin.

**14.50.19**  Men, ancestors, and gods, cattle, beasts, and birds, and whatever other beings there are, moving and unmoving—

**14.50.20**  ever devoted to austerity, they attain perfection always through austerity; and even so the gods, those greatly fortunate ones, went to heaven by austerity.

**14.50.21**  But those who untiringly perform actions joined to desire for reward, bound up with the I-sense—they dwell in the presence of Prajāpati.

**14.50.22**  By pure meditative yoga, free of the sense of mine and of the I-sense, the great-souled ones attain the great and supreme world.

**14.50.23**  Coming through meditative yoga, ever serene in mind, they enter, by their command of self, into the Unmanifest, the storehouse of bliss.

**14.50.24**  Coming through meditative yoga, free of the sense of mine and of the I-sense, they enter here into the Unmanifest, the great and supreme world.

**14.50.25**  Arisen from the Unmanifest itself, the knower of the right season returns again; released from darkness and passion, taking his stand on pure goodness alone,

**14.50.26**  freed from all sins, indivisible, he renounces all. One should know him as the Knower of the Field; and whoever knows him is a knower of the Veda.

**14.50.27**  Reaching pure thought from thought, let the sage sit restrained; becoming of one mind with that on which his thought is set—this is the eternal mystery.

**14.50.28**  That which begins with the Unmanifest and ends with the particulars is held to bear the mark of ignorance. Learn now how this is characterized by the qualities.

**14.50.29**  The two-syllabled word is death; the three-syllabled is the eternal Brahman. 'Mine' is death, and 'not mine' is the everlasting.

**14.50.30**  Some men, the more dull-witted, praise action; but those who are awakened, the great-souled, do not praise action.

**14.50.31**  By action a creature is born, embodied and composed of sixteen parts; but ignorance brings forth the Puruṣa, who is ungraspable and feeds on the deathless.

**14.50.32**  Therefore whoever sees to the farther shore is free of attachment to actions; for this Puruṣa is held to be made of knowledge, not made of action.

**14.50.33**  He who finds this Self—without a prior, deathless, eternal, undivided, ungraspable, feeding on the deathless—he by these means becomes ungraspable, deathless, and steadfast.

**14.50.34**  Casting off all imaginings and restraining the self within the self, he comes to know that auspicious Brahman, beyond which nothing further is found.

**14.50.35**  By the very serenity of his being he attains serenity; for the mark of that serenity is like the vision seen in a dream.

**14.50.36**  This is the course of the liberated, of those firmly established in knowledge, who see that all the workings of the world are born of transformation.

**14.50.37**  This is the course of the unattached, this the eternal law; this is the attainment of those who possess knowledge, this the conduct beyond reproach.

**14.50.38**  By one who is even toward all beings, free of craving and free of desire, looking on all things alike, this course may be traversed everywhere.

**14.50.39**  All this I have declared to you, O most excellent of sage-priests. Conduct yourselves thus without delay, and you shall thereby attain perfection.

**14.50.40**  The teacher said: Thus addressed by Brahmā their teacher, those great-souled sages did accordingly, and thereby won the higher worlds.

**14.50.41**  You too, O greatly blessed one, follow rightly this word of Brahmā as it was spoken, O pure-souled one, and thereby you shall attain perfection.

**14.50.42**  Vāsudeva said: Thus addressed by his teacher, that disciple then performed the whole of this highest law, O son of Kuntī, and thereupon attained release.

**14.50.43**  Having done what was to be done, that disciple, O upholder of the Kuru line, reached that state, having attained which one grieves no more.

**14.50.44**  Arjuna said: Who indeed was that brāhmaṇa, Kṛṣṇa, and who the disciple, O Janārdana? If this is fit for me to hear, then tell it to me, O mighty one.

**14.50.45**  Vāsudeva said: I am the teacher, O mighty-armed one, and know the mind to be my disciple. Out of love for you this secret has been told by me, O Dhanaṃjaya.

**14.50.46**  If you bear love toward me always, O upholder of the Kuru line, then having heard this teaching of the inmost Self, conduct yourself rightly, O man of good vows.

**14.50.47**  Then, when this law has been rightly practiced, O delight of the Kurus, your self cleansed of all sin, you shall attain absolute release.

**14.50.48**  This very thing I told you before as well, when the hour of battle was at hand, O mighty-armed one; therefore fix your mind upon it.

**14.50.49**  But it is long, O best of the Bharatas, since I have seen my father, O lord; I wish to see him, with your consent, O Phalguna.

**14.50.50**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: To Kṛṣṇa, who had spoken these words, Dhanaṃjaya replied: Let us two go this very day, Kṛṣṇa, to the city named after the elephant.

**14.50.51**  Having met there with the king, the righteous-souled Yudhiṣṭhira, and taken leave of that unassailable one, you may set out for your own city.

**14.51.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then Kṛṣṇa urged Dāruka, saying, 'Let the chariot be yoked'; and within a moment Dāruka announced, 'It is yoked.'

**14.51.2**  Likewise the son of Pāṇḍu gave orders for the retinue: 'Make ready; we shall set out for the city named after the elephant.'

**14.51.3**  Thus addressed, those soldiers made themselves ready, O lord of peoples, and announced to Pārtha of boundless splendor, 'All is ready.'

**14.51.4**  Then the two, Kṛṣṇa and the son of Pāṇḍu, mounted the chariot and set forth, exchanging varied and delightful talk, well pleased with each other, O lord of peoples.

**14.51.5**  To Vāsudeva, who stood within the chariot, the mighty Dhanaṃjaya once again spoke these words, O best of the Bharatas.

**14.51.6**  By your grace the king has won victory, O upholder of the Vṛṣṇi line; the foes have been slain, and a kingdom free of thorns has been gained.

**14.51.7**  In you the Pāṇḍavas have found their protector, O slayer of Madhu; making you our raft, we have crossed the ocean that was the Kurus.

**14.51.8**  Maker of all, homage be to you; Soul of all, Source of all—even as I know you, even as my mind is fixed on you.

**14.51.9**  From your splendor the fire that devours the offering ever arises, O slayer of Madhu; delight made of play is yours, and the two world-halves are your magic power, O lord.

**14.51.10**  In you rests all this universe, whatever is fixed or moving; for it is you who fashion the whole eternal host of beings.

**14.51.11**  Earth and the middle air, and all that is fixed and moving—your laughter is the spotless moonlight, and the seasons follow upon the senses as your train.

**14.51.12**  The ever-moving wind is your breath; wrath is the eternal death; and in your favor abides the lotus-seated Śrī, dwelling ever in you, O great-minded one.

**14.51.13**  Delight, contentment, steadfastness, and patience—and this whole world of moving and unmoving things—rest in you; and you alone, O sinless one, are called the dissolution at the ends of the ages.

**14.51.14**  Even in the longest stretch of time your virtues and your supreme Self could not be told by me; homage to you, O lotus-eyed one.

**14.51.15**  You are known to me, O unassailable one, from Nārada and from Devala, and from Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, and likewise from the grandsire of the Kurus.

**14.51.16**  On you all things depend; you alone are the lord of beings; and what has been spoken by you, O sinless one, is joined with grace.

**14.51.17**  All this I shall carry out rightly, O Janārdana; and this exceedingly wondrous deed has been done out of desire for our welfare.

**14.51.18**  For the wicked son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the Kaurava, was slain in battle; it was you who consumed that host, while I but won the victory in the fight.

**14.51.19**  By you was that deed wrought through which I gained the victory over Duryodhana in the war, by your wisdom and your valor.

**14.51.20**  And duly was the means shown for the slaying of Karṇa, and of the wicked lord of the Sindhus, and of Bhūriśravas as well.

**14.51.21**  And whatever you, well pleased, have bidden me, O joy of Devakī, that I shall do; in this I have no hesitation at all.

**14.51.22**  And going to the king, the righteous-souled Yudhiṣṭhira, I shall urge him to grant leave for your departure, O knower of dharma, O sinless one.

**14.51.23**  This journey of yours to Dvārakā pleases me well, O lord; and soon, O slayer of Madhu, you will see your uncle, and the unassailable Baladeva, and the other bulls among the Vṛṣṇis.

**14.51.24**  Conversing thus, the two reached the city named after the elephant, and together they entered it, thronged with men full of joy.

**14.51.25**  Going to the house of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, like the dwelling of Śakra, the two beheld, O great king, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the lord of men.

**14.51.26**  And Vidura of great wisdom, and King Yudhiṣṭhira, and the unassailable Bhīmasena, and the two sons of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas, seated in attendance on Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and the unvanquished Yuyutsu.

**14.51.27**  And Gāndhārī of great wisdom, and Pṛthā, and the lovely Kṛṣṇā, and Subhadrā and all the rest, all the women of the Bharatas—these they beheld, all standing about, gathered around Gāndhārī.

**14.51.28**  Then the two subduers of foes, approaching King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, announced their own names and clasped his feet.

**14.51.29**  And the two great-souled ones clasped likewise the feet of Gāndhārī and of Pṛthā, and of the King of Dharma, and of Bhīma as well.

**14.51.30**  And having honored the steward Vidura and inquired after his unfailing welfare, together with them they then waited upon the aged king.

**14.51.31**  Then at nightfall, O great king, the wise Dhṛtarāṣṭra dismissed the upholders of the Kurus, and Janārdana, to their dwellings.

**14.51.32**  Dismissed by the king, they went each to his own abode; but the valiant Kṛṣṇa went to the very house of Dhanaṃjaya.

**14.51.33**  There honored according to custom, and provided with every comfort, the wise Kṛṣṇa slept, with Dhanaṃjaya for his companion.

**14.51.34**  When the night had brightened into dawn, the two, having performed the morning rites and being highly honored, went to the palace of the King of Dharma, where the great-minded King of Dharma sat with his counselors.

**14.51.35**  Then the two mighty ones, entering, beheld the King of Dharma seated, like the two Aśvins beholding the king of the gods.

**14.51.36**  Approaching the king, those two bulls among the Vṛṣṇis and the Kurus took their seats, given leave by him who was well pleased with them.

**14.51.37**  Then that wise king, best among speakers and most excellent of kings, seeing that the two wished to speak, addressed them with these words.

**14.51.38**  I think you both wish to speak, O heroes, upholders of the Yadus and the Kurus. Say on; I shall do all that you ask without delay—let there be no hesitation.

**14.51.39**  When this was said, Phalguna, skilled in speech, drew near humbly and spoke these words to the King of Dharma.

**14.51.40**  Long has the glorious Vāsudeva dwelt here, O king; with your leave he wishes to go and see his father.

**14.51.41**  Let him go, granted leave by you, if you think it fit; you should give the hero leave to go to the city of the Ānartas.

**14.51.42**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: O lotus-eyed one, blessing be upon you; go, O slayer of Madhu, this day to the city of Dvāravatī, to see the lord, the son of Śūra.

**14.51.43**  Your going pleases me, O mighty-armed Keśava; long is it since you have seen my uncle and the lady Devakī.

**14.51.44**  Honor my uncle Vasudeva and Baladeva, O Mādhava, O greatly wise one, in my name, as is fitting.

**14.51.45**  And remember me always, O Mādhava, and Bhīma, foremost among the strong, and Phalguna, and Nakula, and Sahadeva.

**14.51.46**  When you have looked upon the Ānartas, O mighty-armed one, and your father and the Vṛṣṇis, come again, O sinless one, for my horse-sacrifice.

**14.51.47**  Go then, taking jewels and manifold treasures; and whatever else is pleasing to you, take that too, O Sātvata.

**14.51.48**  For this whole earth, O Mādhava, has come to us by your grace, O hero, and the foes have been slain.

**14.51.49**  As the Kaurava, the King of Dharma, Yudhiṣṭhira, spoke thus, Vāsudeva, best of men, uttered these words.

**14.51.50**  The jewels and the wealth are yours alone, and this whole earth as well, O mighty-armed one; and whatever other riches are in my houses, of those too you are the lord, ever the master.

**14.51.51**  Saying 'So be it,' and thus honored in turn by the son of Dharma, the valiant elder brother of Gada saluted his father's sister according to custom; and being honored, he made his reverent circuit around them.

**14.51.52**  Duly greeted in return by her, and likewise by all, Vidura and the rest, the elder brother of Gada, Hari, then went forth from the city of the elephant on his divine chariot drawn by four horses.

**14.51.53**  Setting the lovely Subhadrā upon his chariot, with the leave of Yudhiṣṭhira and of his father's sister as well, the mighty-armed Janārdana went forth, surrounded by the townsfolk.

**14.51.54**  He whose banner bears the foremost of monkeys followed him, together with Sātyaki, and the two sons of Mādravatī, and Vidura of fathomless wisdom followed the Mādhava, and Bhīma himself, with the might of the king of elephants.

**14.51.55**  Having turned back all those increasers of the Kuru realm, and Vidura too, the valiant Janārdana then said in haste to Dāruka, and Sātyaki likewise, 'Urge on the horses.'

**14.51.56**  Then the crusher of throngs of foes, Janārdana, followed by the hero of Śini's line, set forth—even as Śatakratu, having slain the hosts of his enemies, goes to heaven, so the glorious one went to the city of the Ānartas.

**14.52.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: As the Vārṣṇeya thus set out for Dvārakā, those bulls among the Bharatas, those scorchers of foes, embraced him together with their retinue and turned back.

**14.52.2**  Again and again Phalguna embraced the Vārṣṇeya, and again and again he gazed upon him so long as he remained within sight.

**14.52.3**  With great effort Pārtha at last withdrew his gaze, which had been fixed upon Govinda; and so too did the unvanquished Kṛṣṇa withdraw his.

**14.52.4**  Hear from me, as I tell of them, the many omens of wondrous form that appeared at the departure of that great-souled one.

**14.52.5**  With great force the wind blew before his chariot, making the path free of gravel, free of dust, and free of thorns.

**14.52.6**  And Vāsava rained down water pure and fragrant, and divine flowers as well, before the wielder of the Śārṅga bow.

**14.52.7**  As the mighty-armed one journeyed on across the level desert wastes, he beheld the foremost of sages, Uttaṅka of boundless power.

**14.52.8**  The lustrous, large-eyed one honored the sage, and being himself honored by him, then inquired after his well-being.

**14.52.9**  Being asked after his welfare, Uttaṅka, that foremost of brāhmaṇas, honored the slayer of Madhu in turn and then questioned Mādhava.

**14.52.10**  "O Śauri, having gone to the abode of the Kurus and Pāṇḍavas, did you bring about an unshakable brotherhood between them? That you ought to explain to me."

**14.52.11**  "Have you returned, O Keśava, having reconciled those heroes, your kinsmen ever so dear to you, O bull of the Vṛṣṇis?"

**14.52.12**  "Will the five sons of Pāṇḍu and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra now dwell at ease in the world together with you, O scorcher of foes?"

**14.52.13**  "And the kings, with the Kauravas pacified and with you as their protector, will they enjoy happiness in their own realms, O Mādhava?"

**14.52.14**  "That esteem, my dear, which I have ever held toward you — has it been made fruitful by you, O Kṛṣṇa, with respect to the Bharatas?"
