**14.71.15**  victorious, enduring, and bold—he shall guard it; for he is able to conquer the earth, he who made an end of the Nivātakavacas.

**14.71.16**  In him are the celestial weapons, and a celestial frame likewise, a celestial bow and two quivers; he shall follow after it.

**14.71.17**  For he is skilled in dharma and in profit, accomplished in all learning; according to the scriptures, O best of kings, he shall cause your horse to roam.

**14.71.18**  The mighty-armed prince, dark of hue and lotus-eyed, the heroic father of Abhimanyu—he shall follow after it."

**14.71.19**  "Bhīmasena too, the splendid son of Kuntī of boundless valour, is able to guard the kingdom, and Nakula as well, O lord of the people.

**14.71.20**  And Sahadeva, O Kaurava, the wise and greatly renowned, shall duly order the whole management of the household."

**14.71.21**  All that, spoken in due form, the upholder of the Kuru line carried out, and he charged Phalguna with the matter of the horse.

**14.71.22**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "Come, Arjuna; by you, O hero, let this horse be guarded; for you are worthy to protect it, and no other man whatsoever.

**14.71.23**  And those kings who may come forth against you, O mighty-armed—so act that there be no strife with them, O sinless one.

**14.71.24**  And this sacrifice of mine should be made known by you everywhere to the kings, O mighty-armed: 'Let them come at the appointed time.'"

**14.71.25**  Having so spoken, the righteous one appointed his brother the ambidextrous archer—and Bhīma and Nakula—to the guarding of the city.

**14.71.26**  And to the management of the household he appointed Sahadeva, the lord of warriors, after Yudhiṣṭhira had taken leave of King Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**14.72.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When the time of consecration had come, those most eminent priests duly consecrated the king for the horse sacrifice.

**14.72.2**  Having performed the binding of the victims and being consecrated, the son of Pāṇḍu, the righteous king of great splendour, shone forth together with his priests.

**14.72.3**  And the horse, for the sake of the horse sacrifice, was released according to the rule of scripture by the expounder of Brahman himself, by Vyāsa of immeasurable splendour.

**14.72.4**  That king, the son of Dharma, being consecrated, shone forth then, O king, garlanded with gold and golden-collared, blazing like fire.

**14.72.5**  Clad in a black antelope-skin, staff in hand, robed in linen, the radiant son of Dharma shone forth still more, like Prajāpati at the sacrifice.

**14.72.6**  And so too all his priests were of like attire, O lord of the people; and Arjuna likewise was like blazing fire.

**14.72.7**  Dhanaṃjaya of the white horses followed after that black-spotted steed, in due form, O guardian of the earth, at the command of the righteous king.

**14.72.8**  Brandishing the Gāṇḍiva, O king, with leathern guard upon his fingers bound, he followed that horse, joined with gladness, O guardian of the earth.

**14.72.9**  Then, O king, the whole city, down to the children, came forth, O lord, eager to behold the best of the Kurus, Dhanaṃjaya, as he was setting out.

**14.72.10**  From the press of them one against another there arose a kind of heat, as they longed to see both that horse and the follower of the horse.

**14.72.11**  Then a clamour arose, O great king, filling the ten quarters, from the men gazing upon the son of Kuntī, Dhanaṃjaya.

**14.72.12**  "There goes the son of Kuntī, and there the radiant steed which the mighty-armed one follows, his hand upon his peerless bow!"

**14.72.13**  Thus did the victorious one of noble mind hear the words of those who spoke: "Blessing be upon you; go unharmed and come again!" O Bhārata.

**14.72.14**  And yet others, O lord of men, the men said these words: "We cannot see him in the crush; only this bow can be seen.

**14.72.15**  For this is the famed Gāṇḍiva bow of terrible roar. May he go in safety along a road unharmed and free from all fear; we shall behold him again upon his return"—thus too they spoke.

**14.72.16**  Such, O bull of the Bharatas, were the sweet words of the men and women, spoken again and again, that he heard.

**14.72.17**  And a disciple of Yājñavalkya, skilled in the rites of sacrifice and versed to the end of the Vedas, set out together with the Pārtha to perform the propitiatory ceremonies.

**14.72.18**  And many Brāhmaṇas, O guardian of the earth, masters of the Vedas, followed that great-souled one, as did Kṣatriyas and Vaiśyas too.

**14.72.19**  O great king, O most excellent one, that horse roamed over the earth conquered by the Pāṇḍavas through the might of their weapons, wandering at will as ordained.

**14.72.20**  The battles that befell the Pāṇḍava there—those I shall recount to you, O hero, wondrous and great as they were.

**14.72.21**  That horse, O king, O subduer of foes, ranged over the earth keeping it to its right, moving first toward the north—mark this, O guardian of the earth.

**14.72.22**  Trampling down the kingdoms of the rulers of the earth, that finest of horses slowly made his circuit, and with him the great chariot-warrior of the white steeds.

**14.72.23**  There was no numbering then, O great king, of the kings by the ten thousands—the Kṣatriyas whose kinsmen had been slain—who joined battle there.

**14.72.24**  There were Kirātas of monstrous shape, O king, many bearers of sword and bow, and Mlecchas of various kinds besides, men formerly cut down in battle.

**14.72.25**  And noble rulers of the earth, their men and mounts full of joy, many of them maddened for war, came together to fight the son of Pāṇḍu.

**14.72.26**  Thus, O guardian of the earth, battles took place here and there between Arjuna and the rulers of the earth dwelling in various lands.

**14.72.27**  Those great battles, O king, sorely fought out on both sides—those of the son of Kuntī I shall recount to you, O sinless one.

**14.73.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: A battle befell the Diademed one with the Trigartas, who had nursed their enmity—men renowned as great chariot-warriors, the sons and grandsons of the slain.

**14.73.2**  Learning that the excellent sacrificial horse had arrived at the border of their realm, those heroes, clad in mail, then surrounded it.

**14.73.3**  Mounted on chariots drawn by fine, well-caparisoned steeds, their quivers bound on, they encircled the horse, O king, and set about to seize it.

**14.73.4**  Then the Diademed one, that subduer of foes, perceiving what those kings intended to do, restrained those heroes with conciliatory words.

**14.73.5**  Disregarding him, they all then struck at him with arrows; but the Diademed one held back those who were enveloped in darkness and dust.

**14.73.6**  And then Jiṣṇu spoke, as if laughing, O Bhārata: "Turn back, you who know not righteousness; your life itself is the better course for you."

**14.73.7**  For that hero, as he was about to set out, had been enjoined by the King of Righteousness: "O Pārtha, those rulers whose kinsmen have been slain are not to be killed."

**14.73.8**  He, then, recalling that word of the wise King of Righteousness, said to them, "Turn back"; yet they did not turn back.

**14.73.9**  Then Dhanaṃjaya, enmeshing in battle the king of the Trigartas, Sūryavarman, in a net of arrows, laughed aloud.

**14.73.10**  Then they, filling all the quarters with the rumble of their chariots and the clatter of hooves and wheel-rims, rushed upon Dhanaṃjaya.

**14.73.11**  Then Sūryavarman loosed hundreds of straight-jointed arrows at the Pārtha, O lord of kings, displaying the lightness of his hand.

**14.73.12**  And likewise the other mighty archers who were his followers, seeking the death of Dhanaṃjaya, poured forth showers of arrows.

**14.73.13**  With many arrows shot from his bowstring the Pāṇḍava cut down those very many shafts, O king, and they fell upon the ground.

**14.73.14**  But the fiery Ketuvarman, that king's younger brother, a youth, fought for his brother's sake against the great-souled Pāṇḍava.

**14.73.15**  Seeing Ketuvarman bearing down upon him in battle, Bībhatsu, the slayer of hostile heroes, struck him with sharp arrows.

**14.73.16**  When Ketuvarman was struck down, the great chariot-warrior Dhṛtavarman, swiftly wheeling round with his chariot, covered Jiṣṇu with arrows.

**14.73.17**  Beholding the swiftness of the young Dhṛtavarman, the mighty and greatly fiery Guḍākeśa was exceedingly pleased.

**14.73.18**  The son of the chastiser of Pāka could not see him nocking nor drawing, but only ever saw him scattering arrows.

**14.73.19**  He honored Dhṛtavarman in that battle in his mind, and for a moment took delight in him in the fray.

**14.73.20**  That youth, wrathful as a serpent, the Kuru hero, as if smiling and out of fondness, O great king, did not deprive of his life.

**14.73.21**  Thus spared by the Pārtha of boundless might, Dhṛtavarman then loosed a sharp arrow at Vijaya.

**14.73.22**  Pierced sorely by it in the hand even as he was swiftly shooting, Vijaya let the Gāṇḍīva slip from his pain, and it fell upon the ground.

**14.73.23**  As the bow fell from the hand of Savyasācin, O lord, its form was like that of Indra's weapon, O best of the Bharatas.

**14.73.24**  When that divine great bow had fallen, O king, Dhṛtavarman laughed aloud a ringing laugh in that great battle.

**14.73.25**  Then Jiṣṇu, filled with fury, wiped the blood from his hand, took up that divine bow, and rained down a shower of arrows.

**14.73.26**  Then a tumultuous uproar arose, touching the heavens, of beings of every kind who exceedingly praised that deed.

**14.73.27**  Then, beholding Jiṣṇu wrathful, like Yama the all-ending Death, the Trigarta warriors swiftly surrounded him.

**14.73.28**  Rushing in to rescue Dhṛtavarman, they then hemmed Guḍākeśa about; thereupon Dhanaṃjaya grew wrathful.

**14.73.29**  Then he swiftly slew eighteen of their warriors with sharp iron arrows like the thunderbolt of great Indra.

**14.73.30**  Seeing them broken, Dhanaṃjaya in haste struck them with arrows shaped like venomous serpents, laughing aloud.

**14.73.31**  All those great Trigarta chariot-warriors, their spirits broken and tormented by Dhanaṃjaya's arrows, fled in all directions.

**14.73.32**  They said to that tiger among men, the destroyer of the sworn warriors: "We are all your servants, and all of us are subject to your will."

**14.73.33**  "Command us, O Pārtha—we stand here humbled as your servants; we shall do all that pleases you, O delight of the Kurus."

**14.73.34**  Hearing this their word, he then said to them all: "Guard your lives, O kings; let my command be accepted."

**14.74.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, coming to Prāgjyotiṣa, that excellent horse roamed about, and the son of Bhagadatta, fierce in battle, came forth there.

**14.74.2**  As the horse of the son of Pāṇḍu reached the border of his realm, King Vajradatta, O best of the Bharatas, gave battle.

**14.74.3**  Sallying forth from the city, that king, the son of Bhagadatta, drove off the approaching horse and turned back toward the city.

**14.74.4**  Marking this, the mighty-armed bull of the Kurus, swiftly twanging the Gāṇḍīva, at once rushed upon him.

**14.74.5**  Then, stupefied by the shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva, that king of a hero released the horse and rushed instead upon the Pārtha.

**14.74.6**  Entering the city again, that best of kings, clad in mail and mounted upon a noble elephant, came forth in his eagerness for battle.

**14.74.7**  With a white parasol held above his head and a white yak-tail fan waving over him, that great chariot-warrior came on.

**14.74.8**  Then, drawing near to the Pārtha, that great chariot-warrior of the Pāṇḍavas, he challenged the Kaurava to combat, out of youth and folly.

**14.74.9**  Wrathful, he then drove his elephant—huge as a mountain, its temples and cheeks streaming with rut—against him of the white steeds.

**14.74.10**  That elephant, pouring forth rut like a raining cloud, a vanquisher of hostile elephants, trained for war according to the science, able to withstand a triple onset and maddened for battle—

**14.74.11**  That mighty elephant, urged on by that king with the goad, appeared as if about to spring up into the sky.

**14.74.12**  Seeing it bearing down upon him, the wrathful Dhanaṃjaya, O king, standing on the ground, fought against him who rode upon the elephant, O Bhārata.

**14.74.13**  But Vajradatta, full of fury, swiftly loosed at Dhanaṃjaya javelins like fire, hurtling like locusts.

**14.74.14**  Arjuna, with arrows sped from the Gāṇḍīva—those that fly through the air—cut them apart, two and threefold, in the very sky before they reached him.

**14.74.15**  Seeing his javelins thus cut to pieces, the son of Bhagadatta swiftly hurled at the Pāṇḍava arrows that flew unhindered.

**14.74.16**  Then Arjuna, more swiftly still and full of wrath, sped against the son of Bhagadatta arrows with golden feathers that flew straight on.

**14.74.17**  Pierced by them, the greatly fiery Vajradatta, struck hard in that great battle, fell to the earth; yet his consciousness did not forsake him.

**14.74.18**  Then, mounting his noble elephant once more in the battle, calm and seeking victory, he drove it against Vijaya.

**14.74.19**  Then Jiṣṇu, full of wrath, sped at him arrows like venomous serpents let loose, blazing as flames of fire.

**14.74.20**  That great elephant, pierced by them and streaming with blood, then shone like the Himālaya, lord of mountains, with its many cascades.

**14.75.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus for three nights did that battle last, O bull of the Bharatas, between Arjuna and that king, as once between Vṛtra and the Performer of a Hundred Sacrifices.

**14.75.2**  Then on the fourth day the mighty Vajradatta laughed aloud a ringing laugh and spoke these words:

**14.75.3**  "Arjuna, Arjuna, stand fast! You shall not escape me alive. Having slain you, I shall make the water-offering to my father according to rule."

**14.75.4**  "By you was my aged father Bhagadatta, the friend of your father, slain—an old man worthy of honor; now do battle with me, who am but a child."

**14.75.5**  Having spoken thus, the wrathful King Vajradatta, O Kaurava, drove his elephant against the Pāṇḍava.

**14.75.6**  That lordly elephant, driven on by the sagacious Vajradatta, rushed upon the Pāṇḍava as if about to spring into the sky.

**14.75.7**  With the spray flung from the tip of its trunk it drenched Phalguna, O great king, as a dark cloud drenches a mountain.

**14.75.8**  Driven on by that king, the elephant, roaring again and again like a cloud, with the trumpeting blare of its mouth charged upon Phalguna.

**14.75.9**  That lordly elephant, as if dancing, urged on by Vajradatta, swiftly closed, O king, with the great chariot-warrior of the Kauravas.

**14.75.10**  Seeing the elephant of Vajradatta bearing down upon him, the strong slayer of foes, relying on the Gāṇḍīva, did not waver.

**14.75.11**  And the Pāṇḍava grew exceedingly wrathful at that king, recalling the hindrance to his task and the old enmity, O Bhārata.

**14.75.12**  Then the wrathful Pāṇḍava held back that elephant with a net of arrows, as the shore holds back the abode of the crocodiles.

**14.75.13**  That noble elephant, checked by the might of Arjuna, stood still, its body bristling with arrows like a porcupine with its quills.

**14.75.14**  Seeing his elephant checked, the king, the son of Bhagadatta, frenzied with rage, let fly sharp arrows at Arjuna.

**14.75.15**  But Arjuna, O great king, with arrows that shattered other arrows warded off those shafts hurled at him; and it seemed a thing of wonder.

**14.75.16**  Then once more, exceedingly enraged, the king who ruled Prāgjyotiṣa sent forth a mighty lord of elephants, swift as the rushing wind.

**14.75.17**  Seeing it bearing down upon him, the mighty son of the chastiser of Pāka loosed against the elephant an iron shaft that blazed like fire.

**14.75.18**  Struck hard by it in a vital part, O king, that elephant fell suddenly to the earth like a mountain shattered by the thunderbolt.

**14.75.19**  As it fell, that elephant, smitten by Dhanaṃjaya's arrow, shone like a great mountain crushed by the thunderbolt sinking into the earth.

**14.75.20**  When that elephant of Vajradatta had fallen, the son of Pāṇḍu said to the king who had come down to the ground, "You need have no fear."

**14.75.21**  For the mighty-spirited Yudhiṣṭhira had said to me as I set out, "Those kings, Dhanaṃjaya, are by no means to be slain by you."

**14.75.22**  "Let all this be ended here, O tiger among men, with only this much done; nor, Dhanaṃjaya, are the warriors to be slain by you in battle."

**14.75.23**  "And the kings are to be told, together with all their friends: 'Let Yudhiṣṭhira's horse-sacrifice be witnessed by you.'"

**14.75.24**  Having heard these words of my brother, I do not slay you, O lord of men. Rise; you have nothing to fear; go in safety, O king.

**14.75.25**  Come, O great king, when the coming full moon of Caitra is at hand; then will be held the horse-sacrifice of the wise King of Righteousness.

**14.75.26**  Thus addressed, that king, the son of Bhagadatta, then said, "So be it," vanquished as he was by the son of Pāṇḍu.

**14.76.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then a battle arose for the diademed one with the men of Sindhu, O great king, with those left alive from the slaughter and with the sons of the slain.

**14.76.2**  Hearing that he of the white steeds had entered their realm, those kings, unable to brook it, marched out against the bull of the Pāṇḍavas.

**14.76.3**  And those men like venom, having seized that horse at the border of their realm, felt no fear of Pārtha, who came close after Bhīmasena.

**14.76.4**  From no great distance they came upon Bībhatsu, bow in hand, standing on foot near the sacrificial horse.

**14.76.5**  Then those kings of great valor, those tigers among men, eager for victory and humiliated before in battle, surrounded him.

**14.76.6**  Proclaiming their names, their lineages, and their manifold deeds, they then covered Pārtha with showers of arrows.

**14.76.7**  Pouring forth keen arrows able to turn back the lordliest of elephants, longing for victory in battle, they hemmed in the son of Kuntī.

**14.76.8**  Without reckoning that hero of terrible deeds in war, all those heroes, mounted on their chariots, fought against him who stood on foot.

**14.76.9**  They struck at that hero, the destroyer of the Nivātakavacas, the slayer of the Saṃśaptakas and the killer of the lord of Sindhu.

**14.76.10**  Then with a thousand chariots and ten thousand horses they penned in the son of Kuntī as in an enclosure and, full of joy, gave him battle.

**14.76.11**  Those heroes remembered the slaying of the wise king of Sindhu, of Jayadratha, O Kaurava, in battle by Savyasācin.

**14.76.12**  Then all of them, like rain-clouds, let loose a downpour of arrows; covered by them, Pārtha shone like the sun amid the clouds.

**14.76.13**  Wholly veiled in arrows, that bull of the Pāṇḍavas appeared, O Bhārata, like a bird moving within a cage.

**14.76.14**  Then, as the son of Kuntī was sore pressed by arrows, the whole triple world cried out in dismay, O king, and the sun grew red with dust.

**14.76.15**  Then there blew, O great king, a wind that made the hair stand on end, and Rāhu swallowed the sun and the moon at one and the same time.

**14.76.16**  And meteors, scattering in every direction, struck the sun, and there came a trembling, O king, upon the great mountain Kailāsa.

**14.76.17**  The seven seers, seized with fear, and the divine seers too, full of grief and sorrow, shed scalding tears.

**14.76.18**  And the hare swiftly broke through the orb of the moon and fell, turned upside down, O king, in that portent of disaster.

**14.76.19**  Clouds the dusky color of an ass, bearing the rainbow and the lightning, covered the sky and rained down flesh and blood.

**14.76.20**  Such were the omens then, when the hero was enveloped in showers of arrows; in this world, O best of the Bharatas, it seemed a thing of wonder.

**14.76.21**  As he was covered on every side by that net of arrows, through a swoon the Gāṇḍīva and the quiver fell from his hand.

**14.76.22**  When he had fallen into that swoon, the men of Sindhu swiftly loosed a yet greater net of arrows upon the great warrior, his strength fled from him.

**14.76.23**  Then, knowing Pārtha sunk in a swoon, all the dwellers of heaven, their minds dismayed, busied themselves to bring him back to himself.

**14.76.24**  Then all the divine seers, and the seven seers as well, and the Brahman-seers murmured prayers for the victory of the wise Pārtha.

**14.76.25**  Then, when Pārtha's energy had been kindled anew by the gods, O king, the wise master of the highest weapons stood firm as a mountain in the fight.

**14.76.26**  Then the joy of the Kurus drew his divine bow, and there arose from it a great sound, again and again, like that of an engine.

**14.76.27**  Then that mighty Pārtha rained down showers of arrows from his bow upon the foe, as the lord of the gods rains down his rains.

**14.76.28**  Then all those warriors of Sindhu, kings and all, strewn over with arrows, were no longer to be seen, like fires covered with locusts.

**14.76.29**  At the sound of his bow the men of Sindhu trembled, and tormented by fear they fled; afflicted with sorrow they shed tears, and some sank down as if asleep.

**14.76.30**  And the mighty best of men, ranging on every side like a whirling circle of firebrands, O king, overwhelmed them all with nets of arrows.

**14.76.31**  That slayer of foes loosed in every quarter that net of shafts, like an enchantment, as the great Indra wields the thunderbolt.

**14.76.32**  Having rent the army that was like a mass of clouds, that best of the Kurus, radiant as the sun, shone like the maker of day in autumn.

**14.77.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then the heroic bearer of the Gāṇḍīva, standing ready for battle, shone unassailable in the fight like the immovable Himālaya.

**14.77.2**  Then those warriors of Sindhu once more took their stand, and in great fury loosed their showers of arrows, O Bhārata.

**14.77.3**  Overpowering them as they stood arrayed once more, the son of Kuntī of great valor then spoke to those bent on death in gentle words.

**14.77.4**  "Fight with all your might, and strive to slay me; do all your tasks, for a great peril has come upon you."

**14.77.5**  "Lo, I shall fight you all, warding off your snare of arrows; stand firm, your minds set on battle—I shall humble your pride."

**14.77.6**  Having said this much in wrath, the Kuru bearer of the Gāṇḍīva then, O Bhārata, called to mind the words of his eldest brother.

**14.77.7**  "The kṣatriyas eager for victory are not to be slain in battle, dear one, but only conquered"—so had it been told by the great-souled King of Righteousness; and Phalguna, that bull among men, then pondered upon it.

**14.77.8**  "Thus was I told by the lord of men, 'The kings are not to be slain.' How may that fair word of the King of Righteousness not here be falsified?"

**14.77.9**  "The kings should not be slain, and yet the king's command should be carried out"—so reflecting, devoted to his brother's pleasure and welfare, that knower of duty then spoke these words to the battle-maddened men of Sindhu.

**14.77.10**  "The boys and women among you who stand arrayed I shall not slay, nor him who, vanquished in the battle, shall say, 'I am yours.'"

**14.77.11**  "Hearing this word of mine, do what is good for yourselves; otherwise you shall fall into distress, tormented by me."

**14.77.12**  Having thus spoken to those heroes, the bull of the Kurus fought on, unhurried and unangered, against those furious men bent on victory.

**14.77.13**  Then the men of Sindhu loosed hundreds of thousands of arrows with smooth-jointed shafts, O king, upon the bearer of the Gāṇḍīva.

**14.77.14**  Those cruel shafts, like the venom of serpents, as they flew toward him, Dhanaṃjaya cut down with his sharp arrows even in mid-course.

**14.77.15**  And having cut down those swift shafts, heron-feathered and whetted on stone, he pierced each man with ten arrows in the battle.

**14.77.16**  Then, remembering the slain Jayadratha, the kings of Sindhu once more hurled javelins and lances at Dhanaṃjaya.

**14.77.17**  The diademed and high-minded one made their purpose vain; cutting them all down in mid-course, the son of Pāṇḍu shouted aloud in joy.

**14.77.18**  And likewise, as those warriors greedy for victory rushed upon him, he struck off their heads with smooth-jointed broad-headed shafts.

**14.77.19**  And of those who fled, and ran again, and turned back once more, there arose a sound like that of the great ocean at the full.

**14.77.20**  Though they were being slain by Pārtha of measureless energy, they gave battle to Arjuna according to their strength and according to their zeal.

**14.77.21**  Then most of them, their mounts and soldiers worn out, were robbed of their senses by Phalguna in the fight with his smooth-jointed arrows.

**14.77.22**  Knowing them all spent, Duḥśalā, daughter of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, then taking up a boy, her grandson, set out by chariot—the heroic, guiltless son of Suratha.

**14.77.23**  For the sake of peace among all the warriors she approached the son of Pāṇḍu; and coming up to Dhanaṃjaya she then uttered a cry of grief, and the mighty Dhanaṃjaya, seeing her, laid down his bow.

**14.77.24**  His bow laid aside, Pārtha then duly greeted his sister and said, "What shall I do?"; and she spoke these words to him.

**14.77.25**  "This child, O best of the Bharatas, is the son of your sister's son; he salutes you, O hero. Behold him, O bull among men."

**14.77.26**  Thus addressed, Arjuna then asked after the child's father, "Where is he?"; and thereupon, O king, Duḥśalā spoke these words.

**14.77.27**  "Consumed with grief for his own father and stricken with despair, this child's father met his death, O hero; hear from me how that befell."

**14.77.28**  "Having heard before that his father had been slain in battle by you, O sinless one, and learning that you had come, following the horse, to do battle, stricken with grief at his father's death, he gave up his life, O Dhanaṃjaya."

**14.77.29**  "Merely on hearing your name, 'Bībhatsu has come,' O sinless one, stricken with despair my son fell to the earth and died."

**14.77.30**  "Seeing him fallen, O lord, I took up this son of his and have come to you today, seeking refuge."

**14.77.31**  Having so spoken, the daughter of Dhṛtarāṣṭra uttered a cry of grief, and, herself wretched, she addressed Pārtha, who stood downcast with his face bowed low.

**14.77.32**  "Look upon me, your sister, and upon this son of your sister's son; you should show me compassion, O knower of duty, O pride of the Kurus, forgetting the king of the Kurus and that fool Jayadratha."

**14.77.33**  "As Parikṣit, slayer of hostile heroes, was born of Abhimanyu, so was this grandson of mine born of Suratha, O mighty-armed one."

**14.77.34**  "Taking him up, O tiger among men, I have come into your presence for the sake of peace among all the warriors; and hear this my word."

**14.77.35**  "This little grandson of that fool has come, O mighty-armed one; therefore you ought to show favor to this child."

**14.77.36**  "With his head bowed in entreaty, together with me, O subduer of foes, he begs of you, O mighty-armed one: make peace, O Dhanaṃjaya."

**14.77.37**  O Pārtha, show grace to this child, whose kinsmen are slain and who knows little of these matters; O knower of dharma, do not yield to the sway of wrath.

**14.77.38**  Forgetting that his grandfather was ignoble, cruel, and a grievous wrongdoer, you ought to grant him your grace.

**14.77.39**  As Duḥśalā spoke thus piteously, Dhanaṃjaya, remembering the lady Gāndhārī and king Dhṛtarāṣṭra, stricken with grief and sorrow, spoke, reviling the warrior's code.

**14.77.40**  "Cursed be that base Duryodhana, greedy for the kingdom and full of pride, for whose sake all our kinsmen were brought by me to the abode of Yama."

**14.77.41**  Having spoken thus and uttered many soothing words, the Victor showed his grace; and embracing her with affection, he sent her back to her home.

**14.77.42**  And Duḥśalā too, of fair face, having held back those warriors from the great battle and honored Pārtha, set out toward her home.

**14.77.43**  Then, having vanquished the warriors of Sindhu, that bull among men once more followed after the horse that roamed at will.

**14.77.44**  The hero followed the sacrificial horse duly, O lord of peoples, as the God of gods, bearer of the Pināka, follows the deer-star across the sky.

**14.77.45**  And that steed, roaming as it pleased through one land after another at its ease and according to its desire, increased the glory of Pārtha's deeds.

**14.77.46**  Thus wandering by stages, O bull of the Bharatas, that horse, accompanied by the Pāṇḍava, came to the realm of the lord of Maṇipūra.

**14.78.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When king Babhruvāhana heard that his heroic father had come, the noble one went forth in humility, with Brāhmaṇas bearing the offering of welcome before him.

**14.78.2**  But the wise Dhanaṃjaya, mindful of the warrior's code, did not welcome the lord of Maṇipūra who had thus come to meet him.

**14.78.3**  And the righteous Phalguna, filled with anger, then said to him: "This conduct of yours is not fitting; you stand outside the duty of a Kṣatriya."

**14.78.4**  "This sacrificial horse of Yudhiṣṭhira, guarded by me, has come to the border of your realm; why then, my son, do you not give me battle?"

**14.78.5**  "Shame upon you, dull-witted and unskilled in the warrior's code, who, when I have come for battle, would receive me with mere words of peace!"

**14.78.6**  "No purpose of manhood is served by your living here, you who, like a woman, would meet me with conciliation when I have come arrayed for battle."

**14.78.7**  "Had I come to you with weapons laid aside, O most foolish one, then this conduct of yours would be fitting, O basest of men."

**14.78.8**  When the serpent's daughter Ulūpī learned that he had been thus addressed by her husband, unable to bear it, she split the earth and came up to him.

**14.78.9**  She then beheld her son, brooding with downcast face, rebuked again and again by her husband who sought combat, O lord.

**14.78.10**  Then Ulūpī, the serpent's daughter, fair in every limb and skilled in the warrior's code, approached him and spoke these words:

**14.78.11**  "Know me to be Ulūpī, your mother, daughter of the serpents. Do as I say, my son, and yours shall be the highest dharma."

**14.78.12**  "Fight with this Dhanaṃjaya, best of the Kurus, O subduer of foes; for thus, beyond doubt, will he be pleased with you."

**14.78.13**  Thus roused by his mother, that king Babhruvāhana of great splendor set his mind upon battle, O bull of the Bharatas.

**14.78.14**  Donning golden armor and a radiant helmet, crowded about with a hundred quivers, he mounted his great chariot.

**14.78.15**  The wise one mounted that chariot furnished with every appurtenance, yoked with horses swift as thought, with fine wheels and fittings, adorned with golden trappings.

**14.78.16**  Raising aloft his highly honored banner bearing a golden lion, that king Babhruvāhana set forth against Pārtha.

**14.78.17**  Then the hero, approaching the sacrificial horse guarded by Pārtha, had it seized by men skilled in the handling of horses.

**14.78.18**  Seeing the steed seized, Dhanaṃjaya was glad at heart; and standing on the ground, he confronted his son, who stood in his chariot, in battle.

**14.78.19**  Then that king tormented the hero with showers of arrows by the thousand, keen and like the venom of serpents.

**14.78.20**  A matchless battle arose between the two, father and son, like the war of gods and Asuras, both of them delighting in it.

**14.78.21**  With a smile, the tiger among men Babhruvāhana pierced the Diademed One with a smooth-jointed arrow in the region of the collarbone.

**14.78.22**  The arrow, feathered shaft and all, sank in as a serpent enters an anthill; and piercing through the son of Kuntī, it entered the earth.

**14.78.23**  Sorely pained, the wise one, leaning upon his excellent bow, gathered up his divine energy and stood as though lifeless to behold.

**14.78.24**  Then, recovering his senses, that bull among men, the son of Śakra, praising his son, spoke these words, O lord of the earth:

**14.78.25**  "Well done, well done, O mighty-armed son of Citrāṅgadā! Seeing this deed worthy of your lineage, I am pleased with you, my child."

**14.78.26**  "I now loose my arrows upon you, my son; stand firm in the fight!" So saying, the slayer of foes rained down iron shafts upon him.

**14.78.27**  Those shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva, bright as thunderbolt and lightning, the king cut every one of them with his own iron arrows, each into three.

**14.78.28**  With his divine arrows Pārtha struck down from his chariot his banner, adorned with gold and shaped like a golden palmyra, with a razor-edged shaft.

**14.78.29**  And the bull of the Pāṇḍavas, with a smile, slew the king's horses, huge of body and of great speed and might, leaving them lifeless.

**14.78.30**  Swiftly descending from his chariot, the king in utmost fury fought his father the Pāṇḍava on foot, out of wrath.

**14.78.31**  Greatly delighted at his son's valor, the bull of the Pāṇḍavas, the son of the wielder of the thunderbolt, did not press his son too hard.

**14.78.32**  Though hard pressed, the mighty Babhruvāhana, finding his father turned aside, once more tormented him with arrows shaped like venomous serpents.

**14.78.33**  Then, out of boyish impetuosity, the mighty Babhruvāhana pierced his father in the breast with a keen, well-feathered, winged shaft.

**14.78.34**  That arrow, blazing with energy like flaming fire, entered the Pāṇḍava, O king, piercing a vital part and causing terrible pain.

**14.78.35**  Thus most grievously wounded by his son, the joy of the Kurus, Dhanaṃjaya, overcome by a swoon, then sank to the earth, O king.

**14.78.36**  When that hero, the foremost bearer of the Kaurava burden, had fallen, the son of Citrāṅgadā too at once swooned away.

**14.78.37**  Worn out by his exertions in the fight, and seeing his father slain, and himself sorely wounded before by Arjuna's floods of arrows, that king swooned.

**14.78.38**  Seeing her husband slain and her son fallen upon the ground, Citrāṅgadā, stricken with terror, entered the field of battle.

**14.78.39**  Her heart consumed with grief, weeping, the fair mother of the lord of Maṇipūra then beheld her slain husband.

**14.79.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then the timid, lotus-eyed lady, lamenting in many ways, swooned from grief; unassailable as she was, she fell upon the ground.

**14.79.2**  And regaining her senses, that lady of divine form, beholding Ulūpī the serpent's daughter, spoke these words:

**14.79.3**  "Ulūpī, behold my husband, the victor in battle, lying slain in the field — slain for your sake by my son, a mere boy!"

**14.79.4**  "Are you not, O noble one, a knower of dharma, and devoted to your husband? Yet for your sake this lord has fallen, your husband slain in battle."

**14.79.5**  "But if Dhanaṃjaya has wholly wronged you this day — forgive him, I beseech you; bring Dhanaṃjaya back to life!"

**14.79.6**  "Are you not, O noble and fair one, a knower of dharma, renowned through the three worlds — that, having caused your husband to be slain here by my son, you do not grieve?"

**14.79.7**  "I do not grieve for my slain son, O daughter of the serpents; it is my husband alone I grieve for, to whom this hospitality has been shown."

**14.79.8**  Having so spoken to the lady Ulūpī, the serpent's daughter, the illustrious one then went up to her husband and said this:

**14.79.9**  "Arise, O beloved, you who would do the will of the chief of the Kurus! O mighty-armed one, this horse of yours has been released by me."

**14.79.10**  "Surely, O hero, this sacrificial horse of the King of Dharma was to be followed by you; why then do you lie upon the bare earth?"

**14.79.11**  "Upon you, O joy of the Kurus, depend the lives of the Kurus; why then have you, the giver of life to others, yourself given up your life?"

**14.79.12**  "Ulūpī, look well upon my husband slain in battle! Having spurred on this boy, my son, and caused him to slay his father, do you not grieve?"

**14.79.13**  "Let this boy sleep on, if he must, gone to the path of the dead upon the ground; but let the red-eyed, thick-haired Vijaya live again — that is well."

**14.79.14**  "It is no fault in men, O fair one, to have many wives; but for women this would be a fault. Let no such thought be yours."

**14.79.15**  "This friendship between us was made by the Creator, eternal and imperishable. Acknowledge this friendship; let our union be true and abiding for you."

**14.79.16**  "If, having caused this husband of mine to be slain by my son, you do not show him to me alive this very day, I will give up my life."

**14.79.17**  "I, O timid one, overwhelmed with grief, bereft of husband and son, will here and now sit fasting unto death before your very eyes, beyond all doubt."

**14.79.18**  Having so spoken to her co-wife, the serpent's daughter, Caitravāhinī then sat down to fast unto death and fell silent, O lord of men.

**14.80.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having lamented thus and fallen silent, the lady, clasping her husband's feet, sat down, gazing at her son who still drew breath.

**14.80.2**  Then, regaining his senses, that king Babhruvāhana, looking upon his mother there on the field of battle, spoke:

**14.80.3**  "What can be more grievous than this, that my mother, reared in comfort, lies upon the earth beside her fallen hero, her dead husband?"

**14.80.4**  "She gazes upon him, the slayer of foes in battle, the best of all bearers of arms, struck down by me in combat. Hard indeed it is to die!"

**14.80.5**  "Ah, how firm is this lady's heart, that it does not break asunder as she gazes upon her slain husband, broad-chested and mighty-armed!"

**14.80.6**  "Hard, I think, is it for a man to die before his appointed hour has come — since neither I nor my mother is parted from life."

**14.80.7**  "Alas, look! The golden ornament from the breast of the Kuru hero, slain here by me his son, lies cast away upon the ground."

**14.80.8**  "Ho, Brāhmaṇas, behold my heroic father lying upon the earth, upon the hero's bed, struck down by me his son!"

**14.80.9**  "Let the Brāhmaṇas appointed as keepers of the horse for the chief of the Kurus now perform the rite of expiation for him whom I have slain this day in battle."

**14.80.10**  "And let the Brāhmaṇas prescribe what penance is now to be mine — cruel and sinful as I am, the slayer of my own father on the field of battle."

**14.80.11**  "For me, most cruel one, having this day slain my father, a hard penance of twelve years, clad in this hide,"

**14.80.12**  "and eating from the very skull of this my father — no other expiation is there for me, having slain my father this day."

**14.80.13**  "Behold, O daughter of the foremost of serpents, your husband slain by me! A pleasing thing have I done for you this day, by slaying Arjuna in battle."

**14.80.14**  So I too shall this day go the way my father has gone; I cannot, fair one, hold up my own self by my own strength.

**14.80.15**  When I am dead, mother, and likewise the bearer of the Gāṇḍīva, may you be content, O lady; by truth I take hold of my own self.

**14.80.16**  Having spoken thus, the king, stricken with grief and sorrow, touched water, O great king, and out of anguish uttered these words.

**14.80.17**  Let all beings hear, both the moving and the unmoving, and you too, mother, O excellent serpent-maiden, that I speak the truth.

**14.80.18**  If Jaya does not rise up, my father, that bull of the Bharatas, here on this very field of battle I shall let my body waste away.

**14.80.19**  For having slain my father there is no atonement for me anywhere; tormented by the killing of my elder, I shall surely descend into hell.

**14.80.20**  One who slays a heroic Kṣatriya is freed by the gift of a hundred cows; but having slain my father in this way, hard to cross is the atonement for me.

**14.80.21**  For he was unique, the great-splendored son of Pāṇḍu, Dhanaṃjaya, and my father, righteous of soul; from where can there be atonement for me for his death?

**14.80.22**  Having spoken thus, O king, the king who was Dhanaṃjaya's son touched water and fell silent, the great-minded one resolved to fast unto death.

**14.81.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When the king, the lord of Maṇipūra, had thus sat down to fast unto death, overcome with grief for his father, together with his mother, O scorcher of foes —

**14.81.2**  Ulūpī then called to mind the life-restoring gem, and it came at once to her, that supreme refuge of the serpents.

**14.81.3**  Taking it up, O Kauravya, the daughter of the lord of the serpent-kings spoke to the soldiers words that gladdened the heart.

**14.81.4**  Rise up, do not grieve, my son! This Jiṣṇu was not slain by you; he cannot be conquered by men, nor even by the gods with Vāsava at their head.

**14.81.5**  It was I who deployed this illusion called Mohinī, this very day, for the sake of pleasing the lord of men, your glorious father.

**14.81.6**  For this Kaurava, wishing to test your strength, my son — that slayer of enemy heroes — came, O king, to do battle with you in combat.

**14.81.7**  Therefore, my son, you were urged by me to give battle; do not suspect any sin in yourself, my son, not even the slightest, O lord.

**14.81.8**  This man is a seer of great splendor, eternal and imperishable; not even Śakra is able to conquer him in battle, my little son.

**14.81.9**  And this divine gem of mine I have brought, O lord of peoples — the one that ever and again brings back to life the serpent-lords who have died.

**14.81.10**  Place this upon your father's breast, O lord; then, my son, you shall behold the son of Pāṇḍu restored to life once more.

**14.81.11**  Thus addressed, he then placed the gem upon the breast of Pārtha, that one of boundless splendor, out of love for his father, he who had done no sin.

**14.81.12**  When that gem was set in place, O hero, the lord Jiṣṇu was revived; he rose up as if waking from sleep, rubbing his reddened eyes.

**14.81.13**  Beholding his father risen, the great-souled and wise one restored to consciousness and well, Babhruvāhana saluted him.

**14.81.14**  When that tiger among men had risen again in all his splendor, O lord, the chastiser of Pāka rained down divine and holy blossoms.

**14.81.15**  Drums, unstruck, resounded with the voice of thunderclouds, and there arose in the sky a mighty cry of 'Well done! Well done!'

**14.81.16**  Having risen, the mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya, now revived, embraced Babhruvāhana and kissed him upon the head.

**14.81.17**  And he saw not far off his son's mother, wasted with grief, standing together with Ulūpī; then Dhanaṃjaya asked:

**14.81.18**  What is all this that appears here, this battlefield marked with grief and wonder and joy, O slayer of foes? If you know, tell me.

**14.81.19**  And why has your mother come to the field of battle, and why has this Ulūpī, daughter of the serpent-king, come hither?

**14.81.20**  I know that this battle was waged by you at my bidding; but the reason for these women's coming I wish to learn.

**14.81.21**  Then the lord of Maṇipūra, thus questioned, the wise one, bowed his head in entreaty and said to him: 'Let Ulūpī be asked.'

**14.82.1**  Arjuna said: What is the purpose of your coming, O delight of the Kaurava line, and likewise that of the mother of the lord of Maṇipūra, here upon the battlefield?

**14.82.2**  Do you wish well to this king, O serpent's daughter, or to me, you of the restless glances? Do you desire good for us?

**14.82.3**  I hope, O broad-hipped one of fair appearance, that I have done nothing unwelcome to you in ignorance, nor has this Babhruvāhana.

**14.82.4**  And I hope that the princess, your co-wife of the line of Citravāhana, the fair-limbed Citrāṅgadā, has in no way given offense.

**14.82.5**  Then the daughter of the serpent-lord answered him with a smile: Neither have you offended me, nor has King Babhruvāhana, nor this his mother, who stands by me like a servant.

**14.82.6**  Let it be heard how and why all this was contrived by me; you must not be angry with me — with bowed head I entreat you.

**14.82.7**  For it was for your sake, O Kauravya, that this was done by me, sinless one; hear it in full, O mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya.

**14.82.8**  In the great war of the Bhāratas, the king who was Śāntanu's son was slain by you unrighteously, O Pārtha; this is the atonement made for that.

**14.82.9**  For Bhīṣma was not struck down by you, O hero, while he was fighting; engaged with Śikhaṇḍin, and using him as a shield, you slew him.

**14.82.10**  Had you given up your life without performing the expiation for that deed, by that sinful act you would surely have fallen into hell.

**14.82.11**  But this expiation was ordained, which you have received at your son's hands, by the Vasus, O guardian of the earth, and by Gaṅgā, O greatly wise one.

**14.82.12**  For long ago I heard this, told by the Vasus, when, after King Śāntanu's son had been slain, they came to the bank of the Gaṅgā.

**14.82.13**  Having bathed and gathered at the great river, the Vasu gods then spoke these terrible words, with the consent of Bhāgīrathī:

**14.82.14**  This Bhīṣma, son of Śāntanu, has been slain by Savyasācin while he was not fighting, being engaged with another in the combat, O fair lady.

**14.82.15**  Therefore, on account of this assault, we too, O fair one, shall lay a curse upon Arjuna — so they said; and she replied, 'So be it.'

**14.82.16**  Having reported this to my father, I became deeply distressed in all my senses; and he, hearing it, fell into the utmost despair.

**14.82.17**  But my father went to the Vasus and pleaded for your sake, again and again entreating them; and they spoke this to him:

**14.82.18**  The young lord of Maṇipūra, O greatly blessed one, his very son, shall fell him to the ground with arrows as he stands in the midst of battle;

**14.82.19**  when this is done, O serpent-king, he shall be released from the curse. 'Go,' he was told by the Vasus; and he related this to me.

**14.82.20**  Having heard this, I have freed you from that curse; for not even the king of the gods could defeat you in battle.

**14.82.21**  The son is held to be one's own self; therefore you were defeated here by him. In this I see no fault — what do you think, O lord?

**14.82.22**  Thus addressed, Vijaya, his soul now serene, said this: All that you have done, O lady, is most pleasing to me.

**14.82.23**  Having spoken thus, Jaya then addressed his son, the lord of Maṇipūra, while Citrāṅgadā and the daughter of the Kaurava listened:

**14.82.24**  Yudhiṣṭhira's horse-sacrifice will take place on the coming full moon of Caitra; come there, O king, with your ministers and together with your mothers.

**14.82.25**  Thus addressed by Pārtha, that king Babhruvāhana, the wise one, with eyes clouded by tears, said this to his father:

**14.82.26**  I shall come, O knower of duty, at your command, to the great sacrifice of the horse, to serve the twice-born there.

**14.82.27**  But to do me a favor, enter your own city together with your two wives, O slayer of foes; let there be no hesitation in you about this.

**14.82.28**  Having dwelt here at ease in your own dwelling, your wounds healed, O lord, you may again take up the following of the horse, O best of the victorious.

**14.82.29**  Thus addressed by his son, that one whose banner bore the ape, the son of Kuntī, then smiling spoke to the son of Citrāṅgadā:

**14.82.30**  You know, O mighty-armed one, how I am bound by my consecration; therefore I shall not enter your city, O wide-eyed one.

**14.82.31**  This sacrificial steed goes where it will; may it be well with you — I must depart, for no stopping is allowed me.

**14.82.32**  There, duly honored by him, the son of the chastiser of Pāka, taking leave of his two wives, departed — that best of the Bharatas.

**14.83.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: But that horse, having wandered round this earth bounded by the ocean, turned back, O king, heading toward the city named for the elephant.

**14.83.2**  And the splendid wearer of the diadem, following it back, by chance came then upon the city of Rājagṛha.

**14.83.3**  As he drew near, the king who was the grandson of Jarāsaṃdha, a hero steadfast in the law of the warrior, summoned him to battle.

**14.83.4**  Then, issuing from the city, mounted on his chariot, bow and arrows and hand-guard ready, Meghasaṃdhi rushed upon Dhanaṃjaya, who was on foot.

**14.83.5**  And drawing near to Dhanaṃjaya, the greatly splendid Meghasaṃdhi, out of boyishness, O great king, and not from skill, spoke thus:

**14.83.6**  Why is this horse being led about as though in the midst of women, O Bhārata? I shall carry this steed off; make your effort to set it free.

**14.83.7**  If you were not taught submission in battle by my forefathers, I shall show you hospitality; strike, or I shall strike.

**14.83.8**  Thus addressed, the son of Pāṇḍu answered him with something like a laugh: It is my vow laid down that whoever raises obstruction must be warded off by me.

**14.83.9**  This was enjoined by my eldest brother, O king, and is surely known to you as well; strike to the best of your power — there is no anger in me.

**14.83.10**  Thus addressed, the lord of Magadha struck first at the son of Pāṇḍu, scattering thousands of arrows as the thousand-eyed one pours down rains.

**14.83.11**  Then the heroic bearer of the Gāṇḍīva, with arrows sped from the Gāṇḍīva, rendered those shafts vain without effort, O bull of the Bharatas.

**14.83.12**  Having made the flood of his arrows fruitless, he of the ape-banner loosed blazing shafts like serpents with flaming mouths.

**14.83.13**  Upon the banner, upon the staffs of the pennants, upon the chariot's frame and the horses, and upon the other parts of the car — but not upon the body, nor upon the charioteer.

**14.83.14**  Being thus spared by Pārtha as to his body, the man of Magadha, taking this for his own valor, kept loosing his arrows at Phalguna.

**14.83.15**  Then the heroic bearer of the Gāṇḍīva, struck by the Magadhan, shone like a great palāśa tree of spring covered with blossoms.

**14.83.16**  Not being slain himself, the Magadhan went on striking the bull of the Pāṇḍavas; and by that, O Kauravya, he held his ground in the sight of the world's hero.

**14.83.17**  But Savyasācin, wrathful, drawing his bow with might, made the horses lifeless and struck off the charioteer's head.

**14.83.18**  And with a razor-edged arrow he cut apart that great and splendid bow of his, and brought down his hand-guard, his pennant, and his banner.

**14.83.19**  Shaken, his horses gone, his bow broken, his charioteer slain, that king snatched up his mace and rushed swiftly upon the son of Kuntī.

**14.83.20**  As he came charging, Arjuna at once cut his gold-adorned mace into many pieces with a host of vulture-feathered shafts.

**14.83.21**  That mace, shattered into fragments, its jeweled mountings broken loose, fell in a thousand pieces, like a serpent sloughing off its skin.

**14.83.22**  Chariotless, bowless, stripped now even of his mace—the wise Arjuna, foremost in battle, did not wish to strike him.

**14.83.23**  Then to him, downcast yet steadfast in the warrior's duty, the hero whose banner bore the ape spoke these gentle words.

**14.83.24**  "Enough—you have shown the warrior's code in full, my son. Go now. Great is this deed of yours in battle, O king, young as you are."

**14.83.25**  "Yudhiṣṭhira's command is that kings are not to be slain. By that order you live, O king, though you offended me in the fight."

**14.83.26**  The Māgadha king, understanding himself thus dismissed and knowing the words to be true, with joined palms paid him honor in return.

**14.83.27**  Arjuna reassured him and spoke again: "You must come at the next full moon of Caitra, to our king's horse-sacrifice."

**14.83.28**  Thus addressed, the son of Sahadeva said "So be it," and duly did honor both to the horse and to Phalguna, the best of warriors.

**14.83.29**  Then once more the maned steed roamed where it pleased, going along the seacoast to the Vaṅgas, the Puṇḍras, and the Keralas.

**14.83.30**  And there, O king, in place after place, Dhanaṃjaya conquered with his bow Gāṇḍīva the many great hosts of the Mlecchas.

**14.84.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Honored by the Māgadha king, O sovereign, the Pāṇḍava of the white steeds turned to the southern quarter and let the horse range on.

**14.84.2**  Then the strong horse, wandering at its will, turned back and reached the lovely city of the Cedis, called Śuktimatī.

**14.84.3**  There the mighty hero was honored by Śarabha, son of Śiśupāla, with respect and homage preceded by a token fight.

**14.84.4**  Honored there, O king, that finest of horses then went on to the Kāśis, the Andhras, the Kosalas, the Kirātas, and the Taṅgaṇas.

**14.84.5**  There the Pāṇḍava received their homage as was due, and the son of Kuntī turned again and came to the Daśārṇas.

**14.84.6**  There a mighty lord of the earth named Citrāṅgada gave Vijaya a most terrible battle.

**14.84.7**  Having brought him too to submission, the diademed bull among men passed into the realm of Ekalavya, king of the Niṣādas.

**14.84.8**  And Ekalavya's son confronted him then in battle, and he waged with the Niṣādas a fight that made the hair stand on end.

**14.84.9**  Then the son of Kuntī, unvanquished in battles, the hero conquered him too in combat, risen up to hinder the sacrifice.

**14.84.10**  Having conquered the son of the Niṣāda, O great king, the offspring of Indra, the chastiser of Pāka, was honored and went on once more toward the southern ocean.

**14.84.11**  There too the diademed one had battle with the Draviḍas, the Andhras, the fierce Māhiṣakas, and the people of Kollagiri.

**14.84.12**  Then, following the horse's lead, he went on toward the Surāṣṭras, reached Gokarṇa, and came also to Prabhāsa.

**14.84.13**  Then the splendid sacrificial horse of the Kuru king reached lovely Dvāravatī, guarded by the heroes of the Vṛṣṇis.

**14.84.14**  The young princes of the Yādavas, harrying that best of horses, set out against it, but Ugrasena, O king, held them back.

**14.84.15**  Then the lord of the Vṛṣṇis and Andhakas came forth from the city, together with Vasudeva, the maternal uncle of the diademed hero.

**14.84.16**  The two came up to the foremost of the Kurus and stood ready to honor that best of the Bharatas duly and with affection, with the highest homage; then, taking leave of them, he went on by the way the horse had gone.

**14.84.17**  Then the horse roamed in turn through the prosperous western country of the sea, and from there it came to the land of the Five Rivers.

**14.84.18**  And from there, O Kaurava, the horse wandered at its will into the realm of the Gāndhāras, followed all the while by the son of Kuntī.

**14.84.19**  There the great-souled one had a fearful battle with the king of the Gāndhāras, the son of Śakuni, who pursued the old enmity.

**14.85.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: The valiant son of Śakuni, great chariot-warrior of the Gāndhāras, advanced against Guḍākeśa, surrounded by a vast army full of elephants, horses, and chariots and garlanded with banners and flags.

**14.85.2**  Those warriors, unable to bear the slaying of King Śakuni, advanced together upon Pārtha with bows drawn.

**14.85.3**  To them the righteous and unvanquished Bībhatsu spoke Yudhiṣṭhira's words, but they would not heed his wholesome counsel.

**14.85.4**  Though restrained by Pārtha with conciliating words, the enraged men surrounded the horse and seized it; thereupon the Pāṇḍava grew wrathful.

**14.85.5**  Then with flame-tipped razor-shafts loosed from Gāṇḍīva, Arjuna the Pāṇḍava cut off their heads as if with scarcely any effort.

**14.85.6**  Cut down by Pārtha, they let go the horse in their panic and turned back, O great king, sorely tormented by the rain of arrows.

**14.85.7**  Though harassed by those Gāndhāras, the lustrous bull of the Pāṇḍavas, marking each man out one by one, sent their heads tumbling down.

**14.85.8**  As the Gāndhāras were being slain on every side in the fray, that king, the son of Śakuni, moved to check the Pāṇḍava.

**14.85.9**  To that king as he fought, steadfast in the warrior's duty, Pārtha said: "Kings are not to be slain by me, by the king's command. Enough of fighting, O hero—there is no defeat for you this day."

**14.85.10**  Thus addressed, he slighted those words, deluded by ignorance, and showered with arrows the one whose deeds equaled Śakra's.

**14.85.11**  Calmly, with a crescent-headed feathered shaft, Pārtha swept away his helmet, as once he had taken the head of Jayadratha.

**14.85.12**  Seeing this, all the Gāndhāras were filled with wonder, and they understood that the king had not been slain only because Arjuna so willed.

**14.85.13**  But the son of the Gāndhāra king, intent upon flight, looked, together with those frightened men, like a small beast among the timid creatures.

**14.85.14**  As they scattered swiftly about that very field, Pārtha struck off their heads with broad-bladed shafts well-jointed at the bend.

**14.85.15**  Some did not even feel their upraised arms carried away by the broad arrows loosed from Gāṇḍīva and sped by Pārtha.

**14.85.16**  Then that host, its men, elephants, and horses thrown into confusion, broke and fled, most of it slain and shattered, wheeling about again and again.

**14.85.17**  For not one of the foes, struck down before that hero of foremost deeds, could be seen to withstand his mighty arrows.

**14.85.18**  Then the mother of the Gāndhāra king, led by aged ministers, came forth in fear, bearing before her the choicest offering of welcome.

**14.85.19**  Composed, she restrained her son, mad with battle-frenzy, and she sought to appease the victorious one of untiring deeds.

**14.85.20**  The son of Kuntī honored her and then granted his grace, and soothing the son of Śakuni too he spoke thus.

**14.85.21**  "It does not please me, O mighty-armed slayer of foes, that you set your mind to fight against me; you are a brother to me, O sinless one."

**14.85.22**  "Remembering my mother Gāndhārī, and for Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sake—by that you live, O king, though your followers are slain."

**14.85.23**  "Be not thus; let the enmity be quelled; let your mind no longer be of this sort. You must come at the next full moon of Caitra, to our king's horse-sacrifice."

**14.86.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having spoken thus, Pārtha followed the horse that wandered at its will, and then the steed turned back toward the city named for the elephant.

**14.86.2**  Yudhiṣṭhira learned through a spy that it had turned back, and on hearing that Arjuna was well he was glad at heart.

**14.86.3**  And hearing of Vijaya's deeds in the realm of the Gāndhāras and in the other lands, the king was greatly pleased.

**14.86.4**  At that very season the Dharma King Yudhiṣṭhira chose the twelfth day of the fortnight of Māgha and a favorable lunar mansion.

**14.86.5**  The greatly lustrous, high-minded Kaurava summoned all his brothers together—Bhīma, and Nakula, and Sahadeva.

**14.86.6**  Then in due time that best of the upholders of dharma, the foremost of speakers, addressing Bhīma of terrible prowess, spoke these words.

**14.86.7**  "Bhīmasena, your younger brother is coming with the horse, as the men told me who followed in Dhanaṃjaya's track."

**14.86.8**  "The time has come, and the horse is near at hand; the full moon of Māgha approaches, and but a month remains, O Vṛkodara."

**14.86.9**  "So let learned Brahmins, masters of the Vedas, be sent forth; for the accomplishment of the horse-sacrifice let them survey a fit ground."

**14.86.10**  Thus addressed, Bhīma carried out the king's command, rejoicing to hear from his sovereign that Savyasācin was returning.

**14.86.11**  Then Bhīmasena set out with skilled master-builders, placing at their head Brahmins versed in the rites of sacrifice.

**14.86.12**  The Kaurava had measured out the sacrificial enclosure according to rule, with its rows of halls and dwellings, its broad streets and turreted gateways.

**14.86.13**  He had built, duly and adorned with gems and gold, the assembly hall, the queens' pavilion, and beyond it the chamber of the fire-priest.

**14.86.14**  He set up pillars bright with gold and lofty archways throughout the precincts of the sacrificial site, bestowing pure gold as well.

**14.86.15**  And the righteous one had inner apartments built, each in its place as was fitting, for the kings dwelling in their various lands.

**14.86.16**  And for the Brahmins who had gathered from many lands, Bhīma had dwellings built, varied and manifold.

**14.86.17**  Likewise, O great king, by the sovereign's order Bhīmasena dispatched envoys to the kings of untiring deeds.

**14.86.18**  To please the lord of the Kurus those best of kings came, bringing many jewels, women, horses, and weapons.

**14.86.19**  As they settled by the thousands in those encampments, the tumult, like the roaring of the sea, seemed to touch the heavens.

**14.86.20**  For those who had come, the lotus-eyed king ordered food and drink and couches of more than human splendor.

**14.86.21**  And for the draught animals the Dharma King ordered manifold stalls, furnished with rice, sugarcane, and milk, O tiger among men.

**14.86.22**  And at that great sacrifice of the wise Dharma King many bands of sages, expounders of the sacred word, assembled.

**14.86.23**  And the most eminent of the twice-born who were there, O lord of the earth, gathered together with their disciples, and the Kaurava received them.

**14.86.24**  And the greatly lustrous Yudhiṣṭhira himself, casting off all pride, accompanied each of them as far as his lodging.

**14.86.25**  Then the master-builders and the other craftsmen, having completed the whole apparatus of the sacrifice, reported it, O king, to the Dharma King.

**14.86.26**  Hearing that all had been done faultlessly, the Dharma King, that steadfast sovereign, grew joyful in form together with his brothers.

**14.87.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When that sacrifice was under way, eloquent men skilled in reasoning, each eager to outdo the other, propounded many arguments of logic.

**14.87.2**  The kings beheld that supreme rite of sacrifice, ordered by Bhīma as if it were the very rite of the lord of the gods, O delight of the Kurus.

**14.87.3**  There they saw archways wrought of gold, and very many couches, seats, and pleasure-halls adorned with jewels.

**14.87.4**  Pots, platters, cauldrons, water-jars, and covered vessels—the princes saw nothing there that was not made of gold.

**14.87.5**  And the sacrificial posts, prescribed by scripture, made of wood and adorned with gold, were set up in due time and order, blazing with abundant splendor.

**14.87.6**  Whatever creatures there are, born of the land or born of the water, O lord—the kings saw them all brought together there.

**14.87.7**  Cows and buffaloes, and aged women too, water-dwelling creatures, beasts of prey, and birds—

**14.87.8**  creatures born of the womb, born of the egg, born of sweat, and sprung from the soil, dwellers of mountains, marshlands, and forests—all these they beheld.

**14.87.9**  Seeing the sacrificial enclosure thus everywhere rejoicing with cattle, herds, and grain, rich and abounding in plentiful well-dressed food for the brāhmaṇas and the common folk, the kings were filled with the highest wonder.

**14.87.10**  When a full hundred thousand brāhmaṇas were feasting there, the kettledrum, deep-voiced as a thundercloud, was struck again and again.

**14.87.11**  Day after day it resounded without ceasing; thus did the sacrifice of the wise King of Righteousness proceed.

**14.87.12**  Many heaps of food, O king, like mountains were poured forth, and men saw streams of curds and lakes of clarified butter dark as collyrium.

**14.87.13**  For the whole of Jambūdvīpa, thronged with its many peoples and realms, seemed gathered into one place, O king, at that great rite of the king.

**14.87.14**  Thither came thousands upon thousands of men of every caste from this place and that, bringing much wealth, O bull of the Bharatas.

**14.87.15**  And kings too, garlanded and wearing well-polished jeweled earrings, waited upon those foremost of the twice-born, by hundreds and by thousands.

**14.87.16**  The various foods and drinks fit for a king's enjoyment, meant for the men who were their attendants, those kings gave away to the brāhmaṇas.

**14.88.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Seeing the knowers of the Veda assembled and the kings, the lords of the earth, King Yudhiṣṭhira then spoke to Bhīmasena.

**14.88.2**  "These tigers among men, these lords of the world, have come hither; let honor be done to them, for these rulers of men are worthy of honor."

**14.88.3**  Thus addressed by the illustrious king, Bhīmasena of great splendor did accordingly, together with the twins, O Bhārata.

**14.88.4**  Then Govinda, foremost of all living beings, approached the son of Dharma, accompanied by the Vṛṣṇis and with Baladeva at their head.

**14.88.5**  He came together with Yuyudhāna, with Pradyumna and Gada, with Niśaṭha and Sāmba, and likewise with Kṛtavarman.

**14.88.6**  To them too the mighty-armed Bhīma did the highest honor, and those bulls among men entered dwellings rich with jewels.

**14.88.7**  And in Yudhiṣṭhira's presence, at the close of their converse, the Slayer of Madhu spoke of Arjuna, worn thin by his many battles.

**14.88.8**  The son of Kuntī, that subduer of foes, asked him again and again about his brother; and the Lord of the world, the King of Righteousness, told him of the victorious Jiṣṇu.

**14.88.9**  "A trusted man of mine, a dweller in Dvārakā, came to me, O king, one who had seen the best of the Pāṇḍavas, worn thin by his many battles.

**14.88.10**  And he told me, O lord, that the mighty-armed one was near at hand: 'Carry out, O son of Kuntī, the preparations for the accomplishment of the horse-sacrifice.'"

**14.88.11**  Thus addressed, the King of Righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira, replied to him: "By good fortune the victorious one is well and is drawing near, O Mādhava.

**14.88.12**  What that foremost in strength of the Pāṇḍavas sent word to you, that I wish to hear told here by you, O delight of the Yadus."

**14.88.13**  When this was said, O tiger among kings, the eloquent lord of the Vṛṣṇis and Andhakas then spoke these words to the righteous Yudhiṣṭhira.

**14.88.14**  "This message of Pārtha's, O great king, that man spoke to me: 'Yudhiṣṭhira is to be told, O Kṛṣṇa, in due time, this word of mine.

**14.88.15**  Kings will come from every quarter to the Kauravas; to each one of them honor should be done, for this is fitting for us.

**14.88.16**  By this word of mine the king is to be informed, O bestower of honor, lest there be any neglect in the offering of the guest-water.'"

**14.88.17**  "The king ought to see to it, and let you also approve, lest through hostility to the kings these subjects of yours perish, O king."

**14.88.18**  "And this other word of Dhanaṃjaya's that man spoke, O son of Kuntī; hear it, O king, as I declare it to you.

**14.88.19**  'To our sacrifice will come the king who is lord of Maṇipūra, my son of great splendor, my beloved Babhruvāhana.

**14.88.20**  Him you should duly honor for my sake, for he is ever devoted and attached to me'—so he said, O lord."

**14.88.21**  Hearing these words, the King of Righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira, welcomed that speech of his and spoke these words.

**14.89.1**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: I have heard this pleasing word, O Kṛṣṇa, such as is worthy of you to speak; it is like the taste of nectar to me and gladdens my heart, O mighty lord.

**14.89.2**  Many battles, it is said, did Vijaya wage against kings, again and again here and there, so I have heard, O Hṛṣīkeśa.

**14.89.3**  For my sake that wise Pārtha, Vijaya, has ever been deprived of comfort—at this exceedingly my mind grieves.

**14.89.4**  I ever ponder in secret, O scion of Vṛṣṇi, upon the son of Kuntī: what unlucky mark can there be, O Kṛṣṇa, upon his body, honored with every auspicious sign, by reason of which he suffers such sorrows?

**14.89.5**  Exceedingly does that son of Kuntī forever bear sorrow, yet I see nothing blameworthy in the limbs of Bībhatsu; if this is to be heard by me, you ought to explain it to me.

**14.89.6**  Thus addressed, Hṛṣīkeśa, that Viṣṇu who is the increaser of the Bhoja royal line, having reflected a long while, spoke to the king.

**14.89.7**  "I perceive nothing inauspicious in this lion among men, O king, save that the calves of his legs are overly long.

**14.89.8**  By reason of these that tiger among men is forever bound to journeys upon the roads; nothing else do I see by which Jaya bears such sorrow."

**14.89.9**  Thus told the truth by the wise Kṛṣṇa, that best of the Kurus replied to the tiger of the Vṛṣṇis: "It is even so," O lord.

**14.89.10**  But Kṛṣṇā, that is Draupadī, looked askance at Kṛṣṇa with some resentment, and the Slayer of Keśin accepted that fondness of hers; for Hṛṣīkeśa was a friend to his friend, even as another Dhanaṃjaya in the flesh.

**14.89.11**  There Bhīma and the rest, both Kurus and Yādavas, took delight in hearing the tales of Dhanaṃjaya, marvelous in their import, O lord.

**14.89.12**  And even as they were thus telling stories of Arjuna, a mortal messenger came with word from the great-souled Vijaya.

**14.89.13**  Approaching the best of the Kurus and bowing to him, the prudent man announced that the tiger among men, Arjuna, was drawing near.

**14.89.14**  Hearing this, the king, his eyes brimming with tears of joy, then gave much wealth to him for the sake of that welcome tiding.

**14.89.15**  Then on the second day a great clamor swelled up as that tiger among men, the foremost bearer of the Pāṇḍavas' burden, drew near.

**14.89.16**  Then the dust that arose shone about his steed as it moved, even as it does about Uccaiḥśravas.

**14.89.17**  There Arjuna heard the joyful murmuring voices of the people: "By good fortune you are safe, O Pārtha; blessed is King Yudhiṣṭhira!

**14.89.18**  For who other than Arjuna, having conquered the whole earth with its kings and let the finest of horses roam free, could return again?"

**14.89.19**  "Of the great-souled kings who have passed away, Sagara and the rest, not even of them have we heard of any such deed as this.

**14.89.20**  Nor will the kings of the earth yet to come accomplish this hard deed that you, O best of the Kuru race, have done here."

**14.89.21**  Hearing the words of the people thus speaking, sweet to his ears, the righteous Phalguna entered the sacrificial enclosure.

**14.89.22**  Then the king with his counsellors, and Kṛṣṇa the delight of the Yadus, putting Dhṛtarāṣṭra at their head, went forth then to meet him.

**14.89.23**  Saluting the feet of his father and of the wise King of Righteousness, and honoring Bhīma and the rest, he embraced Keśava.

**14.89.24**  Honored by them as they met him, and honoring them in return as was due, the righteous one rested then, like a crosser of the waters who has gained the farther shore.

**14.89.25**  At this very time the wise King Babhruvāhana, together with his two mothers, came to the Kurus.

**14.89.26**  Having met all the Kurus and being welcomed by them all, he entered the excellent abode of his grandmother Kuntī.

**14.90.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having duly entered the dwelling of the Pāṇḍavas, he greeted his grandmother with the most gracious courtesy.

**14.90.2**  And likewise Queen Citrāṅgadā, together with the daughter of the Kauravya, approached with humility Pṛthā and Kṛṣṇā, and Subhadrā in due form, and the other women of the Kurus.

**14.90.3**  Then Kuntī gave to the two of them jewels of various kinds, and Draupadī and Subhadrā and the other women gave gifts as well.

**14.90.4**  There the two queens dwelt upon costly beds and seats, well honored by Kuntī herself out of her wish to please Pārtha.

**14.90.5**  And that king of great valor, Babhruvāhana, being honored, waited duly upon Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the ruler of the earth.

**14.90.6**  And approaching King Yudhiṣṭhira and Bhīma and the other Pāṇḍavas, the one of great splendor saluted them with humility.

**14.90.7**  Embraced by them with affection and duly honored, he was given abundant wealth by those great chariot-warriors, well pleased with him.

**14.90.8**  And likewise that ruler of the earth waited with humility upon Kṛṣṇa, the bearer of discus and mace, even as Pradyumna waits upon Govinda.

**14.90.9**  To that king Kṛṣṇa gave a costly and much-prized chariot, adorned with gold and yoked with celestial horses, the finest of its kind.

**14.90.10**  And the King of Righteousness and Bhīma, the twins and Phalguna, each in turn, honored him exceedingly, for he was worthy of honor.

**14.90.11**  Then on the third day the sage, the son of Satyavatī, the eloquent one, came to Yudhiṣṭhira and spoke these words.

**14.90.12**  "From this day onward, O son of Kuntī, perform your sacrifice, for your time has come; the auspicious moment for the rite is at hand, and the officiating priests are urging it on.

**14.90.13**  This rite of yours, O lord of kings, named Ahīna and offering choices, is renowned as the Bahusuvarṇaka, the 'rich-in-gold,' because of the abundance of its gold.

**14.90.14**  Even so, O great king, make the priestly fee threefold; let it come to threefold, O king, for the brāhmaṇas are the cause herein.

**14.90.15**  By attaining herein three horse-sacrifices with abundant fees, you will cast off the sin incurred by the slaying of kinsmen, O ruler of men.

**14.90.16**  For this purifying bath at the close of the horse-sacrifice, which you shall attain, O delight of the Kurus, is the supreme purifier, the cleanser of cleansers."

**14.90.17**  Thus addressed by Vyāsa of measureless splendor, that splendid and righteous one then entered upon the consecration for the attaining of the horse-sacrifice; and the ruler of men offered up the horse-sacrifice, that great rite.

**14.90.18**  There, O king, the officiating priests, versed in the Vedas, knowers of the sacred science and well trained, moving about in their circuits, performed the rites according to ordinance.

**14.90.19**  Among them there was no stumbling nor any wrong oblation; those bulls among the twice-born performed every act there in due sequence and with precision.

**14.90.20**  Having duly performed the Pravargya rite, those best of the twice-born, knowers of the law, then, O king, carried out the pressing of the Soma according to ordinance.

**14.90.21**  Having pressed the Soma, O king, those foremost of Soma-drinkers, following the scriptures, performed the pressings one after another in their proper order.

**14.90.22**  There was not there a single wretched man, nor a poor one, nor any hungry, sorrowful, or lowly person.

**14.90.23**  Bhīmasena of great splendour, at the king's constant command, had food given out without ceasing to all who sought to eat.

**14.90.24**  Skilled too in the laying of the sacrificial grass, the priests, their eyes fixed on the meaning of the scriptures, performed all the rites day after day.

**14.90.25**  Not one of that wise king's assembled members was ignorant of the six auxiliary sciences; none was without his vows, without a teacher, or unequal to debate.

**14.90.26**  Then, O bull of the Bharatas, when the time came for raising the sacrificial posts, they set up six of bilva wood, as many of khadira, and the same number wrought of bilva timber, of every kind.

**14.90.27**  In the sacrifice of the lord of the Kurus the priests caused to be made two posts of devadāru wood and one of śleṣmātaka.

**14.90.28**  And for splendour's sake, O bull among men, Bhīma had other posts made of gold, at the command of the Dharma-king.

**14.90.29**  Adorned with cloths, O royal sage, those posts shone forth, attended by lords of men, like the seven sages waiting upon the gods in heaven.

**14.90.30**  There bricks of gold too were made for the building of the fire-altar, and the altar shone there as had that of Dakṣa, lord of creatures.

**14.90.31**  His altar was built of four layers and measured eighteen cubits; piled with golden wings, threefold in size, it had the shape of Garuḍa.

**14.90.32**  Then the sacrificial victims were appointed by the wise according to the scriptures, the birds and the beasts, each dedicated to its own particular deity.

**14.90.33**  Bulls prescribed by the scriptures, and such creatures as dwell in the waters — all of these they yoked there to the rite of the building of the fire.

**14.90.34**  At the posts there were appointed three hundred beasts in all, with the jewel of a horse foremost among them, for the great-souled king, son of Kuntī.

**14.90.35**  That sacrifice of his shone forth, thronged in person with divine sages, crowded with hosts of Gandharvas, and graced by troops of Apsarases.

**14.90.36**  It was made beautiful by the songs of Kiṃpuruṣas and by Kinnaras, and encompassed on every side by the dwellings of Siddhas and brāhmaṇas.

**14.90.37**  Ever present in that assembly were the disciples of Vyāsa, foremost of the twice-born, authors of all the scriptures and skilled in the works of sacrifice.

**14.90.38**  And there were present Nārada and Tumburu of great lustre, Viśvāvasu, Citrasena, and others skilled in song.

**14.90.39**  The Gandharvas, skilled in song and accomplished in dance, delighted those brāhmaṇas in the intervals between the rites of the sacrifice.

**14.91.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having dispatched the other victims according to ordinance, those best of the twice-born, the brāhmaṇas, seized the horse in the manner the scriptures prescribe.

**14.91.2**  Then, O king, having duly quieted the horse, those bulls among priests had the high-minded daughter of Drupada lie down beside it, with the three portions performed according to rule.

**14.91.3**  Drawing out its omentum according to the scriptures, O bull of the Bharatas, those bulls among the twice-born, unhurried, cooked it as the scriptures direct.

**14.91.4**  And the Dharma-king, together with his younger brothers, then inhaled in due form that smoke-scent of the omentum, which takes away all sin.

**14.91.5**  Whatever limbs of that horse remained, O lord of men, the steadfast sixteen officiating priests, all together, offered into the fire.

**14.91.6**  Having thus brought to completion the sacrifice of that king of Indra-like splendour, the blessed Vyāsa, with his disciples, exalted that monarch with benedictions.

**14.91.7**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira gave, according to ordinance, a thousand crores of gold coins to the assembled members, and to Vyāsa he gave the earth itself.
